Disjointness of rescalings of smooth area preserving flows on surfaces
Przemyslaw Berk, Corinna Ulcigrai

Abstract
. We consider the problem of disjointness of rescalings ( φ κ ​ t ) t ∈ ℝ (\varphi_{\kappa t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}} , κ ∈ ℝ \kappa\in\mathbb{R} of a flow ( φ t ) t ∈ ℝ (\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}} in the context of smooth flows preserving a smooth invariant measure, or, equivalently, locally Hamiltonian flows on compact orientable surfaces. We show that, when the genus of the surface is g ≥ 2 g\geq 2 , almost every locally Hamiltonian flow with 2g-2 non-degenerate simple saddles is such that any distinct two rational rescalings ( φ κ ​ t ) t ∈ ℝ (\varphi_{\kappa t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}} and ( φ κ ′ ​ t ) t ∈ ℝ (\varphi_{\kappa^{\prime}t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}} , with κ = p / q \kappa=p/q and κ ′ = p ′ / q ′ \kappa^{\prime}=p^{\prime}/q^{\prime} of different absolute values, are disjoint. Previous results on disjointness of rescalings were available only for locally Hamiltonian flows and their special flow representations in genus one. The result is proved using a criterion for disjointness based on the study of the distribution of Birkhoff sums of a special representation and in particular estimates on their exponential tails decay. A key novel geometric ingredient in the proof is the existence of a sequence of rigidity times which display what we call bounded-type rigidity, so that a large set of points comes back in time q q with distance O ⁡ ( 1 / q ) O(1/q) . To produce such bounded-type rigidity times we exploit a particular way of degeneration of a translation surface to a flat torus for which the vertical flow has bounded-type rotation number.



1.
Introduction

We push in this paper the investigation of
disjointness
in the context of smooth area-preserving flows on surfaces. The definition of disjointness and the question of disjointness among flows and within their time-changes goes back to seminar work by Furstenberg
[
Fu
]
, see e.g.
[
Gl
]
and the references therein. The notion of
disjointness of rescalings
more specifically has seen a revival of interest in recent years, in particular in connection with Sarnak’s Moebius orthogonality conjecture and in the context of parabolic dynamics (see §
1.1.2
). We pursue in this paper the investigation of disjointness of rescalings as a fundamental property of parabolic flows, by proving its typicality among a class of smooth-area preserving flows on surfaces. Our main result is stated in §
1.2
.

1.1.
Disjointness in parabolic dynamics

Let us first recall the notion of disjointness of rescalings and its relevance.

1.1.1.
Disjointness of rescalings

Given a (measure-preserving) flow
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}:=(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on a measure space
(X,\mu)
, we can investigate what happens when we
rescale
time linearly, namely consider, for a given
\kappa\neq 0
, the flow
\varphi^{\kappa}_{\mathbb{R}}:=(\varphi^{\kappa}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
where
\varphi^{\kappa}_{t}:=\varphi_{\kappa t}
. Even such a simple time-reparametrization can drastically change the ergodic theoretical properties of the flow. In particular, a non-trivial rescaling
\varphi^{\kappa}_{\mathbb{R}}
(where by non-trivial we mean that
\kappa\neq 1
) can fail to be
isomorphic
to the original flow.
Disjointness
can be seen as a strong form of lack of isomorphism. There are several notions of disjointness, for example
spectral disjointness
(see §
2.2
).
We will focus in this paper on
disjointness in the sense of Furstenberg
, or absence of common joinings (see §
2.2
for definitions), a notion introduced by Furstenberg in
[
Fu
]
.

1.1.2.
Disjointness to study Moebius orthogonality

A surge of activity in the investigation of
disjointness
in several classes of entropy zero flows, was initially motivated by a famous conjecture by Sarnak
[
2
,
3
]
on orthogonality of the Moebius functions with systems of zero entropy, see for example the survey
[
1
]
and the references therein. Disjointness of rescalings has been exploited in several recent results (starting from the seminal work in this direction
[
BSZ
]
by Bourgain, Sarnak and Ziegler on horocycle flows) as a key tool which implies orthogonality with the Moebius (and other multiplicative) functions.

This recent activity on disjointness has at the same time led to the realization that
the property of
disjointness of rescalings
may be a distinctive phenomenon for
parabolic
flows (see for example
[
KLU
]
and the references therein). It is in this spirit that we push the investigation of this property within the fundamental class of smooth area-preserving flows on surfaces.

As a disclaimer, although several results on disjointness of rescalings were also proved for entropy zero
maps
(see e.g.
[
CE
]
and
[
BK
]
for reducible IETs or
[
ALR
]
for rank one transformations)
we focus in this paper on
flows
, and hence provide only a brief survey of the literature on disjointness in entropy zero
flows
, known as
parabolic flows
.

1.1.3.
Parabolic flows

Parabolic flows (and more in general parabolic dynamical systems) are flows which display
slow
sensitive dependence on initial conditions, in the sense that the speed of divergence of nearby orbits is subexponential, typically polynomial or subpolynomial (in contrast with
hyperbolic flows
, which exhibit exponential divergence), see for example the surveys
[
Fo
,
Ul13
]
. Classical examples of parabolic flows include the
horocycle
flow on a surface with constant negative curvature and its time-changes, time-changes of nilflows on nilmanifolds and smooth area-preserving flows on surfaces (for which we refer the interested reader to the surveys
[
Ul23
,
FU23
]
and the references therein).

One of the key discoveries by Marina Ratner in her seminal work in homogeneous dynamics was the phenomenon of
joining rigidity
of (time-changes of) horocycle and unipotent flows: informally, two such flows (or rescalings) are disjoint unless there is an algebraic reason for them to be isomorphic, see e.g.
[
Rat86
,
Rat87
]
. The investigation of joining rigidity of time-changes of horocycle and unipotent flow is still an active area of research, as demonstrated by the several results recently proved, see for example
[
FF
,
AFR
,
DKW
,
Ta22
,
Ta23
]
.

If we consider disjointness of
rescalings
,
the standard horocycle flow
h_{\mathbb{R}}
on
SL(2,\mathbb{R})/SL(2,\mathbb{Z})

fails
to have such property: indeed
h_{\mathbb{R}}
is
conjugated
to (and hence in particular
not disjoint
from) all its rescalings (via the geodesic flow
g_{t}
, in view of the relation
g_{t}\circ h_{s}=h_{e^{t}}s\circ g_{t}
).
However, for many non-trivial
time-changes
(also called time
reparametrizations
) of horocycle flows, different
rescalings
(i.e.
p\neq q
) are disjoint, see
[
KLU
,
FF
]
. This can be interpreted by saying that the (standard) horocycle flow (in view of its homogeneity and hence additional symmetries) is rather an
exception
among reparametrizations of horocycle flows:
typical
time-changes
do
display disjointness of rescalings.

Results on disjointness and disjointness of rescalings for other classes of parabolic flows are not many. In the context of nilflows on nilmanifolds, Furstenberg disjointness of rescalings was only proved for typical time-changes of special (bounded type) Heisenberg nilflows by Forni and Kanigowski
[
FoKa
]
. The problem for higher rank Heisenberg flows, and even for typical (Diophantine) Heisenberg nilflows, remains currently widely open.

1.1.4.
Disjoint rescalings for surface flows

In the context of smooth area-preserving flows, some results on disjointness of rescalings were proved, but up to now (to the best of our knowledge), typicality of this property was only shown to hold in some genus one settings, that we now recall.

In
[
KLU
]
, Kanigowski, Lemanczyk and the second author proved that Furstenberg disjointness of rescalings holds for typical
Arnold flows
on genus one surfaces: these are given by (the restriction to the unique minimal component of) a flow on a torus with one center and one saddle.
Arnold flows admit a representation as special flows over a rotation of the circle, under a roof function with so called
logarithmic asymmetric singularities
(see §
2.1.6
).

The same type of special representation, when the base is an interval exchange transformation, provides a description of minimal components of locally Hamiltonian flows on higher genus surfaces (in presence of a saddle loop homologous to zero). A typical flow on a higher genus surface (i.e. a surface with genus
g\geqslant 2
) which is
minimal
, on the other hand, admits a representation
as a special flows over a IET under a roof function with
symmetric
logarithmic singularities (see the definitions in §
2.1.5
).
The special case in which the base is a rotation (which does not correspond to any locally Hamiltonian flow, but provides the prototype for the study of minimal locally Hamiltonian flows in higher genus) was studied by Kanigowski and the first author in
[
BK
]
, where they showed that distinct
rational
rescalings (i.e. rescalings of the form
\varphi^{\kappa}_{\mathbb{R}}
where
\kappa=p/q\in\mathbb{Q}
) of a typical such flow are disjoint.

Another class of special flows for which spectral disjointness of powers was proved, namely so-called
von Neumann flows
. This class (introduced in
[
vN
]
to produce examples of systems with continuous spectrum)
contains special flows over rotations under a piecewise linear roof;
one can show that they arise from the study of linear flows on surfaces with boundary, see
[
FL09
,
CF
]
.
In
[
DK
]
, Kanigowski and
Dong showed that
any
two different rescalings of a Von Neumann flow are disjoint, unless they are isomorphic. In
[
BK
]
, Kanigowski and the first author also consider more generally
von Neumann flows
in higher genus (special flows over interval exchanges under a piece-wise linear roof). They prove that for a typical IET, distinct rational rescalings of any special flow with a piece-wise linear roof function, are disjoint.

1.2.
Main result and open questions

The results summarized above indicate that disjointness of rescalings may be a typical feature of parabolic flows and lead to the natural program of pushing the investigation of this property further in the realm of parabolic dynamics. The first natural question is whether disjointness of rescalings (in Furstenberg or spectral sense, for rational or almost every rescaling) can be proved for smooth area preserving flows on surfaces of higher genus, i.e. of genus
g\geqslant 2
. Our main result is the following:

Theorem 1.1

(Disjointness of rational rescalings)
.

For a
typical
locally Hamiltonian flow
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
on a surface
M
of any genus
g\geqslant 2
with only simple saddles, any two rational distinct rescalings
\varphi^{\kappa}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varphi^{\kappa^{\prime}}_{\mathbb{R}}
with
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
, such that
|\kappa|\neq|\kappa^{\prime}|
, are
disjoint
in the sense of Furstenberg.

We will provide below the definition of
disjoint
in the sense of Furstenberg. The notion of
typical
, used here in a
measure theoretic sense
, i.e. gives a full measure set with respect to a natural measure class on locally Hamiltonian flows with given singularity types, sometimes referred to as the Katok fundamental class; see §
2.1.2
for definitions.

Let us mention a different disjointness result (from some point of view
weaker
, although of spectral nature) which was recently proved in higher genus.
Instead of asking whether
time-changes
of a typical flows in a certain class are disjoint, or have disjoint rescalings, one can first ask, whether two typical flows from a given (parabolic)
class
are disjoint.
Under the assumption that all singularities are
non-degenerate
, i.e. that all saddles are
simple
, within the open set
1
1

1

Here
open
refers to the topology given by small perturbations by a closed
1
-form. Locally Hamiltonian flows
with only
non-degenerate
(or Morse-type) singularities are indeed divided
in two open sets, often denoted in the literature by
\mathcal{U}_{min}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\neg min}
: in
\mathcal{U}_{min}
the typical flow is
minimal
(as well as
weak mixing

[
Ul09
]
, but not
mixing

[
Ul11
]
).
In the open set
\mathcal{U}_{\neg min}
on the other hand, the typical flow decomposes into (open) union of closed orbits and
invariant subsurfaces (with boundary) on which the restriction of the flow is minimal (the
minimal components
, which are at most
g
, where
g
is the genus), see e.g.
[
Mai
]
, on which the flow is

mixing

[
Ul07
,
Rav17
]
, as well as mixing of all orders
[
KKU
]
.

of minimal locally Hamiltonian flows (or equivalently special flows under roofs with logarithmic
symmetric
singularities),
it was very recently proved in
[
FKU
]
by Frączek, Kanigowski and the second author that
the typical
pair
of such flows is spectrally disjoint.

It is also natural to ask whether
spectral
(rather than Furstenberg) disjointness holds, at least in the genus two setting. Although our proof is inspired by the proof by Kanigowski and the first author of
spectral
disjointness of rescalings (i.e. we use the same type of disjointness criterion) for their analogous genus one setting (namely special flows over rotations under a symmetric logarithm), we pursue a different strategy (as explained in §
1.3
) because of the difficulties of controlling the tails in the higher genus case
2
2

2

One of the crucial differences when passing from rotations (that can be seen as interval exchange transformations with
d=2
intervals) to
interval exchange transformations
(IETs) with
d\geqslant 2
intervals is that the latter fail
Denjoy-Koksma
inequalities and the cohomological equations cannot in general be solved. This leads to a
centering
problem of special Birkhoff sums, see §
1.3
).
, which leads only to the proof of Furstenberg disjointness.

Finally, the question of disjointness of rescalings should also be investigated for the generalization of
Arnold flows
(i.e. special flows under asymmetric logarithmic roofs, see §
2.1.5
) to higher genus
g\geqslant 2
, i.e.
minimal components
of locally Hamiltonian flows with saddle loops homologous to zero
3
3

3

This is the open set often denoted in the literature as
\mathcal{U}_{\neg min}
, see footnote
1
.
. In this setting, Furstenberg disjointness of rescalings
is an open problem as soon as
g>1
(it was only proved in

[
KLU
]
for Arnold flows, i.e. for
g=1
). The stronger notion of
spectral
disjointness of rescalings is open even for Arnold flows with genus one (i.e. special flows over rotations).
This seems a difficult problem, since already the nature of the spectrum of Arnold flows is an open question (see e.g.
[
FKU
]
and the spectral questions therein).

1.3.
Strategy of the proof and bounded-type rigidity

We now conclude the introduction by highlighting some of the novel ideas in
the proof. In particular, we introduce the use of what we call
bounded type rigidity

times, which we build for an IET using a geometrically based mechanism of

degeneration
to a two-tower rigidity (produced constructing
renormalization times where a surface, of which the IET is a Poincaré
section, degenerates to a torus with a bounded type irrational flow, see
§
3.1.2
for details).

1.3.1.
Reduction to Birkhoff sums distributions

To study ergodic and spectral properties of locally Hamiltonian flows, it is
standard to exploit their representation as special flows over an IET (or a
rotation when
g=1
) that we will denote by
T
. The growth of the

Birkhoff sums

S_{n}f=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}f\circ T^{k}
of the roof function

f
and its derivatives plays a crucial role in the proof of properties such as
mixing, weak mixing, multiple mixing, as well as spectral and disjointness
properties (see e.g.
[
Ul09
,
KhSi
,
Ul07
,
Rav17
,
KKU
,
FFK
,
KLU
,
FKU
]
). Similarly, our approach to disjointness of rescalings is also based on the study of Birkhoff sums
S_{n}f
, but rather than of estimates on the growth of their derivatives (which are crucial to study mixing, as well as Ratner forms of shearing, as in
[
Rav17
,
KKU
]
), we are interested in the
distribution
of
S_{n}f-n\int\!f
(seen as a random variable where the initial point
x
is chosen uniformly with respect to Lebesgue measure).

1.3.2.
The disjointness criterion

The
criterion
that we use for proving
disjointness
of special flows (stated in §
2.2.3
) comes from
[
FL04
]
as well as from
[
BFdlR
]

and is based on the study of the
tails
of Birkhoff sums distributions at times where they display a strong form of
tightness
:
heuristically, if one can find a sequence of
rigidity times

(r_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the Birkhoff sums
S_{r_{n}}(f)
are
tight
(in the sense of random variables) and their tail
decays exponentially
and, furthermore, multiples
S_{2r_{n}}(f)
,
S_{3r_{n}}(f),\dots,S_{kr_{n}}(f)
have
tails
of the same order,
one can show that the flow
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
and its rescaling
\varphi^{k}_{\mathbb{R}}
are disjoint (Proposition
2.11
provides the formal statement).

We remark that
tightness
of Birkhoff sums is a feature of
flows which display
absence of mixing
. In a weaker form (tightness along
partial
rigidity sets), it suffices indeed to prevent mixing
4
4

4

An important early criterion for absence of mixing appears in Katok’s work
[
Ka80
]
, which shows that special flows over IETs under roof functions of bounded variation are never mixing, and by Kochergin’s, which shows the absence of mixing for special flows over rotations under a roof with a symmetric logarithmic singularity (see
[
Ko72
,
Ko07
]
). Both criteria require as input
tightness
of Birkhoff sums along some subsequences of
rigidity
(or
partial rigidity
)
times
, i.e. one has to show that there exists a sequence
(q_{n})
of times such that
T^{q_{n}}
converges to identity on subsets
E_{n}
of measure tending to one (if there is rigidity, or measure bounded below in the case of partial rigidity) and at the same time, for some centralizing sequence
(a_{n})
and uniform constant
C
,

Leb\{x\in E_{n}|\ |S_{q_{n}}(f)(x)-a_{n}|<C\}/Leb(E_{n})\to 1
.

see e.g.
[
Ka80
,
Ko72
,
Ko07
,
Sch
,
Ul11
]
. An idea that goes back to
work of Frączek and Lemańczyk

[
FL05
]
, is that if in addition
to
tightness
, one can also control the
tails
of the distribution of the centralized Birkhoff sums
S_{q_{n}}(f)(x)-a_{n}
, one can prove much stronger results (using joinings and Markov operators), in particular spectral disjointness from mixing flows and singularity of the spectrum. This approach lead to the proof of singularity of the spectrum in genus two
[
CFKU
]
and recently in any genus
[
FKU
]
.

1.3.3.
Bounded type degeneration

The main difficulty that we had to overcome is that these by now
classical
rigidity times are
not suitable
to study and
differentiate
the (exponentially small) tails in higher genus to verify the assumptions for disjointness. Roughly speaking, if
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is such a sequence of rigidity times, then the values of Birkhoff sums
S_{q_{n}}(f)(T^{kq_{n}}x)
are essentially the same for a typical point from the interval. To be able to distinguish the tails of distributions Birkhoff sums for different rescalings, we introduce and exploit a new mechanism for tightness: instead than tightness times produced essentially using one large Rohlin tower (or, at the surface level, a large
almost cylinder
), we exploit a new type of rigidity times, which we call
bounded-type
rigidity times (or for short, BT rigid times). The name refers to the type of rigidity displayed by a
bounded-type
rotation, which can be modelled as a
two
Rohlin towers picture for IET (or, geometrically, two rectangles with suitable glueings), see §
3.1.2
.

These times are constructed controlling the degeneration of a surface suspending the IET along the Teichmüller geodesic flow (using ergodicity of
Rauzy-Veech induction) and using
rectangle representations
(a slight generalization of the zippered rectangles construction, see §
3.1.1
) to then infer the existence of suitable Rohlin towers for the IET (see §
3.2
for details).

The geometric picture given by these two towers degeneration allows at the same time to produce rigidity and
match
pieces of orbits which travel together (a mechanism that we call
matching
of orbits and of Birkhoff sums, which is explained in Section
4.1.3
), but also to distinguish two rescalings through the distribution of their tails, since when considering successive
multiples
of this type of rigidity time, since the
closest visit
to the singularity (which is responsible for the tail of the distribution)
moves away
by a definite amount.

We stress that the strategy exploited to study rescalings and their disjointness by Kanigowski and the first author in
[
BK
]
and here are quite different (and the first yields spectral disjointness, while we can only prove Furstenberg disjointness).
The main difference comes from the fact that, unlike in
[
BK
]
, here we do not have control on the distributions of Birkhoff sums of the roof function over the whole interval. Furthermore, it turns out that the measure of the tails (which we use to differentiate between the rescalings) on one hand, and the measure of the set where we do not have the control over the values of Birkhoff sums on the other, are closely tied and related to the bounded rigidity scale. Thus, while in
[
BK
]
the authors could choose to work with arbitrarily large tails (since showing positivity of the measure of the differentiating set was sufficient), in our setting we need to make much more careful choices, since, to guarantee that we can use the criterion for disjointness (see Proposition
2.11
), the measure of tails has to be substantially larger than the measure of the non-controlled set.

1.4.
Structure of the paper

In Section
2
we gather background definitions and material. We first recall in §
2.1
some background on locally Hamiltonian flows and their reduction to special flows in the setting we are studying. We then recall in §
2.2
basic joinings and disjointness notions and then state the criterion that we will use to prove disjointness of rescalings in special flows (Proposition
2.11
). Some basics on translation surfaces appear in
§
2.3
.

In Section
3
we motivate and define the new type of rigidity times we are going to use, that we call BT rigid (short for
bounded type
rigid) (see Definition
3.1
). These times are used to
match
Birkhoff sums, an idea which is first motivated and then explained in §
4.1.3
.
The classical estimates on (Birkhoff sums of) functions with symmetric logarithmic singularities that are needed for the proof of exponential tails are collected in Section
4
. Assuming the existence of a suitable sequence of
good
BT rigidity times (see Definition
4.16
), the proof that exponential tails follow from these estimates, is also provided in this section (see in particular §
4.3
).

In Section
5
we prove the existence of good BT rigidity times, in which, using results by the last author from
[
Ul11
]
(see also
[
FKU
]
) one also has a good control on (trimmed) Birkhoff sums of the derivatives. This section requires the use of Rauzy-Veech induction and its natural extension. The needed facts on Rauzy-Veech induction used are stated in §
5.1
.
Finally, in the final Section
6
, we conclude the proof of disjointness by estimating the measures of the tails of Birkhoff sums distributions for different rescalings at good BT rigid times.

2.
Background material

This background section consists of three subsections, dealing respectively
with locally Hamiltonian flows and their representation as special flows (see
§
2.1
), joinings and disjointness notions and the disjointness
criterion (see §
2.2
) and basics on translation surfaces
in §
2.3
.

2.1.
Locally Hamiltonian flows and their special flow representations

In this section we first recall some basic notions on locally Hamiltonian
flows (from §
2.1.1
to §
2.1.2
) and the definition
of special flows with logarithmic singularities over IETs (see
§
2.1.3
to §
2.1.5
). Then we state the properties of the
special flow representations of the flows were are interested (see
§
2.1.6
).

2.1.1.
Locally Hamiltonian flows

Throughout the paper let
S
denote a smooth, compact, connected, orientable surface with a fixed smooth area form (namely a non-degenerate two form that can be represented in local coordinates on a chart
U\subset S
as
V(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\wedge\mathrm{d}y
positive real valued function
V:U\to\mathbb{R}
). These flows are in one-to-one correspondence with closed smooth
1
-forms on
S
, the form
\eta:=\imath_{X}\omega=\omega(\eta,\,\cdot\,)
, where
\imath_{X}
denotes the contraction operator, being associated to the flow
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
obtained integrating the vector field
X
. The assumption that
\eta
is closed guarantees that
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
preserves the area form and vice versa. The flow
(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is also known in the literature as the
multi-valued Hamiltonian
flow (see
[
No
,
Ar
]
or
locally Hamiltonian flow
(in
[
Ul11
,
Rav17
,
KKU
,
FKU
]
among other more recent papers) associated to
\eta
. Indeed, on each coordinate chart
U\subset S
(but not generally globally) one can find a
local Hamiltonian

H:U\to\mathbb{R}
and coordinates
(x,y)
so that
(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is given by the solution to the equations
\dot{x}={\partial H}/{\partial y}
,
\dot{y}=-{\partial H}/{\partial x}
for some smooth real-valued Hamiltonian function
H
.
When
g\geqslant 2
, the (finite) set of fixed points of
(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is always non-empty. We say that fixed points are
non-degenerate
if the
1
-form
\eta
is
Morse
, i.e. it is locally the differential of a Morse function.

It is well known that any minimal (or minimal component of) locally Hamiltonian flow can be represented as
the special flow
over an
interval exchange transformations
or, for short, IET. We recall this representation, which is the way we will concretely work with these flows in this paper,
in §
2.1.6
.

We will assume in this paper that the surface under consideration has genus
g\geq 2
, that
(\varphi_{t})
is
minimal
and that it has
non-degenerate
fixed points. This implies in particular that there are
2g-2
fixed points, all of which are
simple saddles
(i.e. four-pronged saddles, with two incoming and two outgoing separatrices).

2.1.2.
Katok fundamental class and full measure

Let us denote by
\mathcal{L}_{g}
the set or
locus
(
\mathcal{L}
stands for locus) of minimal locally Hamiltonian flows on a surface of genus
g\geq 2
. When all the singularities of the flow are simple saddles (or equivalently have index
-1
), one can check (e.g. in view of the Poincaré-Bendixon theorem) that their number is exactly
2g-2
. The (measure-theoretical) notion of
typical
on
\mathcal{L}_{g}
(which is the notion used in the statement of Theorem
1.1
) is defined as follows and coincide with the notion of typical induced by the
Katok fundamental class
(introduced by Katok in
[
Ka73
]
, see also
[
NiZh
]
).

Let
\Sigma:=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{2g-2}\}
be the set of saddle points of
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
and let
H_{1}(S,\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
be the relative homology of
S
with respect to
\Sigma:=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{2g-2}\}
. Notice that the dimension
d
of
H_{1}(S,\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
is
2=2g+(2g-2)-1=4g-3
.
Consider the period map
Per:\mathcal{L}_{g}\to\mathbb{R}^{4g-3}
defined as follows. Let
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{4g-3}
be a basis of
H_{1}(S,\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
.
The image of
\eta
by the period map
Per
is
Per(\eta)=(\int_{\gamma_{1}}\eta,\dots,\int_{\gamma_{4g-3}}\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{4g-3}
.
We say that a property holds for a
typical
flow in
\mathcal{L}_{g}
if it
fails
on a set of measure zero with respect to the pull back of the Lebesgue measure class via
Per:\mathcal{L}_{g}\to\mathbb{R}^{4g-3}
, namely it fails on the preimage
Per^{-1}(Z)
of a set
Z\subset\mathbb{R}^{4g-3}
with
Leb(Z)=0
. In §
2.1.6
, we give a reformulation of this notion of typical in terms of special flows representations (see Lemma
2.4
).

2.1.3.
Interval exchange transformations

We now recall the definitions of interval exchange transformations, which appear as Poincaré sections of locally Hamiltonian flows (as well as translation flows and billiards in rational polygons). The locally Hamiltonian flow will then be recovered as a
special flow (§
2.1.4
) over an IET (see §
2.1.6
).

Let
I:=[0,|I|)
. An interval exchange transformation (IET) of
d
intervals
T:I\to I
with
combinatorics

\pi
, where
\pi:\{{1},\dots,d\}\to\{{1},\dots,d\}
is a permutation of
d
symbols) and
endpoints
(of the continuity intervals)

0=:\beta_{0}<\beta_{1}<\dots\beta_{d-1}<\beta_{d}:=|I|

is a piecewise isometry which sends the interval
I_{i}:=[\beta_{i},\beta_{i+1})
, for
0\leqslant i<d
,
by a translation, explicitly given by

T(x)=x-\beta_{i}+\beta_{\pi(i)},\qquad\text{if }\ x\in[\beta_{i},\beta_{i+1}).

We say that
\pi:\{1,\dots,d\}\to\{1,\dots,d\}
is
standard
if
\pi(1)=d
and
\pi(d)=1
. It turns out that by suitably choosing Poincaré sections of translation flows, in every stratum the first return map to this section is an IET whose combinatorial data is given by the standard permutation (see
[
Rau
]
, as well as Lemma
2.4
).

Let us denote by
Disc(T)
the set of endpoints of continuity intervals of
T
(that we call
discontinuity
set), namely

(2.1)

Disc(T):=\{\beta_{0}:=0,\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{d-1},\beta_{d}:=|I|\}

We say that
T
satisfies
Keane condition
if
T^{m}(\beta_{i})\neq\beta_{j}
, for all
m\geq 1
and for all
i,j\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
, except
j=0
with
m=1
. Keane
[
Kea
]
showed that an IET with an irreducible permutation that satisfies the Keane condition is
minimal
.

We say that a result holds
for almost every IET with combinatorics

\pi
if it holds for almost every choice of the lengths
|I_{i}|=\beta_{i+1}-\beta_{i}
of the exchanged intervals, with respect to the restriction of the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the simplex
\Delta_{d-1}=\{(\lambda_{1},\dots\lambda_{d})
such that
\lambda_{i}\geqslant 0
and
\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}=1\}
.

Given
x\in I
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
the
orbit segment
of length
r
starting from
x
, namely

(2.2)

\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r):=\{x,T(x),T^{2}(x),\dots,T^{r-1}(x)\}.

Finally, for any
\beta\in Disc(T)
we denote by
m(x,\beta,r)
the
minimum distance of
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
to the singularity
\beta
of
T
given by

(2.3)

m(x,\beta,r):=\min_{j=0,\ldots,r-1}|T^{j}x-\beta|.

2.1.4.
Special flows

Let us now recall the definition of
special flow
. Let
T
be an automorphism of a standard (Borel) probability space
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
. Let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be an integrable function so that
\inf_{x\in X}f(x)>0
. Let us denote by
S_{n}(f)(x)
the
Birkhoff sum
defined by

S_{n}(f)(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sum_{0\leqslant i<n}f(T^{i}x)&\text{if}&n\geqslant 0\\
-\sum_{n\leqslant i<0}f(T^{i}x)&\text{if}&n<0.\end{array}\right.

The
special flow

(T^{f}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
built
over
the automorphism
T
and
under
the
roof function

f
acts
on

X^{f}:=\{(x,r)\in X\times\mathbb{R}:0\leqslant r<f(x)\}

and is given by

T^{f}_{t}(x,r)=(T^{n}x,r+t-S_{n}(f)(x)),

where
n=n(t,x)\in\mathcal{Z}
is a unique integer number with
S_{n}(f)(x)\leqslant r+t<S_{n+1}(f)(x)
. Under the action of
(T^{f}_{t})_{t>0}
, a point
(x,y)\in X_{f}
moves with unit velocity along the vertical line up to the point
(x,f(x))
, then jumps instantly to the point
\left(T(x),0\right)
, according to the base transformation and afterward it continues its motion along the vertical line until the next jump and so on. The integer
n(t,x)
(for
t>0
) is the number of discrete iterations of the map
T
undergone by the orbit of
x
up to time
t
.

The flow
(T^{f}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
preserves the finite measure
\mu^{f}
which is the restriction of
\mu\times\ Leb_{\mathbb{R}}
to
X^{f}
. The
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}^{f}
on
X^{f}
is
\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
restricted to
X^{f}
.
If
T
is ergodic with respect to
\mu
, it is easy to see then
(T^{f}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is also ergodic (with respect to
\mu^{f}
), see e.g.
[
CFS
]
.

2.1.5.
Roofs with logarithmic singularities

We now define the class of roof functions we will work with, namely roofs with (symmetric) logarithmic singularities, which arise as roof functions in the special flow representation of locally Hamiltonian flows with non-degenerate (
isomorphic
) saddles.

Let
T
be an IET with continuity intervals
I_{0},\dots,I_{d-1}
and endpoints
0:=\beta_{0}<\beta_{1}<\dots\beta_{d-1}<\beta_{d}:=|I|
(see §
2.1.3
).

Definition 2.1

(logarithmic singularities)
.

We say that a function
f
has
pure logarithmic singularities
(at the endpoints of
T
) and write
f\in\ \mathcal{P}Log\left(T\right)
if it is of the form

(2.4)

f(x)=\sum_{0\leqslant i<d}\chi_{(\beta_{i},\beta_{i+1})}(x)\big(-C_{i}^{+}\ \log(x-\beta_{i})-C_{i+1}^{-}\ \log(\beta_{i+1}-x)\big),

for some constants
C_{i}^{\pm}\geqslant 0
, not all simultaneously zero.

We say that
f
has
pure symmetric logarithmic singularities
(at the endpoints of
T
) and write
f\in{\mathcal{P}SymLog\left(T\right)}
if in addition we have that
\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{-}
.

We say that
f
has
logarithmic singularities
(resp.
symmetric logarithmic singularities
) and write
f\in\ Log^{2}{\left(\sqcup_{i=0}^{d-1}I_{i}\right)}
(resp.
f\in SymLog^{2}\left({\sqcup_{i=0}^{d-1}I_{i}}\right)
) if and only if
f
can be written as
f=f^{p}+g
where
f^{p}\in\mathcal{P}Log{\left(T\right)}
(resp.
f\in\mathcal{P}SymLog{\left(T\right)}
) has pure logarithmic (symmetric) singularities and
g:I\to\mathbb{R}
is such that the restrictions
g_{i}:=g|I_{i}:I_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
to each continuity interval
I_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
, extend to a twice differentiable function on the closure
\overline{I}_{i}
, i.e. are restriction of
g_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\overline{I_{i}})
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
.

Remark 2.2
.

In the literature, when one says that
f
has (symmetric) logarithmic
singularities, and writes
f\in SymLog(T)
, it is often only assumed that

f=f^{p}+g
where
f^{p}
as here is in
\mathcal{P}Log{\left(T\right)}
but
g

is only assumed to have
bounded variation
. The apex
2
in the notation

SymLog^{2}(T)
denotes this additional assumption
5
5

5

In
[
FKU
]
the
notation
SymLog^{2+}(T)
is used to recall that
g

extends
to a

\mathcal{C}^{2}
function on each closure
I_{i}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
; we choose to
leave the
+
not to make the notation too heavy.
.
We need the (twice) differentiability of
f
on each
I_{i}
essentially in the proofs in this paper, but one can show that the roofs which arise in representations of locally Hamiltonian flows on surfaces automatically do satisfy this additional regularity assumption (see the reduction explained in §
2.1.6
, in particular Lemma
2.4
and its proof,
or directly
[
FKU
]
for the result used in the reduction).

Notice that the signs in (
2.4
) are chosen so that
f\geqslant 0
. We remark that we allow some of the
C_{i}^{\pm}
to be zero; so
f
could have a finite one-sided limit at some
\beta_{i}
(but we assume that at least one of the singularities is indeed logarithmic).
Let us also introduce a notation for the constant obtained summing the constants
C_{i}^{\pm}
:

Definition 2.3
.

Given
f\in\mathcal{P}SymLog(T)
of the form (
2.4
), we define
C_{f}
to be the constant

C_{f}:=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{-}.

2.1.6.
Reduction to symmetric special flows

The following Lemma provides the reduction of a locally Hamiltonian flow in
\mathcal{L}_{g}
to a special flow
over a symmetric IET, under a roof with symmetric logarithmtic singularities.

Lemma 2.4

(Special flow reduction for flows in
\mathcal{L}_{g}
)
.

If
S
has genus
g\geq 2
and
(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\in\mathcal{L}_{g}
is a minimal locally Hamiltonian flow with
2g-2
non-degenerate simple saddles
p_{1},\ldots,p_{2g-2}\in M
, then it is isomorphic to a special flow over an IET
T
with a standard permutation
\pi
with
d=4g-3
and roof
f\in SymLog^{2}{\left(T\right)}
.
Furthermore, for a property to be
typical
within
\mathcal{L}_{g}
(in the sense of §
2.1.2
), it is enough to show that for almost every IET with standard permutation
\pi
(see §
2.1.3
) with
d=4g-3
, every special flow with symmetric logarithmic singularities
f\in SymLog^{2}{\left(T\right)}
has such property.

The proof of Lemma
2.4
is essentially given in
[
CFKU
]

(see in particular Lemma 2.1 and Corollary 2.2 in Section 2.7 in

[
CFKU
]
), where the precise form of the constants
C^{\pm}_{i}
is
computed, although it is only shown that
f\in SymLog(T)
(see
Remark
2.2
), namely that

g
in the decomposition
f=f_{p}+g
has bounded variation. The proof that one can choose a suitable cross section of the flow for the special representation to ensure the additional regularity assumptions of
g
(i.e. to show that
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
, see Definition
2.1
) is given in
[
FKU
]
(see the proof of Theorem 3 in § 3.1 in
[
FKU
]
).

2.2.
Joinings and disjointness criteria

In this section we recall the definition of joinings and
the definition of
disjointness in the sense of Furstenberg
, introduced by Hillel Furstenberg in
[
Fu
]
. We then state a criterion for disjointness of two flows, which
will provide the key tool to prove spectral disjointness of rescalings in this paper.

2.2.1.
Disjointness

Let
\phi_{\mathbb{R}}=(\phi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
\psi_{\mathbb{R}}=(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
be two measure preserving flows acting respectively on standard Borel probability spaces
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
and
(Y,\mathcal{C},\nu)
. A
joining

\rho
between
\phi_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\psi_{\mathbb{R}}
is a
\phi_{\mathbb{R}}\times\psi_{\mathbb{R}}
invariant probability measure such that
\rho(B\times Y)=\mu(B)
and
\rho(X\times C)=\nu(C)
, for all
B\in\mathcal{B}
and
C\in\mathcal{C}
.
The set of joinings is denoted by
J(\phi_{\mathbb{R}},\psi_{\mathbb{R}})
. Two flows are
disjoint
in the sense of Furstenberg if
J(\phi_{\mathbb{R}},\psi_{\mathbb{R}})=\{\mu\otimes\nu\}
. In particular, it implies that the flows are non-isomorphic. We denote by
J_{2}(\phi_{\mathbb{R}}):=J(\phi_{\mathbb{R}},\phi_{\mathbb{R}})
the set of
self-joinings
of
\phi_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We now present a criterion of two flows being disjoint. Our final goal is to obtain a version of this criterion, useful for our purposes. However, we start from recalling the following classical definition.

Definition 2.5
.

We say that a measure
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
has
exponential decay
if there exist constants
c,b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

P\big((-\infty,-t)\cup(t,\infty)\big)<ce^{-bt}\ \text{ for every }\ t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

Let us also recall that, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the
Koopman

operator
associated to the automorphism
\phi_{t}
, which, abusing the notation, we will denote also by
\phi_{t}
, is the operator

\phi_{t}:L^{2}(X,\mu)\to L^{2}(X,\mu)\quad\text{ given by \ \ }\phi_{t}(f)=f\circ\phi_{t}.

Moreover, for every
P
, the probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
, we consider also the
integral operator

P(\phi)
, given by the formula

P(\phi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{t}\,dP(t).

2.2.2.
A disjointness criterion

The following lemma gives a criterion for two flows to be disjoint in the sense of Furstenberg.

Lemma 2.6

(Furstenberg disjointness criterion)
.

Assume that
\phi_{\mathbb{R}}=(\phi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
\psi_{\mathbb{R}}=(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
are weakly mixing
6
6

6

Note that assuming weak mixing is necessary: indeed, if a flow
(\phi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
has a non-zero eigenvalue
a\in\mathbb{R}
then
KLa
is a common eigenvalue for
(\phi_{Kt})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
(\phi_{Lt})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
for every
K,L\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus the natural powers of a non-trivial ergodic flow which is not weakly mixing are never pairwise Furstenberg disjoint.
flows on probability spaces
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
and
(Y,\mathcal{C},\nu)
respectively. Suppose that there exists a sequence
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
increasing to infinity, a number
\gamma>0
and linear operators
U
on
L^{2}(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
and
V
on
L^{2}(Y,\mathcal{C},\nu)
such that

(2.5)

\phi_{t_{n}}\to(1-\gamma)P(\phi)+\gamma\,U\quad\text{and}\quad\psi_{t_{n}}\to(1-\gamma)Q(\psi)+\gamma\,V\quad\text{weakly},

where
P,Q
are probability measures with exponential decay on
\mathbb{R}
. If there exists a Borel set
A\in\mathbb{R}
such that
P(A)-Q(A)>\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}
then
\phi_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\psi_{\mathbb{R}}
are disjoint in the sense of Furstenberg.

This Lemma can be seen as a special
case of Proposition 3.2 in
[
BFdlR
]
.
7
7

7

In
[
BFdlR
]
, the language of
Markov operators

(a generalization of Koopman operators), as well as the language of

3
-joinings is used (see the proof of Proposition 3.2). However, to
prove Lemma
2.6
, only
2
-joinings are needed, and the proof
(which we sketch here) can be obtained by considering a projection on
two coordinates in the proof of Proposition 3.2 in
[
BFdlR
]
.

We finish this section by explaining a simple argument, that will allow us to focus only on positive rescalings of considered flows.

Lemma 2.7
.

Assume that
\phi_{\mathbb{R}}=(\phi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is a flow for which there exists a sequence
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
increasing to infinity, a number
\gamma>0
and a linear operator
U
on
L^{2}(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
such that

\phi_{t_{n}}\to(1-\gamma)\int\phi_{t}\,dQ(t)+\gamma\,U\quad\text{weakly},

where
Q
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Then

\phi_{-t_{n}}\to(1-\gamma)\int\phi_{-t}\,dQ(t)+\gamma\,U^{*}\quad\text{weakly},

where
U^{*}
is the dual operator of
U
.

Proof.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
be arbitrary. By the assumption of the lemma, we have

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle f\circ\phi_{-t_{n}},g\rangle&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle f,g\circ\phi_{t_{n}}\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{\langle g\circ\phi_{t_{n}},f\rangle}=\overline{\Big\langle(1-\gamma)\int g\circ\phi_{t}\,dQ(t)+\gamma\,U(g),f\Big\rangle}\\
&=(1-\gamma)\int\overline{\langle g\circ\phi_{t},f\rangle\,dQ(t)}+\gamma\,\overline{\langle U(g),f\rangle}=(1-\gamma)\int\overline{\langle g,f\circ\phi_{-t}\rangle\,dQ(t)}+\gamma\,\overline{\langle g,U^{*}(f)\rangle}\\
&=(1-\gamma)\int{\langle f\circ\phi_{-t},g\rangle\,dQ(t)}+\gamma\,{\langle U^{*}(f),g\rangle}=\Big\langle(1-\gamma)\int f\circ\phi_{-t}\,dQ(t)+\gamma\,U^{*}(f),g\Big\rangle.\end{split}

Since
f
and
g
were arbitrary, this means that
\varphi_{t_{n}}
converges weakly to the desired limit and hence finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

If
\phi_{\mathbb{R}}=(\phi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
\psi_{\mathbb{R}}=(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
are two flows satisfying the assumptions of Lemma
2.6
, then
(\phi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is disjoint to both
(\psi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
(\psi_{-t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
.

2.2.3.
Disjointness criterion for special flows

In the setting of special flows, the following criterion for Furstenberg disjointness of
rescalings
was proven in
[
FL05
]
and in more general version in
[
BF
]
.
Let us first recall the definition of
partial rigidity
.

Definition 2.9
.

We say that
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
a partial rigidity sequence
for a dynamical system
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu,T)
if there exists a sequence
(W_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of measurable subsets
W_{n}\in\mathcal{B}
, called

partial rigidity sets
, and
0<\gamma<1
, such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu(W_{n})=1-\gamma

and for every measurable
A\subset\mathcal{B}
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu((A\triangle T^{q_{n}}A)\cap W_{n})=0.

Remark 2.10
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
is endowed with a metric
d

generating the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
. If

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in W_{n}}d(T^{q_{n}}x,x)=0,

then
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a rigidity
sequence for
T
with rigidity sets
(W_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. A proof of this observation can be found for example in
[
BF
]
.

The following proposition will be used as a tool to proving Furstenberg disjointness of different rescalings of a special flow
(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and to reduce it to the study of the distribution of Birkhoff sums
S_{q_{n}}f
of the roof function
f
along a sequence of rigidity times. More precisely, we consider the sequence
(S_{q_{n}}f-a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
where
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
will be chosen appropriately (to
center
Birkhoff sums) and the associated sequence
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of measures on
\mathbb{R}
obtained as pull-back of the invariant measure
\mu
on the base via
(S_{q_{n}}f-a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, namely the measures
P_{n}:=(S_{q_{n}}(f)-a_{n})_{\ast}(\mu)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Explicitly,

(2.6)

P_{n}\left((-\infty,t)\right):=\mu\left(\{x\in X:\;S_{q_{n}}f(x)-a_{n}<t\}\right),\qquad\text{ for every }\ t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

For every measure
\nu
on
(X,\mathcal{B})
we denote by
\nu_{B}
, the conditional measure with respect to
B
, i.e. for every
A\in\mathcal{B}
we have

\nu_{B}:=\frac{\nu(A\cap B)}{\nu(B)}.

The following Proposition is the main criterion used to prove disjointness of rescalings of flows. It follows directly from Theorem 6 in
[
FL05
]
and Lemma
2.6
. For convenience of the reader, we include here its proof, which shows how tail estimates of Birkhoff sums distributions can be used to prove disjointness of special flows.

Proposition 2.11

(Disjointness of powers of special flows)
.

Consider a weakly mixing special flow
T^{f}_{\mathbb{R}}:=(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on probability standard Borel space
(X^{f},\mathcal{B}^{f},\mu^{f})
. Let
K,L\in\mathbb{N}
, with
K<L

Suppose that there exists an increasing sequence of partial rigidity times
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with corresponding partial rigidity sets
(W_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with constant
\gamma>0
. Moreover, suppose that there exists a sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of real numbers
such that the sequences of measures
P_{n}:=(S_{Kq_{n}}(f)-Ka_{n})_{\ast}(\mu_{W_{n}})
and
Q_{n}:=(S_{Lq_{n}}(f)-La_{n})_{\ast}(\mu_{W_{n}})
on
\mathbb{R}

have
uniform
exponential tails, i.e. there exists constants
c,b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(2.7)

\max\{\mu_{W_{n}}\left(\{x\in W_{n}:\;|S_{Kq_{n}}f(x)-Ka_{n}|>t\}\right),\mu_{W_{n}}\left(\{x\in W_{n}:\;|S_{Lq_{n}}f(x)-La_{n}|>t\}\right)\}<ce^{-bt},

for every
t>0
.
Then, if for some
t>0

(2.8)

\begin{split}&\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\mu_{W_{n}}\left(\{x\in W_{n}\;|S_{Kq_{n}}f(x)-Ka_{n}|\geqslant Kt\}\right)\\
&-\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\mu_{W_{n}}\left(\{x\in W_{n}:\;|S_{Lq_{n}}f(x)-La_{n}|\geqslant Lt\}\right)>\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma},\end{split}

then the
K
and
L
rescalings
(T_{Kt}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and
(T_{Lt}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
are disjoint in the sense of Furstenberg.

Remark 2.12
.

In the assumptions of Theorem 6 in
[
FL04
]
, there is a weaker
condition than exponential tails, namely that the condition
(
2.7
) is replaced by

\Big(\int_{W_{n}}|S_{q_{n}}f(x)-a_{n}|^{2}d\,\mu(x)\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\quad\text{is a bounded sequence.}

However, it is an easy exercise that the condition on uniform exponential tails implies the above.

Proof of Proposition
2.11
.

Given a measure
P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
and
w\in\mathbb{R}
, let us denote by
Res_{w}(P)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
the
w
-
rescaling
, namely the measure given by
[Res_{w}(P)](A)=P(w\cdot A)
. Note that if
P
is an absolutely continuous measure with density
x\mapsto f(x)
then
Res_{w}(P)
is also an absolutely continuous measure with density
x\mapsto wf(w\cdot x)
.

By Theorem 6 in
[
FL04
]
, the assumptions of Proposition
2.11
imply that in the weak operator topology, we have the following convergences:

(2.9)

T_{Ka_{n}}^{f}\to(1-\gamma)[Res_{K}]_{\ast}P\left((T^{f}_{Kt})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\right)+\gamma\tilde{P}\quad\text{and}\quad T_{La_{n}}^{f}\to(1-\gamma)[Res_{L}]_{\ast}Q\left((T^{f}_{Lt})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\right)+\gamma\tilde{Q},

where
P:=\lim_{n\to\infty}P_{n}
and
Q:=\lim_{n\to\infty}Q_{n}
and
\tilde{P},\tilde{Q}
are linear operators on
L^{2}(X^{f},\mathcal{B}^{f},\mu^{f})
. Note that
P
and
Q
exist up to taking a subsequence due to (
2.7
) and Prokhorov Theorem.

We now apply Lemma
2.6
to measures
[Res_{K}]_{\ast}P
and
[Res_{L}]_{\ast}Q
, considering the
tail
set

A:=(-\infty,-t]\cup[t,\infty).

Then it remains to notice, that the assumption (
2.8
) gives exactly that

[Res_{K}]_{\ast}P(A)-[Res_{L}]_{\ast}Q(A)>\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma},

so that the conclusion of Proposition
2.11
follows from the Furstenberg disjointness criterion (Lemma
2.6
).
∎

2.3.
Translation surfaces and suspensions

A translation surface
M
is a closed surface with a Euclidean metric outside a set
\Sigma
consisting of finitely many points where the metric has conical singularities. On
M\backslash\Sigma
, there exists a natural
translation structure
, namely an atlas of charts on
M\setminus\Sigma
such that each transition map is a translation. At each conical singularity, the cone-angle is of the form
2\pi k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by
\mathcal{H}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{d})
the
stratum
consisting of all translation surfaces with
d
singularities with conical angles
2\pi(k_{1}+1),\dots,2\pi(k_{1}+d)
.

Translation surfaces are obtained by gluing polygons in the plane, by identifying pairs of parallel, isometric sides, by translations. We will recall here only few basic properties and the construction of a
suspension
of an IET, namely a translation surface that has the given IET as a Poincaré section, which will be useful in the proof. As a reference on background material on translation surfaces, we refer the
reader to one of the surveys
[
Vi
,
Yo10
]
.

2.3.1.
Suspension polygons.

Fix a
standard
permutation
\pi
of
d
elements and take
\lambda\in\Lambda_{d-1}
. To build a translation surface, we need to also choose a vector
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, known as
suspension datum
, such that for every
1\leq j<d
we have

(2.10)

\sum_{1\leq i<j}\tau_{i}>0\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{i\mid 1\leq\pi(i)<j}\tau_{i}<0.

We denote the set of
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying (
2.10
) by
\Theta_{\pi}
.
We then consider a polygon in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which we identify with
\mathbb{C}
, we made by connecting the vertices given by

v^{+}_{i}:=\sum_{i=1}^{j}(\lambda_{i}+i\tau_{i}),\ \text{for}\ 1\leqslant i\leqslant d,\quad\text{ and}\quad v_{i}^{-}:=\sum_{i=1}^{j}(\lambda_{\pi(i)}+i\tau_{\pi(i)})\ \text{for}\ 1\leqslant i\leqslant d,

and the vertex
v_{0}:=0
. Due to (
2.10
), this polygon is made of two broken lines, one
upper
, whose all non-extreme vertices are above the real axis, made connecting
v_{0},v_{1}^{+},\cdots,v_{d}^{+}
on another,
lower
, obtained connecting
v_{0},v_{1}^{-},\cdots,v_{d}^{-}
whose all non-extreme vertices are below the real axis. Notice that
v_{d}^{+}=v_{d}^{-}
and (because the permutation is symmetric) the two broken lines do not self-intersect and hence bound a polygon (see Figure
1
) with
2d-2
vertices. By construction, for each edge
E^{+}_{i}:=\overline{v_{i}^{+}v^{+}_{i+1}}
of the top line, there exist an edge
E^{-}_{i}:=\overline{v_{j}^{-}v^{-}_{j+1}}
which is parallel and isometric.
By idenfitfying all of such pairs by the unique translation which maps
E^{+}_{i}
to
E^{-}_{i}
, we obtain a
translation surface
that we denote
M=(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
. We denote by
\Sigma=\Sigma(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
the set of vertices of
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
which form the singularities of
M
.

Figure 1.
An example of a polygon representing a translation surface with
\pi=(54321)
.

Finally let us recall that any translation surface can be represented in more than one way as a polygon, for example
cutting
the polygon along a straight line with endpoints in
\sigma
, and
glueing
it back using the identifications of another pairs of sides. The (invertible)
Rauzy-Veech algorithm
, described in Section
5.1.3
, provides a way to produce a sequence of suspensions which all give different polygonal representations of the same translation surface.

2.3.2.
The vertical flow.

On any translation surface
M
one can define a global notion of direction induced by directions on
S^{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and from this one can define directional flows on
M
. We will consider one of such flows in particular, namely the
vertical translation flow

\varphi^{v}_{\mathbb{R}}=(\varphi_{t}^{v})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
which moves points upwards with unit speed. This flow is defined on
M\backslash\Sigma
. We often refer to the points in
\Sigma
as
singularities
of
\varphi^{v}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Conical singularities of angle
4\pi
give rise to simple saddle points of the linear flow. We write
\mathcal{H}(1^{d})
for the so-called
principal
stratum, which consists of translation surfaces with
d
conical angles
4\pi
(and linear flows with only simple saddles). If the surface has genus
g
,
d=2g-2
.
It is easy to see that the directional flows preserve the Lebesgue measure on
M
, which can be seen as a restriction of the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

When
M=M(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
is the suspension defined above, by considering the horizontal interval
I\subseteq M
starting at
0
and of length
|\lambda|
as a Poincaré section for
\varphi^{v}_{\mathbb{R}}
, one can see that the first return map to
I
is exactly the IET
T=(\pi,\lambda)
.
We remark that in this setting the discontinuities of
T=(\pi,\lambda)
are created by the singularities
\Sigma=\Sigma(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
of
M=(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
: more precisely, the trajectories of
\varphi^{v}_{\mathbb{R}}
which start from a discontinuity of

T
end in a singularity of
M
, and hence are incoming
separatrices
of a
saddle
of
\varphi^{v}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

3.
Bounded type rigid rectangles and towers

We describe in this section the key geometric construction on which many of
the analytic estimates rely upon. We start by considering translation surfaces
obtained from glueing rectangles (through
rectangles presentations
,
which are a generalization of the notion of zippered rectangles, see
§
3.1.1
). We first describe the geometry of an irrational
linear flow with a
bounded-type
rotation number in terms of

bounded-type rigidity
(see Def.
3.1
) presentations made by
two rectangles of comparable areas, with certain identifications
of their sides.We then describe how the geometry of a translation surface in higher genus can be forced to
degenerate
to that of torus with bounded-type rigidity, by degenerating in a controlled way rectangle presentations (see §
3.1.2
). Rectangle presentations will be used to build
Rokhlin towers
presentations for IETs, (defined in §
3.2
).

3.1.
Bounded type rigidity via rectangle presentations

We will work with rectangle presentation of translation surfaces, which are a generalization of the zippered rectangles construction.

3.1.1.
Rectangle presentations

Let
S
be a translation surface. When given by a rectangle presentation,
S
is represented as a union of rectangles with glueings of the sides.

A
rectangle presentation

\mathcal{R}(S)
, or simply a
presentation
of
S
, is a collection

\mathcal{R}(S):=\{R_{1},R_{2},\cdots,R_{d};\,\sim_{\mathcal{R}(S)}\}

where, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
,
R_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a (closed) rectangle with horizontal and vertical sides and
\sim_{\mathcal{R}(S)}
is equivalence relation identifying the boundaries of the rectangle by parallel translations so that

S=R_{1}\sqcup R_{2}\sqcup\dots R_{d}/\sim_{\mathcal{R}(S)}.

For the reader who is familiar with them,
zippered rectangles
(as defined by Veech in
[
Ve82
]
, see also
[
Yo10
]
) are a special type of rectangle presentations. We will work with rectangle presentations build similarly to zippered rectangles: the top sides of
R_{i}
will be identified with points belonging to the union of the bottom sides of the rectangles, while each vertical side of a rectangle is union of two segments (one of which could be reduced to a point), so that each of the segments belonging to a left (respectively right) vertical side is identified by a (restriction of a planar) translation to a (isometric) segment contained in a right (respectively left) vertical side.
An example of a rectangle presentation build in this way is shown in Figure
2
.

Figure 2.
An example of a rectangle presentation. The dotted line are glued to the according to the adjacency as seen on the picture. The gluing of the remaining segments is glued with accordance to the color.

3.1.2.
Bounded-type rigidity

We will be interested in building rectangle presentations of a translation surface
S
which allow us to visualize
\epsilon
-(partial)
rigidity times
for the vertical flow
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}^{v}:=\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}^{v}
on
S
, i.e. times
q
such that, for a set
S_{\epsilon}\subset S
of measure
1-\epsilon
of initial points
x\in S_{\epsilon}
the trajectory of
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
starting at
x
returns after time
q

1/q
-close to
x
, i.e.

d(x,\varphi_{q}(x))<1/q,\quad\text{for\ all}\ x\in S_{\epsilon}

(where
d
is the Euclidean metric induced on
S
, see §
2.3
).

The classical example of a rectangle presentation which gives (partial)
rigidity
times consists of one large rectangle, say
R_{1}
, which covers area
1-\epsilon
and other
d-1
rectangles whose total area is at most
\epsilon
, see Figure
3
. In this case, the height
q_{1}
of the rectangle
R_{1}
is a rigidity time, as we can see setting
S_{\epsilon}:=R_{1}\cap\varphi_{-q_{1}}(R_{1})
(so that if
x\in S_{\epsilon}
,
x\in R_{1}
it travels to the top of
R_{1}
under the vertical flow and
returns
and stays in
R_{1}
up to time
q_{1}
). Notice that in this case the displacement of
I_{1}
(base interval of
R_{1}
) under the IET which identifies the top and bottom of the rectangles is at most
\epsilon
, so that for any
x\in S_{\epsilon}
, the distance
d(x,\varphi_{q_{1}}(x))
is bounded above
\epsilon/{q_{1}}
.

Figure 3.
One dominating cylinder
R_{1}
of height
q_{1}
, yields rigid orbits of length
q_{1}
.

3.1.3.
Bounded-type rigidity times

We want to work with (a sequence of increasing) partial rigidity times
q
such that
d(\varphi_{q}(x),x)\geqslant c/q
for some
0<c<1
, i.e. the
relative displacement

q\,d(x,\varphi_{q}x)
is bounded below.
The name
bounded type rigidity
comes from rotations of
bounded-type
, which display this type of controlled rigidity.
In particular, if
S
is a flat torus (i.e.
S\in\mathcal{H}(0)
is a genus one translation surface with one marked point), such that the Poincaré map of the vertical flow on a horizontal interval
I\subset S
is a rotation
R_{\alpha}:I\to I
of
bounded-type
, i.e. such that there exists
0<c<1
so that

\left|{q\alpha}-p\right|\geqslant\frac{c}{q},\qquad\text{for\ infinitely\ many}\ p\in\mathbb{Z},\ q\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\},

then we have times
q
such that for all points
x
on the torus
qd(x,\varphi_{q}x)\geqslant c
.
We will also need to
specify
the relative displacement range, i.e. guarantee that, given an interval
C=(c_{0},c_{1})
, we have
c_{0}\leqslant q\,d(x,\varphi_{q}x)\leqslant c_{1}
. Let us hence introduce the following definition:

Definition 3.1

(BT rigidity times)
.

Given an interval
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
where

0<c_{0}<c_{1}\leq 1
and
\epsilon>0
, we say that
q
is a
(C,\epsilon)
-BT rigidity time, or simply a
(C,\epsilon)
-rigidity time, if
q
is an
\epsilon
-rigidity time and

\frac{c_{0}}{q}\leqslant d(x,\varphi_{q}x)\leqslant\frac{c_{1}}{q},\quad\text{for\ any}\ x\in S_{\epsilon}.

A sequence
(q_{n})_{n}
is
a sequence of
C
-
BT rigidity times
(or simply BT rigidity times) if for any
\epsilon>0
there exists

n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
q_{n}
is a
(C,\epsilon)
-BT rigidity time.
For example, if
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
is as above the vertical flow on a flat torus with a Poincaré map which is a rotation
R_{\alpha}
with
\alpha
of bounded type,
the sequence of the denominators
(q_{n})_{n}
of the convergents of
\alpha
form a sequence of
C
-BT rigidity times for some
C=[c,1]
with
c>0
.

3.1.4.
BT rigidity using rectangles

Notice that sequences of
\epsilon_{n}
-rigid rectangle presentations with one rectangle
R_{n}^{1}
of height
q_{n}
and area
1-\epsilon_{n}
increasing to
1

cannot
be used to produce the type of BT rigidity in Definition
3.1
, since as remarked above
\Delta_{n}:=q_{n}d(x,\varphi_{q_{n}}x))<\epsilon_{n}\to 0
as
n
grow.
On the other hand, BT-rigity times can be built using
two
towers presentations.

Example
(BT rigidity with
d=2
). If
S
is a flat torus, we can represent it via a rectangle presentation
\mathcal{R}(S):=\{R_{1},R_{2};\,\sim_{\mathcal{R}(S)}\}
with
d=2
rectangles, where the right vertical side of
R_{2}
is glued to the top part of the left vertical side of
R_{1}
(see Figure
4
).
If the base of
R_{1}
is larger than
c
, then the height
q_{1}
of
R_{1}

is a
c
-BT rigidity time: to see this, given
x\in S
, consider separately the two
cases when
x\in R_{1}
or
x\in R_{2}
, as illustrated in Figure
4
). Notice that when
x\in R_{2}
, the glueings of the vertical sides are essential to get rigidity. Notice also that the relative displacement
\Delta:=q\,d(x,\varphi_{q}(x))
is exactly the
q\lambda_{1}
, where
\lambda_{1}
is the width of the base of the first rectangle
R_{1}
, so the two rectangles should have comparable width to have a BT rigidity time.

Figure 4.
A
d=2
rectangle presentation of a torus. Colors indicate vertical gluings. The height
q
of the larger rectangle is a rigidity time. Picture on the right illustrates rigidity for
x
and
y
on the circle, exhibiting two different rigidity phenomena.

3.1.5.
BT rigid presentations in higher genus

In higher genus/for higher number
d>2
of exchanged intervals, we will work with BT rigidity times which are given by a rectangle presentation with three
large
rectangles (two with the same height) and additional ones which have
small
area. One can obtain this type of BT rigid rectangle presentations with
d>2
rectangles by
cutting
in two one of the two rectangles in
d=2
presentation glued as in the above example, say
R_{1}
, and inserting additional rectangles in the middle (see Figure
5
).
If the area of these additional rectangles is less than
0<\epsilon<1
, one can get in this way
\epsilon
-rigidity times which are also BT rigid. This is encoded in the following definition. Some comments on the meaning of the assumptions are given just below the definition.

Figure 5.
A BT rigid rectangle presentation with
d=5
, illustrating the properties of Definition
3.2
. The green and purple sides are identified. The
bad zone
where rigidity fails is indicated in grey. On the left-hand side picture, we illustrate three rigid orbits with the short horizontal segments connecting the beginning and the end of the orbit. On the right-hand side picture, we illustrate absence of rigidity in the "bad zone", by drawing two dotted horizontal segments starting from the endpoint of the orbit. One can see that unlike in the first picture, these segments do not realign with the initial point of the orbit.

Definition 3.2

(BT rigid rectangles presentations for
d\geqslant 3
.)
.

A rectangle presentation
\mathcal{R}(S)
with
d\geqslant 3
rectangles
R_{1},\cdots,R_{d}
with heights
q_{1},\cdots,q_{d}
is a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid
presentation, where
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\epsilon>0
and
\rho>0
, if

(i)

the rectangles
R_{3},\cdots,R_{d-1}
have total area

\mathrm{Area}(R_{3}\cup\cdots\cup R_{d-1})<\epsilon
;

(ii)

the two rectangles
R_{2}
and
R_{d}
have the same height
q:=q_{2}=q_{d}
;

(iii)

the rescaled displacement
\Delta:=q\lambda_{1}
where
\lambda_{1}
is the width of the rectangle
R_{1}
belongs to
C
, i.e.
c_{0}\leqslant\Delta\leqslant c_{1}
.

(iv)

the left vertical side of
R_{1}
is glued to the top part of the right vertical side of
R_{d}
(in particular
q_{d}>q_{1}
).

(v)

the points of discontinuities of the glueings of the vertical sides
between the rectangles
R_{2},\cdots,R_{d-1}
have (vertical) distance less than

\rho\,q
.

(vi)

for every
z\in R_{3},\cdots,R_{d-1}
, we have

\max\left\{\operatorname{dist}(z,R_{2}),\operatorname{dist}(z,R_{d})\right\}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{q},

where
dist(p,q)
is the Euclidean distance between two points
p,q
on the translation surface
S
and

dist(z,R)
denotes the distance of the point
x
from the rectangle
R
, i.e.
\min\{dist(z,p)
,
p\in R\}
.

In this rectangle presentation, in view of
(ii)
, the rectangles
R_{2}
and
R_{d}
(of equal heights) should be thought as obtained from the same rectangle, by cutting it vertically
8
8

8

The reason why we do not simply define a BT rigid rectangle presentation with two large rectangles and the other small ones is technical: the degenerations that we build for a typical translation surface to this type of BT rigid presentation lead us to this type of picture (obtained by cutting and pasting a related zippered rectangles picture obtained by Rauzy-Veech induction, see Section
5
for details).
to add the additional
small
rectangles
R_{3},\cdots,R_{d-1}
. Thus, by
(i)
, the rectangles
R_{1},R_{2}
and
R_{d}
occupy a
1-\epsilon
proportion of space; we will call them
large
; the other rectangles will be called
small
.

Given a BT rigid presentation, one can show that
q:=q_{2}=q_{d}
, the common height of
R_{2}
and
R_{d}
, is a BT rigid time (see
§
5.2
, in particular the proof of Corollary
3.4
). Notice that, as in the
d=2
example, the
vertical glueings
between the vertical sides of the three
large
rectangles
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
and
R_{d}
given by
(iv)
play a crucial role, since it allows for rigidity. Condition
(v)
will be referred to as
cramping of discontinuities
and guarantees that all discontinuities of small rectangles have vertical distance from each other which takes a proportion less than
\rho
of the height
q
of
R_{2}
and
R_{d}
. This is needed because rigidity may not hold in a rectangular region inside
R_{d}
(i.e. the region shaded in Figure
5
, see §
5.2.2
for further details) and condition
(v)
guarantees that this region has area of order
\rho
(so that the rigidity set which is obtained by removing both the union of small rectangles
R_{3}\cup\cdots\cup R_{d-1}
, their images by
\phi_{-q}
, which has area less then
\epsilon
, and this rectangular region of area proportional to
\rho
). Finally, condition
(vi)
guarantees that the distance of a point to a singularity, is very well controlled by the distance of this point to the side of a zippered rectangle.

3.1.6.
Existence of BT rigid degenerations

We now show that almost every translation surface in
\mathcal{H}(1^{2g-2})
(namely with genus
g\geqslant 1
and
2g-2
conical singularities of angle
4\pi
, see §
2.3
) admits a sequence of BT rigidity times. The same result could be proved for other strata, we only restrict to
\mathcal{H}(1^{2g-2})
since these are the translation surfaces underlying
9
9

9

Locally Hamiltonian flows in
\mathcal{U}_{min}
, namely locally Hamiltonian flow with only
2g-2
simple saddles (see §
2.1.1
) are indeed time-reparametrizations of linear flows on translation surfaces in
\mathcal{H}(1^{2g-2})
.
them are of this type.

Proposition 3.3

(Existence of bounded-type rigid presentations)
.

For
any interval
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
with
0<c_{0}<c_{1}<1
and any choice of
0<\rho<1
and
0<\epsilon<1
, for almost every translation surface
S\in\mathcal{H}(1^{2g-2})
,
there exist a sequence of

(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid rectangle presentations (as in
Definition
3.2
) with
d=4g-2
rectangles, with heights growing to infinity.

As a corollary, we also have the existence of BT rigidity times:

Corollary 3.4
.

For almost every
{S\in\mathcal{H}(1^{2g-2})}
, for any non-empty interval
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]\subset{\mathcal{R}_{+}}
and any
0<\epsilon<1
, there exists a sequence
(q_{n})_{n}
with
q_{n}\to\infty
of
(C,\epsilon)-
BT rigidity times.

The proof of both the Proposition and its Corollary will be presented in §
5.2
, after having recalled in §
5.1
basic notation from Rauzy-Veech induction. Indeed, we will prove the Proposition by defining
subsets of the parameter space (see Lemma
5.1
) of zippered rectangles which yield BT-rigid rectangle presentations (in the sense of Definition
3.2
) and exploiting ergodicity of Rauzy-Veech induction to visit them.

Remark 3.5
.

Geometrically, the surfaces given by rectangle presentations as in Definition
3.2
can be thought of as obtained by glueing with a
slit
construction
10
10

10

To glue, build a translation surface which is the connected sum of two translation surfaces
S_{1},S_{2}
, one can consider a
slit

\gamma
on both
S_{1},S_{2}
, i.e. consider parallel, isometric (flat) segments
\gamma_{i}\subset S_{i}
for
i=1,2
(which one can consider as
cuts
) and identifying the two sides of each with the opposite side of the other.
a higher genus flat surface
S_{2}
with
Area(S_{2})<\epsilon
to a torus (given by a BT rigid rectangle presentations with
d=2
rectangles). The existence for
S
of a sequence of BT rigidity times given by these rectangle presentations implies that the
Teichmueller geodesic

(g_{t}S)_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
through
S
admits a sequence
(t_{n})_{n}
of times such that
(g_{t_{n}}S)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

degenerates
to a genus one surface (with a
C
-BT rigid presentation).

3.2.
Rohlin towers with BT rigidity

From the existence of rectangle presentations with a certain geometry, one can deduce the existence of
Rohlin towers
(see §
3.2.1
below) with similar properties (for the IET which describes the top/bottom glueings of the initial rectangle presentation).

3.2.1.
Rohlin towers for IETs

Let us first recall the definition of Rohlin tower (by intervals).

Definition 3.6

(Rohlin tower by intervals)
.

Let
T:I\to I
be an IET with
|I|\leqslant 1
. Given an interval
J_{n}:=(a_{n},b_{n})\subset I
and an integer
h_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
we say that the union
\bigcup_{i=0}^{h_{n}-1}T^{i}J_{n}
is a (Rohlin)
tower by intervals
of
base

J_{n}
of
height

h_{n}
if and only if the images
T^{i}J_{n},0\leqslant i<h_{n}
are pairwise disjoint intervals.

Notice that since
T
preserves the Lebesgue measure
\lambda
, all floors of
\mathcal{T}
have the same (Lebesgue) measure
|b-a|
and the measure of
\mathcal{T}
is then
\lambda(\mathcal{T})=h_{n}|b_{n}-a_{n}|
.

Definition 3.7

(Towers representation)
.

A
tower representation
(also called a
skyscraper
representation)
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}(T)
of an IET
T:I\to I
is a (finite) collection

\mathcal{T}:=\{\mathcal{T}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{T}_{k}\}

of Rohlin towers
\mathcal{T}_{i}
for
T
,
0\leqslant i\leqslant k
, that
partition

I
, i.e. such that
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{i}=I
.

Given an IET
T:I\to I
and a subinterval
J\subset I
, it is well known that the induced (or first return map) of
T
to
J
is also an IET
T_{J}
. A tower representation of
T
can then be obtained taking the Rohlin towers
\mathcal{T}_{i}
each of which has as a base one of the continuity intervals
J_{i}\subset J
of the induced map
T_{J}
, and as height the (first) return time of
J_{i}
to
J
under
T
. The action of
T
can then be seen as
moving upwards
by one every floor of a tower but the last one, which is identified to the base through
T_{J}
.

3.2.2.
BT rigid Rohlin towers presentations

Towers presentations can be thought of (and formally related to, see §
3.2.3
)
discretizations
of rectangle presentations. In this spirit, one can define BT rigid tower presentations in analogy to the definition of BT rigid rectangle presentations.

Figure 6.
The BT rigid tower presentation in genus 1 case is simply the two towers obtained by the classical Gauss map, when the partial quotient is bounded. Even though the measure of large tower is bounded away from 1, the heights of such towers still form a rigidity sequence for the rotation, as illustrated on the picture.

Definition 3.8

(BT rigid towers presentations.)
.

A tower presentation
\mathcal{T}=\{\mathcal{T}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{d}\}
with
d\geqslant 3
towers with heights
q_{1},\cdots,q_{d}
is a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid presentation
for some
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]\subset[0,1]
,
0<\epsilon,\rho<1
,
if

(i)

the towers
\mathcal{T}_{3},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{d-1}
have total measure
\lambda(\mathcal{T}_{3}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{T}_{d-1})<\epsilon
;

(ii)

the two towers
\mathcal{T}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}_{d}
have the same height
q:=q_{2}=q_{d}
;

(iii)

the relative displacement
\Delta:=q\lambda_{1}
, where
\lambda_{1}

is the width of the tower
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, belongs to
C
, i.e.
c_{0}<\Delta<c_{1}
;

(iv)

the tower
\mathcal{T}_{d}
has height
q_{d}>q_{1}
and the left endpoints of floors of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
are glued to the right endpoints of the top
q_{1}
floors of
\mathcal{T}_{d}
, while top
q_{2}-q_{1}
left endpoints of floors of
\mathcal{T}_{2}
are glued to the right endpoints of the bottom
q_{2}-q_{1}=q_{d}-q_{1}
floors of
\mathcal{T}_{d}
;

(v)

for
i=2,\dots,d-1
, the right endpoints of the bottom
k_{i}

floors of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
are glued to the left endpoints of the corresponding floors of
\mathcal{T}_{i+1}
, while the right endpoints of the top
q_{i}-k_{i}
floors are glued to
to the left endpoints of the corresponding floors of
\mathcal{T}_{\pi^{-1}(\pi(i)+1)}
. Moreover,

\max_{3\leqslant i,j<d}|k_{i}-k_{j}|\leqslant{\rho\,q.}

(vi)

for every
x\in\mathcal{T}_{2}\cup\mathcal{T}_{d}
and every
\beta\in Disc(T)
if
x_{\beta}^{+}(x_{\beta}^{-})
is the closest visit to
\beta
from the right (left) in time
q
, and
(a^{+},b^{+})
(or
(a^{-},b^{-})
) is the level of
\mathcal{T}_{2}
or
\mathcal{T}_{d}
which contains
x_{\beta}^{+}(x_{\beta}^{-})
, then

\max\{\big||x_{\beta}^{+}-\beta|-|x_{\beta}^{+}-a^{+}|\big|,\big||x_{\beta}^{-}-\beta|-|x_{\beta}^{-}-b^{-}|\big|\}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{q}.

Notice that the condition
(v)
is a discretized version of condition
(v)
for BT rigid rectangle presentations (see Definition
3.2
).

3.2.3.
Rohlin towers from rectangle presentations

Let us finish this section by hinting at the relation between rectangle representations of a translation surface
S
and Rokhlin towers presentations of a IET
T
. Given an IET
T
, let
S
be a translation surface such that
T
appears as a Poincaré section of the vertical flow
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
on an horizontal interval
I\subset S
(we say in this case that
S
is a
suspension
of
T
). Then, given a sequence of rectangle presentations
(\mathcal{R}_{n})_{n}
for
S
with increasing heights,
one can produce a sequence of towers representations for
T
, which asymptotically have the same
geometry
as the rectangle presentations (meaning the same ratios of width, heights, and the same gluing patterns between rectangles and towers), see Figure
7
.

Figure 7.
Building Rohlin towers using rectangle presentations. The section which winds up around the rectangle presentation
\mathcal{R}(S)
of the surface
S
, makes it possible to
deduce properties of the Rokhlin towers by seeing them inside
rectangle presentations. Recall that the discontinuities of IET correspond to the first intersections of incoming separatrices, see §
2.3.2
.

To see this, notice that the interval
I\subset S
can be visualized inside each
\mathcal{R}_{n}
as a union of horizontal segments contained in the rectangles of
\mathcal{R}_{n}
(see Figure
7
);
the horizontal segments are identified with each other according to the rectangle identifications of
{\mathcal{R}_{n}}
(since they are part of the connected horizontal interval
I\subset S
.
From this correspondence, one can see that the dynamics of
T
(which is the first return map of the vertical flow on
S
to
I
is exactly that one of a Rokhlin towers skyscraper (each tower being the union of floors contained in the same rectangle). We will use this correspondence to build Rokhlin towers for
T
in Section
5
(see in particular §
3.1.6
).

4.
Birkhoff sums, matchings and exponential tails.

In this section we introduce the tools that we need to verify the assumptions
of the disjointness criterion for special flow (given by
Proposition
2.11
). We need to study the distribution of
Birkhoff sums
S_{q}f
of functions with logarithmic singularities and to
show that they have exponential tails along BT rigidity times.
In particular, we need estimates on the derivatives

S_{q}f^{\prime}
and
S_{q}f^{\prime\prime}
(which are rather classical), as well as the notion (which is new to this paper), of
matchings of Birkhoff sums
. We motivate and define the notion of
matchings
(as well as
balanced
matchings, see Definition
4.6
) in §
4.1
.
In §
4.2
we recall some results providing estimates on Birkhoff sums of the roof and its derivatives; Then, in §
4.3
, we exploit the existence of matchings with good properties and trimmed Birkhoff sums linear bounds, to prove the exponential tails estimates.

4.1.
Matching orbits and Birkhoff sums

In this section, we introduce

matchings
. We first heuristically explain in §
4.1.1
the motivation behind this notion.
We then formally define the notion of
matchings of orbits
and then
matchings of Birkhoff sums
in §
4.1.2
and §
4.1.3
respectively.
We then define
balanced
matchings (see §
4.1.5
), which are matching sets with good geometry control of the distance from singularities.

4.1.1.
Motivation for matchings.

Let
x,y
be two points in
I
, fix
r\in\mathbb{N}
and consider the Birkhoff sums
S_{r}(x)
and
S_{r}(y)
.
If we want to estimate the difference
|S_{r}f(x)-S_{r}f(y)|
(or the analogous differences between Birkhoff sums of the derivatives
f^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime\prime}
of the roof function) using the mean-value theorem, it is necessary that
x,y
both belong to the same continuity interval
(a,b)
for
S_{r}f
, or, equivalently, that all the intervals
(a,b),T(a,b),\dots,T^{r-1}(a,b)
do not contain singularities of
T
.
This is the case, for example, if
(a,b)
is the base of a Rohlin tower for
T
with height
q
.
More generally, we can estimate
|S_{r}f(x)-S_{r}f(y)|
by decomposing each Birkhoff sum
S_{r}f(x)
and
S_{r}f(y)
and, correspondingly (using the notation (
2.2
) introduced in §
2.1.3
to denote orbit segments), decomposing

\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
and

\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,r)
into finitely many orbit segments, so that these orbit segments can be
matched
, i.e. for each orbit segment of the form
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{i},r_{i})\subset\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
there is an orbit segment of the same length
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{i},r_{i})\subset\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,r)
such that
|S_{r_{i}}f(x_{i})-S_{r_{i}}f(y_{i})|
can be estimated, as hinted above, using the mean-value theorem. This is the case when the interval
J:=I(x_{i},y_{i})
is contained in an interval
(a_{i},b_{i})
such that its iterates
T^{j}(a_{i},b_{i})
for
0\leqslant m<r_{i}
form a Rohlin tower.

4.1.2.
Matching of orbits

Given
x_{0},y_{0}\in I_{0}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
consider the two orbit segments
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{0},r)
and
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{0},r)
(see §
2.1.3
). A
matching
of these two orbit segments is formally defined as follows.

Definition 4.1
.

Given a positive integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that two orbit segments
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
and
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,r)

admit a
k
-matching
if there exists

k
-partition of
r
, namely positive integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
with
r_{1}+r_{2}+\dots r_{k}=r
, such that we can decompose each orbit segments in
k
disjoint orbit segments, namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{1},r_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{2},r_{2})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{k},r_{k}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,r)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{1},r_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{2},r_{2})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{k},r_{k}),

so that, for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
there exists an interval
(a_{i},b_{i})
which contains both points
x_{i},y_{i}
and
is the base of a Rohlin tower by intervals of height
r_{i}
.
We then say that
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{i},r_{i})
and
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{i},r_{i})
admit a matching or simply can be matched if they admit a
k
-matching for some positive integer
k
.

Notice that the
order
of the
\kappa
orbit segments inside the bigger orbit segment of length
r
may not be the natural one. For example, we can by convention set

x_{1}:=x,\quad x_{2}:=T_{r_{1}}(x_{1}),\quad\dots\quad x_{k}:=T^{r_{k}}(x_{\kappa-1})=T^{r_{1}+\dots+r_{\kappa}}(x_{0})

but then the corresponding points
