y_{i}
do not have the same order inside
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,r)
. We can describe their order by a permutation
\nu:\{1,\dots,\kappa\}\to\{1,\dots,\kappa\}
, chosen so that

y_{\nu(1)}:=y,\quad y_{\nu(2)}=T^{r_{\nu(1)}}y_{\nu(1)},\quad\dots\quad,y_{\nu(\kappa)}:=T^{r_{\nu(\kappa-1)}}(y_{\nu(\kappa-1})).

4.1.3.
Matching of Birkhoff sums

The following definition of matching of Birkhoff sums is based on matching of the underlying orbit segments (given in Definition
4.2
above).

Definition 4.2
.

Given
x,y\in I_{0}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that the Birkhoff sums
S_{r}f(x)
and
S_{r}f(y)
can be
matched
if the orbits
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
and
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,r)
can be matched.

Remark 4.3
.

If
S_{r}f(x)
and
S_{r}f(y)
can be matched, we can decompose each as

S_{r}f(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}S_{r_{i}}f(x_{i}),\qquad S_{r}f(y)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}S_{r_{i}}f(y_{i}),

so that, for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
,
S_{r_{i}}f(x)
, as well as its first and second derivatives
S_{r_{i}}f^{\prime}(x)
and
S_{r_{i}}f^{\prime\prime}(x)
, are continuous for
x
in an interval
(a_{i},b_{i})
containing both
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
, so that the difference
|S_{r_{i}}f(x_{i})-S_{r_{i}}f(y_{i})|
can be studied using mean value on
(a_{i},b_{i})
.

4.1.4.
Matching sets

If we now fix a reference Birkhoff sum
S_{q}f(y)
and consider the set of points
x\in I_{0}
such that
S_{r}f(x)
and
S_{r}f(y)
can be matched, this gives a set which we call
(q,y)
-matching set:

Definition 4.4

(matching sets)
.

Given
y\in I_{0}
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that a subset
M\subset I_{0}
is a
(y,q)
-matching set
if for every
x\in M
the Birhoff sum
S_{q}f(x)

can be matched with the Birkhoff sum
S_{q}f(y)
. We say furthermore that
M
is a
(y,q)
-
\kappa
matching set
if,
for every
x\in M
,
S_{q}f(x)
admits a
k
-matching with

S_{q}f(y)
for a positive integer
k\leqslant\kappa
, i.e. it can be matched with
S_{q}f(y)
by decomposing it in at most
\kappa
orbit segments.

Let us give two examples of matching sets. Assume first that
T
has a Rohlin towers presentation with
d=2
towers, which correspond (in the sense of §
3.2.3
) to the BT rigid presentation of
d=2
rectangles described in the Example in §
3.1.4
. Then, for any
y
in the base of the towers and
q=q_{1}+q_{2}
equal to the sum of the two tower heights, the whole interval
I
is a
(y,q)
-matching set, since every orbit
\mathcal{O}(x,q)
of length
q
can be matched with
\mathcal{O}(y,q)
, as shown in Figure
8
.

Figure 8.
An example of matching on genus 1 surface. The orbit of
x
of length
q
, where
q
is the height of the bigger tower, is matched to the orbit of
y
of the same length. The colors indicate different ways with which the orbits are being matched. Notice that the connecting segments are not of the same length.

On the other hand, if we consider Rohlin towers which correspond to a BT rigid presentation as in Definition
3.8
in §
3.2.2
, there is a
bad zone
for rigidity (near the
cramping
of discontinuities) which should be removed to obtain the matching set
M
(see the comments in §
3.1.4
after Definition
3.2
). In both these examples,
\kappa=3
, since all matchings can be achieved decomposing each orbit in
3
segments. These type of matchings will be heavily exploited later, to bound Birkhoff sums using the mean value theorem (see §
4.1.1
for an introduction to the role of matchings in the proof).

Finally, to prove disjointness of an
L
-rescalings
(T_{Lt}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
with
the
K\leqslant L
rescaling
(T_{Kt}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of the special flow

(T_{t}^{f})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
for positive integers
K<L
, we will need to match not only
orbits of length
q_{n}
with a reference orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})
, but
also orbits of length
Lq_{n}
with
L
(and hence also
K
) copies of the
orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})
(splitting the orbit of length
Lq_{n}
into
L

(resp.
K
) segments of length
q_{n}
each of which can be matched with

\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})
). For this, we define what we call
L
-fold
matchings
:

Definition 4.5

(
L
-fold matching sets)
.

Given a positive integer
L\in\mathbb{N}
,
y\in I_{0}
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that a subset
M\subset I_{0}
is an
L
-fold

(y,q)
-matching set
if for every
x\in M
the
L
Birhoff sum

S_{q}f(x),\ S_{q}f(T^{q}x),\ S_{q}f(T^{2q}x),\cdots,S_{q}f(T^{(L-1)q}x)

can each be matched with the reference Birkhoff sum
S_{q}f(y)
. We say furthermore that
M
is a
L
-fold
(y,q)
-
\kappa
matching set
if each
S_{q}f(T^{iq}x)
for
0\leqslant i<L
admits a
k
-matching with

S_{q}f(y)
for some
1\leqslant k\leqslant\kappa
.

4.1.5.
Balanced matchings

To estimate the values of the Birkhoff sum
S_{q}f
and its derivatives at points in a
(y,q)
-matching set
M
, we need additional assumptions on the matching set. In particular, since (as we recall in the next §
4.2
) controlling closests visits is essential to control Birkhoff sums of derivatives (see e.g. Proposition
5.3
in §
5.3
), we need
to guarantee that the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z,q)
of
z\in M
does not get too close to singularities. We will also need that the intervals used for matchings are not too small.

These additional requirements are axiomatised in the following Definition
4.6
of what we call
balanced
matching sets.
We recall that
m(z,\beta,q)
is the distance of
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z,r)
from the set of discontinuities
Disc(T)
(see §
2.1.3
).

Definition 4.6

(Balanced matchings)
.

For
0<c<1
, we say that a
(y,q)
-matching set
M
is
c
-
balanced
if:

(B1)

the orbits
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,q)
and
\mathcal{O}_{T}(T^{-q}y,q)

c/q
-close, nor
1/cq
-far from any discontinuity in
Disc(T)
, namely

\frac{c}{q}\leqslant m(y,\beta,q)\leqslant\frac{1}{cq},\quad\frac{c}{q}\leqslant m(T^{-q}y,\beta,q)\leqslant\frac{1}{cq}\qquad\text{ for\ any}\ \beta\in Disc(T).

(B2)

for any
\beta\in Disc(T)
and any
x\in M
, there exists at most one point
z\in\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q)
such that
d(z,\beta)\leqslant\frac{1}{cq}
;

(B3)

for all
x\in M
the
k
intervals
(a_{i},b_{i})
given by Definition
4.1
are such that
b_{i}-a_{i}\leqslant\frac{1}{cq}
for any
i=1,\dots,k
;

(B4)

for every
x\in M
we have
\min_{j,j^{\prime}\in\{0,\ldots,q-1\};\,j\neq j^{\prime}}|T^{j}x-T^{j^{\prime}}x|\geq\frac{c}{q}
.

Remark 4.7
.

Note that since
T
acts as a translation on the matching intervals, for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
any point
z\in(a_{i},b_{i})
satisfies (B2) and also (B4) with
j,j^{\prime}\in\{0,\ldots,r_{i}-1\}
. Hence some of the later estimates are possible also for points that not necessarily belong to
M
.

Definition
4.6
can be easily extended to consider orbits of length multiplicity of
q
.

Definition 4.8
.

For
0<c<1
, we say that an
L
-fold
(y,q)
-matching set
M
is

c
-balanced, if the conditions (B1) to (B4) in Definition

4.6
are satisfied not only by each
x\in M
, but also by
all points of the form
T^{iq}(x)
, for
x\in M
and
0\leq i<L
.

4.2.
Birkhoff sums estimates

In this section we collect several estimates on Birkhoff sums of the roof and its derivatives that will be used in the next section in combination with matchings.

4.2.1.
Closest visits

The contributions of the
closest visits
of an orbit to the singularities play a key role in the study of Birkhoff sums of the derivatives of functions with logarithmic singularities
(see e.g. Proposition
5.3
for the first derivative and Lemma
4.14
for the second).
We therefore introduce notation for closest visits to each singularity, from each side (i.e. from the right and from the left).

Given an IET
T:I\to I
, a point
x\in I
and a natural number
r
, let us consider the orbit segment of length
r
,
and for any discontinuity
\beta\in Disc(T)
, let us denote by

x_{\beta}^{+}=x_{\beta}^{+}(x,r)=T^{\ell^{+}_{\beta}}(x)
, where
0\leq\ell^{+}_{\beta}<r
(resp.
x_{\beta}^{-}=x_{\beta}^{-}(x,r)=T^{\ell^{-}_{\beta}}(x)
, where
0\leq\ell^{-}_{\beta}<r
) the closest visits of the orbit segment
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
to
\beta

from the right
(resp. from the left). Correspondingly, we denote by

m_{\beta}^{+}=m_{\beta}^{+}(x,r)
(respectively
m_{\beta}^{-}=m_{\beta}^{-}(x,r)
) the minimum distance of the orbit points to
\beta

from the right
(resp.
from the left
), namely, denoting by
(x)^{pos}
the ’positive’ part
of
x
(defined by
(x)^{pos}:=x
if
x>0
,
(x)^{pos}:=0
otherwise):

\displaystyle m_{\beta}^{+}

\displaystyle=m_{\beta}^{+}(x,r):=\min\{(T^{j}x-{\beta})^{pos},\ 0\leqslant j<r\}=(T^{l_{\beta}^{+}}x-{\beta})^{pos}=x^{+}_{\beta}-{\beta},

\displaystyle m_{\beta}^{-}

\displaystyle=m_{\beta}^{-}(x,r):=\min\{({\beta}-T^{j}x)^{pos},\ 0\leqslant j<r\}=({\beta}-T^{l_{\beta}^{-}}x)^{pos}=\beta-x^{-}_{\beta},

We also set
m_{i}(x,r):=\min\{m_{i}^{+}(x,r),m_{i}^{-}(x,r)\}
and
give a name to the iterates where this closest distances (from either side) is achieved:

Definition 4.9
.

For any
x\in I
and
r\geq 0
,
\beta\in Disc(T)
,
let
0\leq\ell_{\beta}=\ell_{\beta}(x,r)<r

be defined as the iterate satisfying

m_{\beta}(x,r)=|T^{\ell_{\beta}}(x)-\beta|=\min_{0\leq j<r}|T^{j}(x)-\beta|.

Notice then the distance
m(x,r)
of
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
from
Disc(T)
(defined in (
2.3
) in §
2.1.3
) is then simply

m(x,r):=\min\{m_{\beta}(x,r),\ \beta\in Disc(T)\},\quad\text{where}\ m_{\beta}(x,r)=\min\{m^{+}_{\beta}(x,r),m_{\beta}^{-}(x,r)\}.

4.2.2.
Trimmed Birkhoff sums

The derivative
f^{\prime}
of
f
with logarithmic singuarities
is not in
L^{1}
(since singularities of type
1/x
are
not
integrable). To bound the
derivative Birkhoff sums
S_{n}f^{\prime}
it is necessary to consider separately the
contributions of the largest terms, which are controlled by the
closest
visits
of an orbit to the singularities. We therefore introduce here the
following notion of
trimmed
Birkhoff sum.
11
11

11

We remark that
other possible definitions of trimming appear in the literature. In particular
in
[
FKU
]
the notion of
trimming
and the notation
\tilde{S}_{r}(f)(x)
refers to a sum to which
both
closest visits from the

right
and from the
left
are removed. We only remove the closest
of the two since we will use trimming for orbits which only get close to the
singularity
\beta
from the right,
or
from the left.

Let
f
be a function continuous and differentiable on each continuity
intervals of
T
, possibly singular at
Disc(T)
. For any orbit segment

\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
, we recall that
\ell_{\beta}
and
m_{\beta}(x,r)
denote
respectivecly the index and distance of the closest visit to singularities (see
Definition
4.9

in the above subsection).

Definition 4.10

(Trimmed Birkhoff sums)
.

For any
x\in I
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
the
trimmed
r^{th}
Birkhoff sum
of
f
is

(4.1)

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{r}(f)(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant j<r-1\vskip 0.85358pt\\
j\notin\{\ell_{\beta}\mid\,\beta\in Disc(T)\}\end{subarray}}f(T^{j}(x))=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}f(T^{j}(x))-\sum_{\beta\in Disc(T)}f(T^{\ell_{\beta}}(x)).

Thus, the trimmed
12
12

12

The use of the terminology
trimmed
Birkhoff sums is used in the literature to refer to the operation of
(
trimming
a Birkhoff sums
by removing the largest elements (one, finitely many, or an increasing number, according to context) from the sum, in order to obtain for example limit theorems, see e.g.
[
AS
]
).
Birkhoff sum for us is defined removing from Birkhoff sums all contributions given by all the closest visits to singularities.

4.2.3.
Linear bounds on trimmed derivatives

The estimates that we need to prove tightness with exponential tails are what we call
linear
control of the trimmed Birkhoff sums which correspond to subsegments of an orbit segment, as given by the following definition.

Definition 4.11

(Orbits with linear bounds on trimmed derivatives)
.

We say that an orbit segment
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q)
, where
x\in I
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
, has
B
-
linear bounds on trimmed derivatives
of
f
for some
B>0
(or simply
has linear bounds
) if

\left|\widetilde{S}_{r}f^{\prime}(x)\right|\leqslant B\,\!q\ \ \text{for \ any}\ 0\leqslant r\leqslant q.

A result which yields linear bounds on trimmed derivatives was proved by the second author in
[
Ul11
]
to prove absence of mixing (there, linear bounds were shown for orbit segments
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q)
with
x
in the base and
q
equal to the height of special Rohlin towers built using Rauzy-Veech induction, see Proposition
5.3
for the precise result
13
13

13

The definition of trimming in
[
Ul11
]
is different, in that
both
the closest visit from the right
and
from the left are removed, but in the cases which we consider, removing the closest visit only leads to a sum of comparable order. We refer the reader to Section
4.2.2
for details.
).

4.2.4.
Relative trimming and its comparability in balanced matchings

Given an orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,r)

matched
to a given orbit segment
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
, the closest visits to a given singularity may not appear at matched times. In some proofs, it will be useful for us to trim
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,r)
not removing the contribution of closest visits, but
removing the contributions of points which are
matched
with the closest visits of
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
.
14
14

14

For example, if the reference orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q)
of a
(x,q)
-matching
matching set
M
, can be chosen to stay
far
from
Disc(T)
(as in Definition
4.6
of a balanced matching), it is not necessary (and it complicates the estimates) to remove all the closest visits, since they may be actually very far from the discontinuities.
We call this
relative trimming
, formally defined as follows.

Definition 4.12
.

Assume that
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,q)
is matched to
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q)
and consider
any orbit subsegment
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z,r)\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,q)
, where the inclusion of orbits segments means that
z=T^{j}y
for some
0\leqslant j<q
and
0\leqslant r\leqslant q-j
. We define the
relatively trimmed Birkhoff sum

S_{r}^{x}(f)(z)
trimmed relatively to the matched orbit of
x
as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{r}^{x}(f)(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{x}(z,r)}f(T^{j}(z)),\quad\textrm{where}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{x}(z,r)

\displaystyle:=\{0\leqslant j<r|\ T^{j}(z)\ \text{ is matched to }T^{\ell_{\beta}}(x)\text{ for some }\beta\in Disc(T)\}.

Notice that the contributions removed, of the form
T^{j}(z)
for
j\in\mathcal{M}^{x}(z,r)
are, by definition of
\mathcal{M}^{x}(z,r)
, those of the iterates which are matched to the closest visits in the orbit segment
\mathcal{O}(x,q)
of
x
.

Within a balanced matching set (see Definition
4.6
), relative trimming and trimming of Birkhoff sums are comparable, as the following lemma shows.

Lemma 4.13

(Comparability of trimmings in a balanced matching set)
.

Given an IET
T
and a function
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
and let
M
be
a balanced
\mathcal{O}(y,q)
-matching set.
Then there exists
E\in\mathbb{R}
such that,
for every
x\in M
,

(4.2)

|\tilde{S}_{q}^{x}(f)(y)-\tilde{S}_{q}(f)(y)|\leq E,

Proof.

First note that for any
g
restricted to any
I_{i}
, with

i=1,\ldots,d
, is
extendable to a
C^{2}
function on
\overline{I_{i}}
, then for any
x\in M
we
have

|\tilde{S}_{q}^{x}(g)(y)-\tilde{S}_{q}(g)(y)|\leq\#Disc(T)\cdot\|g\|_{\infty}.

Thus, we can assume that
f\in\mathcal{P}SymLog(T)
.

For every
\beta\in Disc(T)
, let
\ell_{\beta}^{x}:=\ell_{\beta}^{x}(y,q)
be such that
T^{\ell^{x}_{\beta}}(y)
is matched to
T^{\ell_{\beta}}(x)
.
Then the set of indexes
\mathcal{M}^{x}(y,q)
(see Definition
4.12
of relative trimming) is exactly

\mathcal{M}^{x}(y,q)=\{\ell_{\beta}^{x}=\ell_{\beta}^{x}(y,q),

\beta\in Disc(T)\}
.
Thus, recalling the Definition
4.12
of relative trimming and
the form of the function
f
, we have

\tilde{S}_{q}^{x}(f)(y)=S_{q}^{x}(f)(y)-\sum_{\beta\in Disc(T)}-C_{\beta}\log(|\beta-T^{\ell_{\beta}^{x}(y,q)}(y)|)

where the constants
C_{\beta}
for
\beta\in Disc(T)
are defined to be

(4.3)

C_{\beta}:=\begin{cases}C_{i}^{+}&\text{if}\ \beta=\beta_{i}\ \text{ and\ the\ closest\ visit\ occurs\ from\ the\ \emph{right},\ or}\\
C_{i}^{-}&\text{if}\ \beta=\beta_{i}\ \text{ and\ the\ closest\ visit\ occurrs\ from\ the\ \emph{left}. }\end{cases}

Hence

\tilde{S}_{q}^{x}(f)(y)-\tilde{S}_{q}(f)(y)=\sum_{\beta\in Disc(T)}-C_{\beta}\log(|\beta-T^{\ell_{\beta}^{x}}(y)|)-\sum_{\beta\in Disc(T)}-C_{\beta}\log(|\beta-T^{\ell_{\beta}}(y)|).

By (B3), and then by (B2), we have that

|T^{\ell_{\beta}^{x}}(y)-T^{\ell_{\beta}}(x)|<\frac{1}{cq}\quad\Rightarrow\quad|T^{\ell_{\beta}^{x}}(y)-\beta|<\frac{c+c^{-1}}{q}.

From this and from (B1) we get that

C_{\beta}\log(|\beta-T^{\ell_{\beta}^{x}}(y)|)-C_{\beta}\log(|\beta-T^{\ell_{\beta}}(y)|)<C_{\beta}(\log(c+c^{-1})-\log(c)),

which finishes the proof.
∎

4.2.5.
Second derivative estimates

To control trimmed Birkhoff sums of the second derivative of
f
in a balanced matching, we can use a simple classical estimate, which goes back to the work of Kocergin (see e.g.
[
Ko72
]
) and is based on the sum of reciprocals of squares. This estimate holds whenever there is sufficient
spacing
between points in the orbit (which given by assumption (B4) in the Definition
4.6
of a balanced matching).

Lemma 4.14
.

Given any
f\in{{\mathcal{P}}SymLog\left(T\right)}
with symmetric
logarithmic singularities, let
M
be a
c
-balanced
(y,q)
-matchings and let

x\in M
.
Then there exists
\hat{C}>0
, which depends only on
c
and
f
(but not
on the matching set as long as it is
c
-balanced), such that for every

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq r<q
and any
z\in M,r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z,r)\subset\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q)
we have the following estimates on the
trimmed Birkhoff sums of the second derivative
f^{\prime\prime}
:

0\leqslant\tilde{S}_{r}(f^{\prime\prime})(z)\leq\hat{C}q^{2}.

Moreover, if
z
satisfies additionally that
m(z,r)\geq\frac{c}{q}
, then we have also a similar estimate for the second derivative
relative
trimmed Birkhoff sums, namely:

0\leqslant\tilde{S}_{r}^{x}(f^{\prime\prime})(z)\leq\hat{C}q^{2}.

Proof.

Notice first that since
f\in{\mathcal{P}}SymLog\left(T\right)
,
f^{\prime\prime}\geqslant 0
and hence both
\tilde{S}_{r}(f^{\prime\prime})
and
\tilde{S}_{r}^{x}(f^{\prime\prime})
are non-negative.
Since
x\in M
, which is by assumption
c
-balanced, and
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z,r)\subset\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q)
,
by assumptions
(B2)
and
(B4)
of a
c
-balanced matching (see Definition
4.6
),
we have

0\leqslant\tilde{S}_{r}(f^{\prime\prime})(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}f^{\prime\prime}(T^{i}z)\leq C_{f}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left(\frac{ic}{q}\right)^{-2}=q^{2}\cdot c^{-2}C_{f}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{1}{i^{2}}\leq\frac{c^{-2}C_{f}\pi^{2}}{6}\cdot q^{2}.

Furthermore, if
z
is as in assumptions of the lemma, then we can also estimate the relative trimmed Birkhoff sums
\tilde{S}_{r}^{x}(f_{p}^{\prime\prime})(z)
by:

|\tilde{S}_{r}(f^{\prime\prime})(z)-\tilde{S}_{r}^{x}(f^{\prime\prime})(z)|\leq 2c^{-2}d\cdot q^{2}

Hence, the desired estimate holds for
\hat{C}:=\frac{c^{-2}C_{f}\pi^{2}}{6}+2c^{-2}d
. ∎

Remark 4.15
.

Notice that in the proof we have not used all the properties given by the
assumption that
M
is a
c
-balanced matching, but only
(B2)
and
(B4)
.
Thus, the estimate also holds for any suborbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x^{\prime},r)
of an
orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q)
that satisfies
(B2)
and
(B4)
in the
Definition
4.6
of a
c
-balanced
(y,q)
-matching.

4.3.
Exponential tails via matchings

In this section we show that combining matchings (from §
4.1
) with linear bounds on trimmed derivatives (see Def.
4.11
in §
4.2.3
) one can prove

exponential upper bounds
for the tail of Birkhoff sums of the roof function.
We first introduce in §
4.3.1
the notion of
good matchings
(adding further requirements to the matching orbit and the matching set of a balanced sequence of matchings which will be used to control Birkhoff sums, see Definition
4.16
), and state a result on their existence (see Proposition
4.18
). In §
4.3.2
we show that in good matchings one has good control on trimmed Birkhoff sums of matched orbits. Finally, in §
4.3
, we state and prove a criterion which gives exponential bounds from the existence of a sequence of good matchings.

4.3.1.
Sequences of good matchings

A sequence of good matchings is a sequence of
balanced
matching sets (see Definitions
4.4
and
4.6
respectively) with additional requirements on the
matching orbit

\mathcal{O}(y,q)
: we want the matching orbit to satisfy linear bounds on trimmed derivatives (in the sense of Definition
4.11
).
These requirements are formalized in the following Definition
4.16

of
good matchings
.

Definition 4.16

(good matchings for exponential tightness)
.

Given
T
and
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
,
0<\gamma<1

we say that a sequence of (
L
-fold) matching sets
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is

\gamma
-
good for exponential tightness
(or for short,

\gamma
-good
, or simply
good
) for
T
and
f
, if there exists
0<c<1
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and and
B_{0}>0
, such
that:

(G0)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}Leb([0,1]\backslash M_{n})\leq\gamma
as
n\to\infty
.

(G1)

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
M_{n}
is a
c
-balanced (
L
-fold)
(y_{n},q_{n})
-
\kappa
-matching set, with
q_{n}\to\infty
as
n
grows;

(G2)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the matching orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})
has
B_{0}
-
linear bounds on trimmed derivatives
.

In addition, we say that we say that a sequence of (
L
-fold) matching sets

(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
(\gamma,B)
-
good for exponential tightness
(or
for short,
(\gamma,B)
-good
) for some
B>0
and
0<\gamma<1
if

(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
\gamma
-
good for exponential tightness
and in
addition:

(G3)

all orbits
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{n})
with
x\in M_{n}
have
B
-
linear bounds on trimmed derivatives, i.e.

(4.4)

|\tilde{S}_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|\leq B\,{q}\quad\text{ for\ any\ }n\in\mathbb{N},\ \text{any}\ x\in M_{n}\text{ and }0\leq r\leq q_{n}.

Here assumption
(G2)
concerns only the reference orbits

\mathcal{O}(y_{n},q_{n})
to which all other orbits in each
M_{n}
are matched,
while the last assumption
(G3)
, requires that all points in
M_{n}
satisfy
linear bounds on trimmed derivatives (in the sense of
Definition
4.11
) with a
uniform
constant
B
independent of

n
. We will show that
(G3)
can be derived from
(G2)
under suitable
assumptions (see Lemma
4.19
in the next
§
4.3.2
).

We will show the following result, giving the
existence
of good
matchings for exponential tails for typical IETs. We will build matching sets using BT rigid towers presentations.

Definition 4.17

(Matching set given by a BT rigidity time)
.

We say that
a (
L
-fold)
(y,q)
-matching
M
is
given by
a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigidity presentation with
d
rectangles if

q
is the common height of the towers
\mathcal{T}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}_{d}
of a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid tower presentation.

For any
f\in SymLog(T)
, let us recall that
C_{f}
is the constant
C_{f}:=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}
(see Definition
2.3
) when
f
is written in the form given by Definition
2.1
.

Proposition 4.18

(Existence of good matchings)
.

For almost every
\lambda\in\Delta_{d}
, if we consider the IET
T=(\pi,\lambda)
with
a standard permutation
\pi
, for every
L\in\mathbb{N}
, for any
0<\rho<1
, and for any
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
, there exists
B>0
such that, for any
0<\epsilon<1

and any non empty interval
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]\subset{[0,1/10]}
, there exists a
sequence of
L
-fold matchings
(M_{n})_{n}
which are
(\gamma,B)
-good for
exponential tails for

\gamma=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}Leb(I\setminus M_{n})\leq(L+1)\rho c_{1}+\epsilon
and
given by
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid presentation.

The order of quantifiers in the statement is delicate and important in the
later proofs. In particular we remark that the rigidity range
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
and
the
\epsilon
parameters that enter the definition of
(C,\epsilon)
-BT
rigidity can be chosen
independently
on
\rho
and in turn
B
(which
depends only on
\rho
and on
f
). More precisely, one can show that the
dependence of
B
on
f
, if we write
f=f^{p}+g
(by
Definition
2.1
) is through the constant
C_{f}
and
\|g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
and is linear in both.
The proof of this Proposition will be given in Section
5
.

4.3.2.
Trimmed BS bounds in good matchings

We conclude this subsection proving that
\gamma
-good matchings are actually
automatically
(\gamma,B)
-good matchings (see Definition
4.16
).

Lemma 4.19

(Trimmed derivatives bounds)
.

For any
0<\gamma,c<1
,
given
any sequence

(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\gamma
-good matchings for
T
and
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
,
so that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
M_{n}
is a
(y_{n},q_{n})
-matching given by

c
-balanced presentation,
there exists
B>0
, independent of
\gamma
, such that
(M_{n})_{n}
is a sequence
of
(\gamma,B)
-good matchings.

The constant
B>0
(which, by Definition
4.16
of

(\gamma,B)
-good matchings, given the trimmed derivative bounds
(
4.4
) for all
x\in M_{n}
,
depends only on the constant
B_{0}>0
in the bound for the trimmed derivatives of the matching orbit
\mathcal{O}(y_{n},q_{n})
(which exists by
(G2)
in the Definition
4.16
of
\gamma
-good matching sets) and the balance constant
c
(from Definition
4.6
), but not on other parameters of the BT-rigid rectangle presentation inside which
M_{n}
is built (in particular not on the parameters
\epsilon>0
and
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
). This will be important in the disjointness proof.

Proof.

In view of the Definition
2.1
of
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
, we can
write
f=f_{p}+g
where
f_{p}\in{\mathcal{P}SymLog\left(T\right)}
. Let us
first discuss
g
.
Notice that since
g
in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on each
I_{i}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
, we trivially have that
\|\tilde{S}_{r}(g^{\prime})(x)\|_{\infty}\leq\|g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\cdot{q}
for any
0\leq r\leq q
. Hence we can assume WLOG that
f=f_{p}
i.e. that
f\in\mathcal{P}SymLog(T)
.

Let us first show that (
4.4
) holds if
\mathcal{O}(x,r)\subset\mathcal{O}(y_{n},q_{n})
is an orbit subsegment of the matching orbit
\mathcal{O}(y_{n},q_{n})
for
M_{n}
, or, explicitely, if we assume that
x=T^{j}y_{n}
for some
0\leqslant j<q_{n}
and
0\leqslant r<q_{n}-j
. In this case, we can write

\displaystyle|\tilde{S}_{r}f^{\prime}(x)|=|\tilde{S}_{r}f^{\prime}(T^{j}y_{n})|

\displaystyle=|\tilde{S}_{r+j}f^{\prime}(y_{n})-\tilde{S}_{j}f^{\prime}(y_{n})|\leqslant

\displaystyle|\tilde{S}_{r+j}f^{\prime}(y_{n})|+|\tilde{S}_{j}f^{\prime}(y_{n})|\leqslant B_{0}(r+j)+B_{0}j\leqslant 2B_{0}q_{n},

where the last estimate follows from the linear bound assumption on
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})
(see Definition
4.11
) and the assumption that
j<q_{n}
and
r+j<q_{n}
(in view of the orbit segments inclusions).

Consider now a generic
x\in M_{n}
. Since
M_{n}
is a
(y_{n},q_{n})

\kappa
-matching set,
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{n})
is matched with
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})
(in the sense of Definition
4.1
). Thus,
since
0\leqslant r<q_{n}
and hence
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)\subset\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{n})
, we can decompose also
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)
into
\ell\leqslant\kappa
disjoint orbit subsegments

\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,r)=\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{1},r_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{2},r_{2})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{k},r_{\ell}),\quad\text{with}\ r_{1}+\ldots+r_{\ell}=r,

and
match
them with
\ell
disjoint orbit subsegments of
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})
, which we will denote by

\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n,1},r_{1})
,
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n,2},r_{2})
,
\cdots,

\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n,\ell},r_{\ell})
.
Then, by definition of matchings, we use mean value to estimate

\left|\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}f^{\prime}(x_{i})-\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}^{x_{i}}f^{\prime}(y_{n,i})\right|\leqslant|\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}^{x_{i}}f^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{i})||b_{i}-a_{i}|,\qquad 1\leqslant i\leqslant k,

for some
\xi_{i}\in(a_{i},b_{i})
, where
(a_{i},b_{i})
is the interval given by the definition of matching between
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{i},r_{i})
and
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n,i},r_{i})
.
By the Condition (B1) applied to
y_{n,i}
and by the condition (B4) applied to
x_{i}
, we have either

\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}^{x_{i}}f^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{i})=\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}f^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{i})\qquad\text{or}\qquad m(\xi_{i},r_{i})\geq\frac{c}{q}.

Now, using the first part of the proof to estimate the Birkhoff sum
\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}f^{\prime}(y_{n,i})
along the orbit subsegment
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n,i},r_{i})\subset\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})
, and Lemma
4.14
(which can be applied since
f\in\mathcal{P}SymLog(T)
) to estimate
|\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}^{x_{i}}f^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{i})|
, we get that, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, since
b_{i}-a_{i}\leqslant c/q_{n}
by properties
(B1)
and
(B3)
of balanced matchings (see Definition
4.6
), we get

\left|\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}f^{\prime}(x_{i})\right|\leqslant\left|\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}f^{\prime}(y_{n,i})\right|+\frac{2\kappa}{c}+|\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}f^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{i})||b_{i}-a_{i}|\leqslant 2B_{0}q_{n}+\frac{{\hat{B}q_{n}^{2}}}{cq_{n}}\leqslant\left(2B_{0}+\frac{\hat{B}}{c}\right)\,q_{n},

(where
\hat{B}
is the constant given by Lemma
4.14
, which
depends only on
c>0
). Summing up over
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
, where
\ell\leqslant\kappa
, this concludes the proof of the desired linear bounds on trimmed
derivatives.
∎

4.3.3.
The exponential tails via matchings criterion

We can now state and prove the criterion to prove exponential tails from the existence of good matchings (in the sense of Definition
4.16
).

Proposition 4.20

(Exponential tightness via matchings)
.

Assume
f\in{SymLog^{2}\left(T\right)}
has symmetric logarithmic singularities. Let
(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of
\gamma
-good
c
-balanced

(y_{n},q_{n})
-matchings.
Then there exists
b_{0}>0
and
K>0
such that for every
b\geqslant b_{0}
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}Leb(\{x\in M_{n}:|S_{q_{n}}(f)(x)-S_{q_{n}}(f)(y_{n})|\geqslant b\})\leq Ke^{-b/C_{f}}.

Proof.

Since by assumption the
(y_{n},q_{n})
matchings
M_{n}
are part of a sequence

(M_{n})_{n}
of good matchings,
by Lemma
4.19
, there exists
B>0
such that

(M_{n})_{n}
are
(\gamma,B)
-good matchings, so that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.5)

|\tilde{S}_{r}(f^{\prime})(x)|<Bq_{n}\quad\text{for }0\leq r\leq q_{n},\ {\text{for\ any}\ x\in M_{n}}.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in M_{n}
.
Write again
f=f_{p}+g
where
f_{p}\in{\mathcal{P}SymLog\left(T\right)}
and

g
is in particular bounded (in view of Definition
2.1
).
Let us split the Birkhoff sums keeping aside the contributions of the trimmed visits given by the pure logarithmic singularities of
f_{p}
as:

(4.6)

\begin{split}|S_{q_{n}}&(f_{p})(x)-S_{q_{n}}(f_{p})(y_{n})|\\
&\leq\left|\sum_{\beta\in Disc(T)}{C_{\beta}}\log(m(x,\beta,q_{n}))-{C_{\beta}}\log{(m(y_{n},\beta,q_{n}))}\right|+|\tilde{S}_{q_{n}}(f_{p})(x)-\tilde{S}_{q_{n}}(f_{p})(y_{n})|.\end{split}

Since all good matchings
M_{n}
are
\kappa
-matching sets with the same
\kappa
(see Definition
4.4
), there exists a decomposition of the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{n})
and
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y,q_{n})
into
k\leq\kappa
pieces

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{1},r_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{2},r_{2})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(x_{k},r_{k}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n,1},r_{1})\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n,2},r_{2})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n,k},r_{k}),

with
r_{1}+\ldots+r_{k}=q_{n}
, as in Definition
4.1
.
In view of Lemma
4.13
, we can estimate the Birkhoff sums
in the following way

(4.7)

\displaystyle|\tilde{S}_{q_{n}}(f_{p})(x)-\tilde{S}_{q_{n}}(f_{p})(y_{n})|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\tilde{S}_{q_{n}}(f_{p})(x)-\tilde{S}_{q_{n}}^{x}(f_{p})(y_{n})\right|+E

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\left(\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}(f_{p})(x_{i})-\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}^{x}(f_{p})(y_{n,i})\right)\right|+E,

where
E
is the constant given by Lemma
4.13
.

We claim that we get that for every
i=1,\ldots,k
and every
z\in[x_{i},y_{n,i}]
we have the following estimate

\left|\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}^{x}({f_{p}}^{\prime})(z)\right|\leq B^{\prime}\cdot q_{n}\ \ \text{ for every }z\in[x_{i},y_{n,i}],\qquad\text{where}\ B^{\prime}:=B+\frac{\hat{B}}{c}.

(Notice that, although this is a linear bound on trimmed Birkhoff sums of the derivative, it does not follow from
(G2)
in the Definition
4.16
of good matching since interval
[x_{i},y_{n,i}]
is not necessarily included in
M_{n}
, so we have to prove it.)
To prove the claim, in view of Remark
4.7
, by Lemma
4.14
(and Remark
4.15
), we get

(4.8)

|\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}^{x}({f_{p}}^{\prime\prime})(z)|\leq\hat{B}\cdot q_{n}^{2},\text{ for every }z\in[x_{i},y_{n,i}]

where
\hat{B}
does not depend on the point or
n
.
By (
4.5
) and by (B3) in the Definition
4.6
, we then get the claimed bound.
Now, by Mean Value Theorem we get from (
4.7
) that

(4.9)

|\tilde{S}_{q_{n}}(f_{p})(x)-\tilde{S}_{q_{n}}(f_{p})(y_{n})|\leq B^{\prime\prime},\quad where\,B^{\prime\prime}:=\frac{B^{\prime}\kappa}{c}+E.

To estimate the analogous quantity for
g
, one can again use the
decomposition into
k
matched pairs of suborbits and mean value theorem, with
the trivial estimates
\|S_{r_{i}}g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leqslant r_{i}\|g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
(and recalling that
r_{1}+\cdots+r_{\kappa}=q_{n}
and

q_{n}|I_{n}|\leqslant 1
) to get that

|S_{q_{n}}(g)(x)-S_{q_{n}}(g)(y_{n})|\leq\left|\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\left(\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}(g)(x_{i})-\tilde{S}_{r_{i}}(g)(y_{n,i})\right)\right|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\|g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}r_{i}|I_{n}|\leqslant\frac{\|g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{c}.

Combining these estimates with
(
4.6
) and (
4.9
), we get

(4.10)

|S_{q_{n}}(f)(x)-S_{q_{n}}(f)(y_{n})|\leq\left|\sum_{\beta\in Disc(T)}{C_{\beta}}\log(m(x,\beta,q_{n}))-{C_{\beta}}\log{(m(y_{n},\beta,q_{n}))}\right|+B^{\prime\prime}+\frac{\|g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{c}

Set
B^{\prime\prime\prime}:=B^{\prime\prime}+\|g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}/c
. Let
b_{0}:=10(B^{\prime\prime\prime}/C_{f}-\log c)
, where
c
is given by (B1) and let
b\geq b_{0}
. We claim that if

\min_{\beta\in Disc(T)}m(x,\beta,q_{n})\geq\frac{e^{-b/C_{f}+(B^{\prime\prime\prime}/C_{f}-\log c)}}{q_{n}},

then
|S_{q_{n}}(f)(x)-S_{q_{n}}(f)(y_{n})|<b
. Indeed, by (
4.10
) and properties (B1) and (B2) in the Definition
4.6
of balanced matching, we have

|S_{q_{n}}(f)(x)-S_{q_{n}}(f)(y_{n})|\leq\sum_{\beta\in Disc(T)}C_{\beta}(-\log e^{-b/C_{f}+(B^{\prime\prime\prime}/C_{f}-\log c)}+\log c)+B^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq b.

Thus

\displaystyle\{x\in M_{n}

\displaystyle:|S_{q_{n}}(f)(x)-S_{q_{n}}(f)(y_{n})|\geqslant b\}\subset

\displaystyle\subset\bigcup_{\beta\in Disc(T)}\bigcup_{i=0}^{q_{n}}T^{-i}\left[\beta-\frac{e^{-b/C_{f}+(B^{\prime\prime\prime}/C_{f}-\log c)}}{q_{n}},\beta+\frac{e^{-b/C_{f}+(B^{\prime\prime\prime}/C_{f}-\log c)}}{q_{n}}\right].

Hence we get that

Leb\left(\{x\in M_{n}:|S_{q_{n}}(f)(x)-S_{q_{n}}(f)(y_{n})|\geqslant b\}\right)\leq K\cdot e^{-b/C_{f}},

where
K:=2d\cdot e^{B^{\prime\prime\prime}/C_{f}-\log c}
, which finishes the proof.

∎

5.
Construction of BT rigidity degenerations and good matchings.

The main goal of this section is to prove Proposition
4.18
, i.e. to show that for almost every (Keane) IET we can obtain a sequence of good matchings. To produce good matchings, we first prove Proposition
3.3
, by showing the existence of rectangle presentations (see §
3.1.1
) with BT rigid presentations (in the sense of Definition
3.2
) for almost every translation surface in
\mathcal{H}(1,1)
. These, in turn, are constructed using (natural extensions of) the Rauzy-Veech induction algorithm, by now a classical and powerful tool to study IETs and translation surfaces.

In §
5.1
we first recall some basic definitions and properties concerning Rauzy-Veech induction needed in the rest of this paper. The existence of BT-degenerations (Prop.
3.3
) is proved in §
5.2
cutting and pasting good presentations of zippered rectangles. Then, in §
5.3
we recall a result proved by the second author
[
Ul11
]
(see also
[
FKU
]
for the formulation given here) that allows to produce times with linear bounds on trimmed derivatives using (the natural extension of) Rauzy-Veech induction.
Finally, we combine these two results in §
3.1.6
.
to produce the desired good matchings from BT-degeneration times
q_{n}
such that there is an orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(y_{n},q_{n})
with linear bounds on trimmed Birkhoff sums.

5.1.
Background on Rauzy-Veech induction

We recall here some results on Rauzy-Veech (RV for short) induction. For more details on the definition of the algorithm
we refer e.g. to lecture notes by Yoccoz
[
Yo10
]
or Viana
[
Vi
]
.

5.1.1.
The Rauzy-Veech algorithm for IETs.

The Rauzy-Veech induction algorithm associates to
a.e. IET,
a sequence of IETs which are induced maps of
T
onto a sequence of nested subintervals
I^{(n)}
contained in
[0,1]
. These are chosen so that the induced maps are again IETs of the same number
d
of exchanged intervals. The interval
I^{\prime}:=I^{(1)}\subset I^{(0)}:=[0,1]
associated to one step of the algorithm is defined as follows: let
I^{t}_{d}:=[\beta_{d-1},1]
(where
t
stays for
top
) be the last (i.e. right-most) exchanged subinterval and let
I^{b}_{d}

be the last after the exchange, i.e.
I^{b}_{d}:=T(I_{j_{d}})
where
j_{d}
is such that

T(I_{j_{d}})
has
1
as right endpoint.
If
|I^{t}_{d}|>|I^{b}_{d}|
(called case
top
or
type

0
),
[0,1]\backslash I^{t}_{d}=[0,\beta_{d-1})
, while if
|I^{b}_{d}|>|I^{t}_{d}|
(called case
bottom
, or
type

1
),
I^{\prime}:=[0,1]\backslash I^{b}_{d}
.
We denote by
T^{\prime}
is the induced IET obtained as first return map on
I^{\prime}
.
The map
Rauzy-Veech map

\mathcal{V}
then associates to a
T=(\pi,\lambda)
such that
|I^{t}_{d}|\neq|I^{b}_{d}|

the IET
\mathcal{V}(T)=\mathcal{V}(\pi,\lambda)
obtained by renormalizing
T^{\prime}
by
|I^{\prime}|
so that the renormalized IET is again defined on an unit interval. If
|I^{t}_{d}|=|I^{b}_{d}|
, the induction is not defined.

The Rauzy-Veech map
\mathcal{V}
is then defined for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
on a
full Lebesgue measure subset of the space

(5.1)

X:=X(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}\times\Delta_{d}=\{(\pi,\lambda)\ |\ \pi\in\mathcal{R},\ \lambda\in\Delta_{d}\},

where
\mathcal{R}
is the
Rauzy-class
of
\pi
(namely the smallest invariant set of permutations closed under iterations of
\mathcal{V}
) and
\Delta_{d}
the lengths simplex, defined in §
2.1.3
(precisely on the set
of IETs which satisfy the Keane condition

Veech proved in
[
Ve82
]
that
\mathcal{V}
is conservative and admits an invariant measure
\mu_{\mathcal{V}}
on
X(\mathcal{R})
which is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure, but infinite. Zorich showed in
[
Zo96
]
that one can accelerate
15
15

15

The acceleration of a map is obtained a.e.-defining an integer valued function
z(T)
which gives the return time to an appropriate section. The accelerated map is then given by
\mathcal{Z}(T):=\mathcal{V}^{z(T)}(T)
.
the map
\mathcal{V}
in order to obtain a map
\mathcal{Z}
, which we call
Zorich map
, that admits a
finite
invariant measure
\mu_{\mathcal{Z}}
. Let us also recall that both
\mathcal{V}
and its acceleration
\mathcal{Z}
are
ergodic
with respect to
\mu_{\mathcal{V}}
and
\mu_{\mathcal{Z}}
respectively
[
Ve82
,
Zo96
]
.

5.1.2.
Rauzy-Veech matrices and lengths cylinders.

Given
T=(\pi,\lambda)
in the domain of
\mathcal{V}
, write
\mathcal{V}(\pi,\lambda)=(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
. Then, denoting by
|\cdot|
the vector norm
|{\lambda}|=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}
, one can write

\lambda^{\prime}=\frac{(A)^{-1}\lambda}{|(A)^{-1}\lambda|},\quad\text{where}\ A=A(T)\in SL(d,\mathbb{Z})

is
a non-negative matrix with entries
0
or
1
and with
1
on the diagonal and only one
1
off the diagonal.
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, set
A_{n}:=A(\mathcal{V}^{n}T)
. We will say that
(A_{n})_{n}
is the sequence of Rauzy-Veech matrices associated to
T
. Then

(5.2)

\mathcal{V}^{n}(\pi_{0},\lambda)=\left(\pi^{(n)},\lambda^{(n)}\right),\quad\text{where}\ \lambda^{(n)}=\frac{A(0,n)^{-1}\lambda}{\left|A(0,n)^{-1}\lambda\right|},\ \text{for}\ \ A(0,n):=A_{1}A_{2}\cdots A_{n}.

5.1.3.
Invertible Rauzy-Veech induction and zippered rectangles

The
natural extension

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
(resp.
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
) of the Rauzy-Veech map
\mathcal{V}
(resp. of the Zorich map
\mathcal{Z}
), also known as
invertible Rauzy-Veech
(resp.
invertible Zorich
induction) is an invertible map
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
(resp.
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
) defined on a domain
\widehat{X}
which is an
extension
of
\mathcal{V}
(resp.
\mathcal{Z}
), i.e. such that there exists a projection
p\colon\widehat{X}\rightarrow X
for which
p\widehat{\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{V}p
(resp.
p\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathcal{Z}p
).

To define the domain of the
natural extensions
of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
, for each
\pi\in\mathcal{R}
let
\Theta_{\pi}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the polyhedral cones given by the inequalities

\Theta_{\pi}:=\left\{{\tau}=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \Big|\quad\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{\pi^{-1}i}<0,\,k=1,\dots,d-1\right\},

and let
\widehat{X}_{d}
be the following space of triples (that admits a geometric interpretation in terms of the space of zippered rectangles, see for example
[
Yo10
,
Vi
]
for details):

\widehat{X}_{d}:=\widehat{X}_{d}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathcal{R}}\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{\pi}=\{\,(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau})\ |\quad\pi\in\mathcal{R},\,{\lambda}\in\Delta_{d},\,{\tau}\in\Theta_{\pi}\}.

The action of the invertible extension
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
on triples
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}_{d}
is obtained by extending the projective action of
\mathcal{V}
, i.e. defined by

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}),\qquad\text{where}\ (\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})=\mathcal{V}(\pi,\lambda),\quad\tau^{\prime}=\frac{A^{-1}\tau}{|A^{-1}\lambda|},

where
A=A(T)\in SL(d,\mathbb{Z})
is the Rauzy-Veech matrix defined in
§
5.1.2
. To each triple
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}_{d}
with a (bi-)infinite orbit
16
16

16

This is the case for example
if both
\lambda
and
\tau
have coordinates that are irrationally
independent. More precisely,
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
is
defined for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
if and only if
(\pi,\lambda)
satisfies the
Keane condition (that guarantees that
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
is defined for all
i\geqslant 0
, and the translation surface associated to

(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
has no horizontal saddle connections, see
[
MUY
]
and
[
Be
]
.
under

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
we can associate a
bi-infinite
sequence

(A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
of Rauzy-Veech matrices, by setting

(5.3)

A_{i}:=A(\pi^{(i)},\lambda^{(i)}),\quad\text{where}\ (\pi^{(i)},\lambda^{(i)},\tau^{(i)}):=\mathcal{V}^{i}(\pi,\lambda,\tau),\ \ \text{for \ all}\ i\in\mathbb{Z}.

It is well known that
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
preserves a natural (infinite)
17
17

17

To have a
finite
invariant measure, one can consider again the (invertible) Zorich acceleration
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
, restricted to the hypersurface
\hat{X}^{1}_{d}
of triples
(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau})
with
Area(\pi,{\lambda},{\tau})=1
.
invariant measure
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{V}}
on
\widehat{X}_{d}
, absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
Moreover,
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
is
conservative
and ergodic with respect to
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{V}}
; in particular, Poincaré recurrence holds (see e.g.
[
Vi
]
for details).

5.1.4.
Cylinders and Markovian structure

Given any triple
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
, let
A_{i}:=A(\mathcal{V}^{i}(\pi,\lambda,\tau))
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
be the associated (bi-sided) sequence
of RV matrices, see (
5.3
). Fix two integers
n,m>0
and set

(5.4)

A^{-}:=A_{-m}A_{-m+1}\cdots A_{-1},\quad\text{and}\quad A^{+}:=A_{0}A_{1}\cdots A_{n-1},

and let us define

(5.5)

\Delta_{A^{+}}:=A^{+}\,\Delta_{d}=\left\{\frac{A^{+}\lambda}{|A^{+}\lambda|},\quad\lambda\in\Delta_{d}\right\}\subset\Delta_{d}

the simplex image of
\Delta_{d}
by the projective action of
A^{+}
, and denoting by
\pi^{(-m)}
, as in (
5.3
), the permutation of
\mathcal{V}^{-m}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
,

(5.6)

\Theta_{A^{-}}:=A^{-}\,\Theta_{\pi^{(-m)}}=\left\{{A^{-}\tau},\quad\tau\in\Theta_{\pi^{(-m)}}\right\}\subset\Theta_{\pi}

the simplex image of
\Theta_{\pi^{(-m)}}
by the action of
A^{-}
. Notice that if
A^{\pm}=B^{\pm}C^{\pm}
then

(5.7)

\Delta_{B^{+}C^{+}}\subset\Delta_{B^{+}},\quad\text{while}\quad\Theta_{B^{-}C^{-}}\subset\Theta_{C^{-}}.

The product set
\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A^{+}}\times\Theta_{A^{-}}

plays the role of (bisided)
cylinder
for the symbolic coding of RV induction
18
18

18

Indeed, given any triple
(\pi,\lambda^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
with
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Delta_{A^{+}}
and
\tau^{\prime}\in\Theta_{A^{-}}
, is such that the types and the Rauzy-matrices of the iterates
\mathcal{V}^{i}(S)
for
i=-{m},\dots,n
have the same permutations and the same types than the corresponding iterates
\mathcal{V}^{i}(T)
for
i=-{m},\dots,n-1
of
T
; thus in particular the sequence
(A^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
or Rauzy-Veech matrices for
(\pi,\lambda^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
is such that
A_{i}^{\prime}=A_{i}
for
i=-{m},\dots,n-1
.
. In particular, we have the following relation (see for example
[
FKU
]
, § 8.2), which shows that
\mathcal{V}^{n}
acts as (the
n
-power of) the
shift
map on the symbolic coding given by RV matrices:

(5.8)

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{n}\left(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A^{+}}\times\Theta_{A^{-}}\right)=\{\pi_{n}\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{A^{-}A^{+}}.

We recall that if
A^{+}>0
is a positive matrix (i.e. all entries are strictly positive), then
\Delta_{A^{+}}\subset\Delta_{d}
is compact. We say (following
[
AGY
]
) that
A^{-}
is
strongly positive
if
\Theta_{A^{-}}\subset\Theta_{\pi}
is also compact. For almost every
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
there exists
n,m
such that
A^{+}
and
A^{-}
defined as in (
5.4
) are both strongly positive, so that the product
\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A^{+}}\times\Theta_{A^{-}}
is a compact set in
\hat{X}_{d}
. We say that
K\subset\hat{X}_{d}
is a
neat
compact set if it has the form
K=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A^{+}}\times\Theta_{A^{-}}
where
A^{+},A^{-}
are both strongly positive matrices, that correspond to a
neat
path
19
19

19

We recall that the types of a (finite) sequence of moves of Rauzy-Veech induction can be described in terms of (finite) paths on the Rauzy-class of
\pi
. In
[
AGY
]
, a path
\gamma
is called
neat
if
\gamma=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{e}
implies that either
\gamma=\gamma_{i}
or
\gamma=\gamma_{e}
.
in the sense of
[
AGY
]
.

5.2.
Constructing bounded type rigidity degeneration via Rauzy-Veech

In this section we prove Proposition
3.3
, by showing the existence of BT rigid rectangle presentations (as defined in §
3.1.4
)
for a.e.
S\in\mathcal{H}(1^{2g-2})
. To build these rectangle presentations,
we will use the invertible Rauzy-Veech induction
\hat{\mathcal{V}}
and
zippered rectangles.

5.2.1.
The BT rigidity open sets.

The following Lemma provides some
open sets in the parameter space

\hat{X}_{4g-3}

giving
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigidity:

Lemma 5.1

(Rauzy-Veech parameters for BT rigid rectangle presentations)
.

For every

C\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
, where
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
with
0<c_{0}<c_{1}<1

and any
0<\epsilon,\rho<1
, there exists an open sets
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}(C,\epsilon,\rho)
in the space
of zippered rectangles
\hat{X}_{d}
with
d=4g-3
such that if the
translation
surface
S
is given by a triple
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)

in
\mathcal{U}
, then
S
admits a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid
rectangle presentation.

We anticipate that the BT rigid rectangle presentation that we will build are not directly given by the zippered rectangles corresponding to the triple, but are obtained
from
them by suitably cutting and pasting them (see Figure
10
and the proof of Lemma
5.1
).

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

Let
us first define
auxiliary open set
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(C^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
,
for parameters
\epsilon^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}
in
(0,1)
and
C^{\prime}=[c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}]
with
0<c_{0}^{\prime}<c_{1}^{\prime}<1
.
We will show that, given
\epsilon,\rho
and
C
as in the statement, if
\epsilon^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}
are chosen sufficiently small and
c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}
suitably, then we can take
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(C^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
to be the desired open set. The set
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(C^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})

is the subset of
triples
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
in
\hat{X}_{d}
given by

\mathcal{U}^{\prime}:=\{(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\{\pi_{0}\}\times\Lambda_{c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}}\times\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}\},

where
\pi_{0}
is a standard permutation and
\Lambda_{c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}}\subset\Delta_{d}
and
\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}\subset\Theta_{\pi_{0}}
are given by:

(5.9)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\{\lambda\in\Delta_{d}\mid\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{d-1}<\epsilon^{\prime}\ \text{ and }c_{0}^{\prime}<\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{d}<c_{1}^{\prime}\}\subset\Delta_{d},

(5.10)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\{\tau\in\Theta_{\pi_{0}}\mid 0<\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{d-1}<\rho^{\prime}\}\subset\Theta_{\pi_{0}}.

Since
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is defined by open conditions it is clearly open. An example of a polygonal presentation as well as the corresponding zippered rectangles with data
(\lambda,\tau,\pi)

in
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
are shown in Figure
9
(left). Let us denote
R_{1},\dots,R_{d}
the rectangles of the corresponding zippered rectangles (
R_{i}
being the
rectangle over the interval
I_{i}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
). Observe that that, on
one hand (because of the inequalities on
\lambda
) the ’middle rectangles’

R_{2},\ldots,R_{d-1}
have
\epsilon^{\prime}
-small width, while on the other hand,
there is a substantial shift dynamics
on the remaining two towers (that could be seen as a bounded type rotation-like
dynamics), given by
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{d}
, that by construction satisfies

c_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{d}\leqslant c_{1}^{\prime}
.
Moreover, (in view of the inequalities on
\tau
), the singularities of the
translation surface (shown as red dots in Figure
9
) are all

cramped
, i.e. also have vertical displacement at most
d\rho^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, the ’middle’ heights
q_{2},\ldots,q_{d-1}
are all comparable (they
differ by at most
d\rho^{\prime}
).

Figure 9.
The polygonal representation (left) and the corresponding zippered rectangles presentation (right) given by a triple
(\lambda,\pi,\tau)
in
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.
In this picture the width of the rectangles
R_{2},R_{2},R_{4}
over
I_{2},I_{3},I_{4}
is less then
\epsilon^{\prime}
and as the difference between the vertical coordinates of the singularities (red dots in the picture) is controlled by
\rho^{\prime}
.

To produce a BT rigid rectangle presentation from these zippered rectangles,
we consider the shorter base interval
\widetilde{I}\subset I:=[0,1]
given by

\tilde{I}:=\left[\tfrac{1}{8},\tfrac{9}{8}-\lambda_{d}\right)=\left[\tfrac{1}{8},(1-\lambda_{d})+\tfrac{1}{8}\right)\subset[0,1].

A rectangle presentation over
\tilde{I}
(with
d+1
rectangles) is obtained by
cutting and stacking the rectangles over
I
(see
Figure
10
) to get rectangles
\tilde{R}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{R}_{d+1}
over the intervals
\tilde{I}_{1},\cdots\tilde{I}_{d+1}
of the
IET
\tilde{T}
obtained inducing
T
to
\tilde{I}
. The glueings between the
top sides of the rectangle presentation are given by the IET
\tilde{T}
. One
can check (see Figure
10
) that the permutation

\tilde{\pi}
of
d+1
elements of
\tilde{I}
and the length vector

\tilde{\lambda}=(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}_{d+1})
are given
by:

(5.11)

\tilde{\pi}=(2\ \star\ \star\ \star\ (d+1)\ 1),\qquad\tilde{\lambda}=\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{d},\lambda_{d}-\tfrac{1}{8},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{d-1},\tfrac{1}{8}\right).

Thus, all intervals of continuity of
\tilde{T}
are
\epsilon^{\prime}
-small, but
the first, the second and the last one. The second and the last one,
\tilde{I}_{2}
and
\tilde{I}_{d+1}
, undergo the same displacement (i.e. their distance
is the same before and after the exchange). The
first interval
\tilde{I}_{1}
, by definition of
\Lambda_{c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}}
, has length of
c_{0}^{\prime}<|\tilde{I}_{1}|<c_{1}^{\prime}
and is responsible
for the value of the displacement of
\tilde{I}_{2}
and
\tilde{I}_{d+1}
under

\tilde{T}
.

Notice also that the rectangle over
\tilde{I}_{1}
has height
q_{1}
, and the
rectangles over
\tilde{I}_{2}
and
\tilde{I}_{d+1}
are of equal height (see
Figure
10
), given by

q:=q_{1}+q_{d}
(since they are both obtained cutting and stacking parts of the zippered rectangles over
I_{1}
and
I_{d}
).

Let us estimate the relative displacement
\Delta:=q|\tilde{I}_{1}|
. By construction, since

|\tilde{I}_{1}|=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{d}
, the inequalities on
\lambda
given by
being in

\Lambda_{c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}}
,
given that
c_{0}^{\prime}<|\tilde{I}_{1}|<c_{1}^{\prime}
.
To guarantee that
\Delta\in C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
, we need to show that we can pick
c_{0}^{\prime}
and
c_{1}^{\prime}
so that
c_{0}\leq qc_{0}^{\prime}<qc_{1}^{\prime}\leq c_{1}
. For this purpose, let us evaluate
q
. On one hand we have

1-\operatorname{Area}(\tilde{R}_{1})\geq\operatorname{Area}(\tilde{R}_{2}\cup\tilde{R}_{d+1})=1-\operatorname{Area}(\tilde{R}_{1}\cup\tilde{R}_{3}\cup\ldots\cup\tilde{R}_{d})

and since
\operatorname{Area}(\tilde{R}_{2}\cup\tilde{R}_{d+1})=q(\tilde{\lambda}_{2}+\tilde{\lambda}_{d+1})=q\lambda_{d}
, we get

\lambda_{d}^{-1}(1-\operatorname{Area}(\tilde{R}_{1}))\geq q\geq\lambda_{d}^{-1}(1-\operatorname{Area}(\tilde{R}_{1}\cup\tilde{R}_{3}\cup\ldots\cup\tilde{R}_{d})).

From the conditions on
\lambda_{d}
, we have

2\lambda_{d}-c_{1}^{\prime}\leq\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{d}\leq 1=\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{d}\leq 2\lambda_{d}+\epsilon^{\prime}-c_{0}^{\prime}

and thus
{(1-\epsilon^{\prime}+c_{0}^{\prime})}/{2}\leq\lambda_{d}\leq{(1+c_{1}^{\prime})/}{2}
.
Moreover, since
q_{1}\leq q
, then
\operatorname{Area}(\tilde{R}_{1})\leq qc_{1}^{\prime}
.
Hence

q\leq\lambda_{d}^{-1}(1-\operatorname{Area}(\tilde{R}_{1}))\leq\frac{2}{1-\epsilon^{\prime}+c_{0}^{\prime}}(1-qc_{1}^{\prime})\quad\Rightarrow\quad q\leq\frac{2}{(1-\epsilon^{\prime}+c_{0}^{\prime})(1+c_{1}^{\prime})}.

Moreover, since the heights of zippered rectangles are given by
[\tilde{q}_{j}]_{j=1}^{d}=\Omega_{\tilde{\pi}}^{T}[\tau_{j}]_{j=1}^{d}
, we have that the
heights
q_{3},\ldots,q_{d}
of the rectangles
\tilde{R}_{3},\ldots,\tilde{R}_{d}
satisfy

(5.12)

-d\rho^{\prime}\leq-d\max_{j=2,\ldots,d-1}\tau_{j}\leq\min_{j=3,\ldots,d}(q_{j}-q)\leq\max_{j=3,\ldots,d}(q_{j}-q)\leqslant d\max_{j=2,\ldots,d-1}\tau_{j}\leq d\rho^{\prime}

and thus

q\geq\lambda_{d}^{-1}(1-\operatorname{Area}(\tilde{R}_{1}\cup\tilde{R}_{3}\cup\ldots\cup\tilde{R}_{d}))\geq\frac{2}{1+c_{1}^{\prime}}(1-qc_{1}^{\prime}-q\epsilon^{\prime}-d\rho^{\prime}\epsilon^{\prime}).

Thus we obtain

q\geq\frac{2(1-d\rho^{\prime}\epsilon^{\prime})}{(1+c_{1}^{\prime})(1+c_{1}^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime})}\geq\frac{2(1-3\epsilon^{\prime})}{(1+c_{1}^{\prime})(1+c_{1}^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime})}.

To conclude, for a given
c_{0},c_{1}
, we need to pick
c_{0}^{\prime}
and
c_{1}^{\prime}
so that

(5.13)

c_{0}<\frac{2c_{0}^{\prime}(1-3\epsilon^{\prime})}{(1+c_{1}^{\prime})(1+c_{1}^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime})}\quad\text{ and }\quad c_{1}>\frac{2c_{1}^{\prime}}{(1-\epsilon^{\prime}+c_{0}^{\prime})(1+c_{1}^{\prime})}.

Since the expression on the left-hand side is strictly smaller than the expression on the right-hand side for every choice of
c_{0}^{\prime}
and
c_{1}^{\prime}
, by picking
\epsilon^{\prime}
small enough, depending on
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
, we can guarantee that
\Delta\in[c_{0},c_{1}]
.

Figure 10.
By choosing properly a shorter subinterval as a base of the rectangle presentation, we obtain
a BT rigid rectangle presentation. The green and purple sides are identified.

The glueings of vertical sides of the rectangles shown in (see
Figure
10
) are such that the left side of the
rectangle over
\tilde{I}_{1}
is identified (by a translation) to the top right
side of the last rectangle, over
\tilde{I}_{d+1}
. Moreover, by construction (by the inequalities on
\tau
given by being in

\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
), the points of discontinuity
of the vertical glueings between the rectangles
\tilde{R}_{2},\dots,\tilde{R}_{d+1}
have vertical
distance proportional
q\rho^{\prime}
(with a proportionality constant which depends on the ratios of heights, which are by construction all comparable. This shows that, choosing

\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon/4
sufficiently small so that (
5.13
) is satisfied and
\rho^{\prime}=\rho/4
, one can guarantee that
for any triples
(\lambda,\pi,\tau)
that visit
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(C,\epsilon^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
,
the
areas
(which are proportional to the widths) of
\tilde{R}_{3},\cdots,\tilde{R}_{d}
are less then
\epsilon
and the vertical distance
between discontinuities is less then
\rho q
.
This concludes the proof that
X
admits a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid rectangle representation in the sense of
Definition
3.2
and thus that we can take
\mathcal{U}(C,\epsilon,\rho)
to be equal to this
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(C^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
.
∎

The proof of Proposition
3.3
(namely the existence of BT rigid rectangle presentations) follows immediately by this Lemma and ergodicity of Rauzy-Veech induction.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Consider the set
\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{U}_{C,\epsilon,\rho}
given
by Lemma
5.1
.
In view of the ergodicity of
\hat{\mathcal{V}}
, for almost every

(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\hat{X}_{d}
, there exists an increasing sequence

\{n_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
s.t.

(5.14)

\mathcal{R}^{n_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\mathcal{U}\qquad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

Let us show that this implies that the surface
X
given by

(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
has an increasing sequence
(q_{n})_{n}
of
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid times.
To see this, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
consider the
n_{k}^{th}
inducing subinterval
I^{(n_{k})}
given by Rauzy-Veech induction starting from
T=(\lambda,\pi)
and let

\lambda^{(n_{k})}
be the length vector of
\mathcal{V}^{n_{k}}(T)
. Notice that the lengths of the induced IET on
I^{(n_{k})}
are

|I^{(n_{k})}|=\lambda^{(n_{k})}_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
.
Let us denote by
\tilde{T}_{k}
the induced IET on the interval

\tilde{I}^{k}:=\left[\tfrac{1}{8}|I^{(n_{k})}|,\tfrac{9}{8}|I^{(n_{k})}|-\lambda^{(n_{k})}_{5}\right)\subset I^{(n_{k})}.

We denote the interval exchanged by
\tilde{T}_{k}
by
\tilde{I}^{k}_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant{d+1}
. Then, as in the proof of Lemma
5.1
(see
(
5.11
)),
\tilde{T}_{k}
is an IET with a standard permutation

\tilde{\pi}
and length vector

\tilde{\lambda}^{k}:=|I^{(n_{k})}|\left[\lambda^{(n_{k})}_{1}-\lambda^{(n_{k})}_{d},\ \lambda^{(n_{k})}_{d}-\tfrac{1}{8},\ \lambda^{(n_{k})}_{2},\ \ldots,\ \lambda^{(n_{k})}_{d-1},\ \tfrac{1}{8}\right).

From Lemma
5.1
, since
(\lambda^{(n_{k})},\pi^{(n_{k})},\tau^{(n_{k})})\in\mathcal{U}
, the rectangles
\tilde{R}^{k}_{i}
,

1\leqslant i\leqslant d+1
over the intervals
\tilde{I}_{i}^{k}
give a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT
rigid presentation. Finally, since by construction
|\tilde{I}^{k}|\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, the heights of the rectangles of
\mathcal{R}_{k}
, which are all comparable (see the proof of Lemma
5.1
) grow to infinity as
k
grows.
∎

5.2.2.
Typical existence of BT-rigid times.

Let us now deduce Corollary
3.4
from Proposition
3.3
, by showing that BT rigid rectangle presentations provide BT-rigidity times.

Proof of Corollary
3.4
.

Given
C\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\epsilon>0
, choose
\rho:=\epsilon/3d
.
By Proposition
3.3
, almost every
S
in
\mathcal{H}(1^{2g-2})

admits a sequence of

(C,\epsilon/3,\rho)
-BT rigid rectangle presentations
\{\tilde{R}^{k}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{R}^{k}_{d+1}\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Set

(5.15)

q_{k}:=q^{(n_{k})}_{1}+q^{(n_{k})}_{d},\qquad\Delta_{k}:=q_{k}|\tilde{I}_{1}^{k}|,

so that
q_{k}
is the common height of
\tilde{R}^{k}_{2}
and
\tilde{R}^{k}_{d+1}

(since
\tilde{R}^{k}_{2}
and
\tilde{R}^{k}_{d+1}
come from zippered rectangles over

I^{k}_{1}
and
I^{k}_{d}
respectively) and

\Delta_{k}
the relative displacement of the continuity intervals
\tilde{I}^{k}_{2}
and
\tilde{I}^{k}_{d+1}
of the IET
\tilde{T}_{k}
.

We will show that for each
k\in\mathbb{N},

q_{k}
is a
(C,\epsilon)
-rigidity time (in the sense of Definition
3.1
).

Consider a set of initial points
x
which belong to the subset
S^{k}:=(\tilde{R}^{k}_{1}\cup\tilde{R}^{k}_{2}\cup\tilde{R}^{k}_{d+1})\setminus(\varphi_{-q}(\tilde{R}^{k}_{3}\cup\ldots\cup\tilde{R}^{k}_{d}))
. Notice that
S^{k}
, after glueing the
boundaries of the rectangles according to the identifications, is (essentially)
topologically
a torus with a slit. In order to have rigidity, we need also to remove a
bad zone
, that we call
B_{k}
and is defined as follows. For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
b_{k}:=\tilde{\lambda}^{k}_{1}+\cdots+\tilde{\lambda}^{k}_{d}
be the
right endpoint of
\tilde{I}^{k}_{d}
and let

\underline{\xi}_{k}
and
\overline{\xi}_{k}
be respectively the smallest and
largest heights of the discontinuities on the boundaries of
\tilde{R}_{2},\ldots,\tilde{R}_{d+1}
, namely

\underline{\xi}_{k}:=\min\{\tau_{1}^{(n_{k})},\tau_{1}^{(n_{k})}+\tau_{2}^{(n_{k})},\cdots,\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\tau_{i}^{(n_{k})}\},\qquad\overline{\xi}_{k}:=\max\{\tau_{1}^{(n_{k})},\tau_{1}^{(n_{k})}+\tau_{2}^{(n_{k})},\cdots,\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\tau_{i}^{(n_{k})}\}.

Define
X_{k}:=S^{k}\backslash B_{k}
(or equivalently, recalling the definition
of
S^{k}
,
X_{k}=(\tilde{R}^{k}_{1}\cup\tilde{R}^{k}_{2}\cup\tilde{R}^{k}_{d+1})\backslash B_{k}
), where
B_{k}
is defined to be the rectangular region (shaded in
Figure
5
) of width
\tilde{\lambda}^{k}_{1}
next to the
cramping of the singularities, given by

(5.16)

B_{k}=\left[b_{k},b_{k}+\tilde{\lambda}^{k}_{1}\right]\times\left[\underline{\xi}_{k},\overline{\xi}_{k}\right].

Since
|\overline{\xi}_{k}-\underline{\xi}_{k}|
is at most
d\rho q_{k}
, by
choice of
\rho=\epsilon/d
,

Area(B_{k})=|\overline{\xi}_{k}-\underline{\xi}_{k}|\,\lambda^{k}_{1}\leqslant d\rho=\epsilon/3
.
This, together with the fact that by property
(R1)
of a

(C,\epsilon/3,\rho)
-rigid rectangle
presentation (see Definition
3.2
)
Area(R^{k}_{3}\cup\ldots\cup\tilde{R}^{k}_{d})<\epsilon/3
,
we get that
Area(X_{k})>1-\epsilon
.

We now claim that
X_{k}
is a
C
-rigidity set for the vertical flow
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}=(\varphi_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

on
S
. For all
points
x\in X_{k}
which have distance at least
\lambda_{1}^{k}
from the right
boundary of
\tilde{R}^{k}_{d}
, since
\Delta_{k}=q_{k}|\tilde{I}_{1}^{k}|\in C
, one has that

(5.17)

{\frac{c_{0}}{q_{k}}\leqslant d(\varphi_{q_{k}}(x),x)=\big|\tilde{I^{k}_{1}}\big|\leqslant\frac{c_{1}}{q_{k}}.}

using the BT dynamics of the vertical flow on
X_{k}
and the vertical identifications, as in the Example of BT rigidity with
d=2
rectangles in §
3.1.4
. For all points
x\in X_{k}
close
\lambda_{1}^{k}
-close the right boundary of
\tilde{R}^{k}_{d}
, but
below
the bad region
B_{k}
, (
5.17
) also holds trivially, since all adjacent rectangles are glued to each other below height
\underline{\xi}_{k}
.
To locate
\varphi_{q_{k}}(x)
for the initial points
x

above
the region
B_{k}
,
we note that due to the way
\tilde{R}^{k}
was constructed, one has to exploit the vertical glueings between the top parts of the rectangles
\tilde{R}^{k}_{3},\ldots,\tilde{R}^{k}_{d}
. One can then see that
\varphi_{q_{k}}(x)
has an
horizontal
displacement from
x
equal to
\lambda^{k}_{1}\geqslant 1/8q_{k}
(as in the other cases) and no vertical displacement,
so that (
5.17
) still holds.
Thus, (
5.17
) holds for every
x\in X_{k}
and since we have already
shown above that
Leb(S\setminus X_{k})<\epsilon
,
this concludes the proof that
(q_{k})_{k}
are all
(C,\epsilon)
-BT rigidity times for the
vertical flow
\varphi^{\mathbb{R}}
on
X
in the sense of Definition
3.1
.
∎

5.3.
Trimmed derivative linear bounds using Rauzy-Veech induction

To build good matching sets, we need (in addition to the BT rigid rectangle presentations produced in the previous §
3.1.6
) tools to guarantee that the matching orbit has linear bounds on trimmed derivatives (see condition
(G2)
of Definition
4.16
).
In
[
Ul11
]
(see also
[
FKU
]
) the last author, exploiting Rauzy-Veech induction, builds many orbit segments for which linear bounds on trimmed derivative bounds hold (see Definition
4.11
). These good orbit segments correspond to
Rohlin towers given by (special times of) Rauzy-Veech induction, in the following sense.

5.3.1.
Linear bounds on Rohlin towers given by RV induction.

Let us say that an orbit segment
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q)

goes once up
a Rohlin tower
\mathcal{T}
for
T
if
x\in J
where
J
is the base of
\mathcal{T}
and
q
is the height of
\mathcal{T}
.

Definition 5.2
.

Given
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
, we say that a Rohlin tower
\mathcal{T}
with base
J
and height
q

gives

B
-
linear bounds on trimmed derivatives
(for
f
) for some
B>0
if every orbit segment which goes once up
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
B
-linear bounds on trimmed derivatives, i.e.

\left|\widetilde{S}_{r}f^{\prime}(x)\right|\leqslant Br\ \ \text{for \ any}\ 0\leqslant r\leqslant q\ \text{and \ any}\ \ x\in J.

Given a Keane IET, if
\{I^{(n)},\ {n\in\mathbb{N}}\}
is the sequence of inducing intervals given by Rauzy-Veech induction, we denote by
(\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{j})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
the Rohlin towers over the continuity intervals
I^{(n)}_{j}
given by

\mathcal{T}^{(n)}_{j}:=\left\{I^{(n)}_{j},T(I^{(n)}_{j}),\dots,T^{h^{(n)}_{j}-1}I^{(n)}_{j}\right\},\qquad 1\leqslant j\leqslant d,

where
h^{(n)}_{j}
is the first return time of
I^{(n)}_{j}
to
I^{(n)}
. We will refer to these Rohlin towers are
Rohlin towers given by Rauzy-Veech induction
at time
n
.

The following Proposition is a technical
version of a result proved by the last author in
[
Ul11
]
(refined
20
20

20

While the content of Proposition is essentially the central result in the work
[
Ul11
]
of the second author, the precise form of the accelerating set, and in particular its product structure (which is important here, as well as in
[
FKU
]
, to combine these estimates with rigidity) was not stated in the proof in
[
Ul11
]
. The adaptations to the proof in
[
Ul11
]
that allow to show that the accelerating set has the desired product form appear in the Appendix
C
in
[
FKU
]
.

Rohlin towers given by Rauzy-Veech induction at times which corresponds to visits to the set
E
give linear bounds on trimmed derivatives for any function
f
in
SymLog^{2}(T)
.

Proposition 5.3

(Acceleration for bounded trimmed derivative sums, see Proposition 8.1 in
[
FKU
]
)
.

For any
d\geqslant 2
, any irreducible permutation
\pi
and any neat compact set
K=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A}\times\Theta_{A}
(see §
5.1.4
), there exists
Y\subset\Theta_{A}
such that the product set
E
given by

E:=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A}\times Y=\{(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\hat{X}\,|\,\,\lambda\in\Delta_{A},\,\tau\in Y\}

has
\hat{\mu}_{\hat{\mathcal{}}}(E)>0
, and
given any
f
in
\mathcal{P}SymLog(T)

there exists
B_{0}>0
so that, if

(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
is recurrent to
E

under
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}
and
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is the sequence of visits, i.e.

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{n_{\ell}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E\quad\textrm{for\ every}\ \ell\in\mathbb{N},

then for any
all the Rohlin towers
(\mathcal{T}^{(n_{\ell})}_{j})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
over the inducing intervals
I^{(n_{\ell})}_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
give
B_{0}
-linear bounds on trimmed derivatives.

5.3.2.
Persistence of linear bounds on large towers.

We will need also the following Lemma, that shows that
large
Rohlin towers obtained by cutting and stacking a bounded number of Rohlin towers which give linear bounds on trimmed derivatives, also give linear bounds on trimmed derivatives (up to changing the constant in the linear bound).

Lemma 5.4

(Persistence of linear bounds, Lemma 8.4 in
[
FKU
]
.)
.

Given
T
Keane and
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
, assume that the sequence
(n_{k})_{k}
is such that the Rohlin towers

\mathcal{T}^{(n_{k})}_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
, given by Rauzy-Veech induction at times in the subsequence
(n_{k})_{k}
give
B_{0}
-linear bounds on trimmed derivatives for
f
.

Assume furthermore that
(m_{k})_{k}
is obtained by
shifting

(n_{k})_{k}
, i.e. that there exists an integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

m_{k}:=n_{k}+N,\qquad\text{ for\ every}\ k\in\mathbb{N}.

Then if
(\mathcal{T}^{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of towers of the form
\mathcal{T}^{k}:=\mathcal{T}^{(m_{k})}_{j_{k}}
, where
j_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
are such that there exists
\nu>0
such that the tower
\mathcal{T}^{(m_{k})}_{j_{k}}
has measure

\left|\mathcal{T}^{(m_{k})}_{j_{k}}\right|=\lambda^{(m_{k})}_{j_{k}}h^{(m_{k})}_{j_{k}}\geqslant\nu>0\quad\text{for all}\quad k\in\mathbb{N},

then

f
has
B_{1}
-linear bounds on trimmed derivatives along
(\mathcal{T}^{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
for some constant
B_{1}>0
which depends only on
B_{0},N
and
\nu
.

The proof of this Lemma (which is a simple exercise of decomposition of Birkhoff sums) can be found
21
21

21

The statement of the Lemma is a specical case of Lemma 8.4, since, as remarked in
[
FKU
]
just after the statement, the first assumption (A1) of Lemma 8.4 holds automatically if
the product of the cocycle matrices
between time
n_{k}
and
m_{k}
has uniformly bounded norm independently on
k
, as assumed in Lemma
5.4
here.
in § 8.2.1 in
[
FKU
]
.

5.4.
Proof of existence of good matchings

In this subsection we prove Proposition
4.18
, finding times where BT rigid Rohlin towers (coming from a BT rigid rectangle presentations) give linear bounds on trimmed derivatives. The idea is again to produce this times using Rauzy-Veech induction, by considering RV times
(n_{\ell})_{\ell}
which correspond to visits to the product set
E
given by Proposition
5.3
, followed
by a visit at time
m_{\ell}=n_{\ell}+N
(after a finite number
N
of iterates) to the open set (given by Lemma
5.1
) which gives the desired
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid rectangle presentations. The persistence of the trimmed derivative bounds (given by Lemma
5.4
) is used to show that linear bounds on trimmed derivatives (which hold by construction at each time
n_{\ell}
) still hold at the shifted times

(m_{\ell})_{\ell}
for the matching orbit.

Proof of Proposition
4.18
.

Notice first that
it suffices to prove that for any given
L\in\mathbb{N}
there is a full measure set of IETs with
a standard permutation
\pi
, such that the conclusion is true, since
then we can intersect over
L\in\mathbb{N}
and still get a full measure set. Also, choosing sequence
\rho_{n}\to 0
, it suffices to prove that there is a full measure set of IETs for which the conclusion holds for a given
\rho\in(\rho_{n})_{n}
(since a
(C,\epsilon,\rho_{n})
-rigid presentation is also a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
rigid presentation for every
\rho>\rho_{n})
.
Let us hence fix
L\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\rho<1
.

We now split the proof in steps for clarity.

Step 1 (Definition of the return set)
:
Let us define a suitable neat compact set
K=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A}\times\Theta_{A}
(see §
5.1.4
for definitions and notation), to which we will apply Proposition
5.3
to get an acceleration set for
B_{0}
-linear bounds on trimmed derivatives. Let
\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}\subset\Theta_{\pi}
be the set given by (
5.10
) where
\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\rho}{4}
.Let
A^{-}:=A_{-m}\cdots A_{-1}
where
(A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the sequence associated as in (
5.3
) to a Birkhoff generic
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
with
\tau\in\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
, and

m
is chosen sufficiently large so that
\Theta_{A^{-}}\subset\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}\subset\Theta_{\pi}

(this is possible since
\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
is open and for a Birkhoff generic
\tau\in\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
, the cones
\Theta_{A_{-m}\cdots A_{-1}}
have diameter going to zero as
m
grows, see
[
MUY
]
or
[
Be
]
). Then set
A=A_{-n}\cdots A_{-1}
where
n
is chosen so that
n>m
, so that
A>0
can be written as
A=A^{\prime}A^{-}
(taking
A:=A_{-n}\cdots A_{-m-1}
), it is neat
22
22

22

This can be achieved for example by adding a block of length more than
n/2
of top (or bottom) Rauzy-Veech moves, see
[
AGY
]
.
and
\pi^{(-n)}=\pi
.
Consider now the (neat) compact set
\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A}\times\Theta_{A}
and let
Y\subset\Theta_{A}
and
B_{0}>0
be the set and the constant given by Proposition
5.3
.

For any
\epsilon>0
and
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
, consider now the open set
\mathcal{U}(C,\epsilon,\rho)
given by (the proof of) Lemma
5.1
, which has the product form
\mathcal{U}=\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}\times\Lambda^{\prime}
, where
\Lambda^{\prime}:=\Lambda_{c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}}
is given by (
5.9
) and
c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}
are chosen as in the proof of Lemma
5.1
. We now claim that the set
F=F(\rho,C,\epsilon)\subset\hat{X}_{d}
given by

(5.18)

F=F(\rho,C,\epsilon):=E\cap\mathcal{Z}^{-n}(\mathcal{U}(C,\epsilon,\rho)),\qquad\text{where}\ E:={\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A}\times Y},

has
\mu_{\widehat{Z}}(F)>0
. The proof of this will take the rest of this Step. Let us first consider
the set
{(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A}\times\Theta_{A})}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{-n}(\mathcal{U}(C,\epsilon,\rho))
, which contains
F
and is by construction open, and show that it is non-empty.
To see this, consider any
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
, where
\tau\in\Theta_{\pi}
and
\lambda
is such there
\lambda:=A\lambda^{\prime}/\|A\lambda^{\prime}\|
for some
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}
. Then, by construction

(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A}\times Y
(since
A\lambda^{\prime}/\|A\lambda^{\prime}\|\in\Delta_{A}
by definition (
5.9
) of
\Delta_{A}
). Moreover, since
A
was defined so that
A=A^{\prime}A^{-}
, by (
5.8
) we have that

\mathcal{V}^{n}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\mathcal{V}^{n}(\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{A^{\prime}A^{-}}\times\Theta_{\pi})=\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Theta_{A^{-}A^{\prime}}\subset\{\pi\}\times\Delta_{d}\times\Omega^{\prime},

where the last inequality follows since

\Theta_{A^{\prime}A^{-}}\subset\Theta_{A^{-}}
by (
5.7
) and

A^{-}
was defined so that
\Theta_{A^{-}}\subset\Omega^{\prime}
.
Since by definition the
\lambda
-coordinate of
\mathcal{V}^{n}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
belongs to
\Lambda^{\prime}
, we have that
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in E
also belongs to
\mathcal{V}^{-n}(\Lambda^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime})=\mathcal{V}^{-n}(\mathcal{U}(C,\epsilon,\rho))
, and hence that
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
belongs to
F
.
Remark that
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}
gives positive measure to open non-empty sets. Since
F
is contained in this open, non-empty set, and the Lebesgue measure of
Y
is positive, it follows that
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}}(F)>0
.

Step 2 (Definition of typical IETs and of return times):

Let us now define the set of full measure IETs which will satisfy the conclusion. By ergodicity of
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
, there exists a a set
\widehat{X}^{\ast}
such that
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\hat{X}\backslash\widehat{X}^{\ast})=0
,
such that any
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}^{\ast}
is Birkhoff generic, and thus, in particular its orbit under
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
visit any open, non empty set in
\widehat{X}^{\ast}
. Since the invariant measure
\mu_{\mathcal{V}}
on the IET space
X
is given by the pushforward
\mu_{\mathcal{Z}}=p_{*}\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{Z}}
and is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure on
X
, it follows that there exists a full measure set
X^{\ast}
of IET
T=(\pi,\lambda)
that have a
lift
, i.e. a triple
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
in the fiber
p^{-1}(\pi,\lambda)
of the projection map
p:\widehat{X}\to X
, which belongs to
\widehat{X}^{\ast}
.

For any non-empty
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
0<\epsilon<1
, since the set
F=F(C,\epsilon,\rho)
defined as in (
5.18
) has
\widehat{\mu}_{\mathcal{V}}(F)>0
by Step 1,
for any
(\pi,\lambda)\in X^{\ast}
, there exists a lift
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in\widehat{X}^{\ast}
and
an increasing sequence
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

(5.19)

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{n_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)\in F(C,\epsilon,\rho):=E\cap\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{-N}(\mathcal{U}(C,\epsilon,\rho)),\qquad\forall\ k\in\mathbb{N}.

Moreover, setting
(m_{k})_{k}
to be the
shifted sequence
given by
m_{k}:=n_{k}+n
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, by definition (
5.18
) of
F
as preimage, we also have

(5.20)

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{m_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau)=\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{n}(\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{m_{k}}(\pi,\lambda,\tau))\in\mathcal{U}(C,\epsilon,\rho),\qquad\forall\ k\in\mathbb{N}.

Step 3 (Shifted return times give BT rigid tower presentations)
:
We will now use the sequence
(m_{k})_{k}
of induction times defined in Step 2
to build the desired sequence of good matchings
(M_{n})_{n}
.
it gives a sequence of
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid (Rohlin) towers presentations for the IET
T=(\pi,\lambda)
in the sense of Definition
3.8
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, since by (
5.20
)
m_{k}
is a visit to the set
\mathcal{U}(C,\epsilon,\rho)
, by Lemma
5.1
, the translation surface
S
associated to the lift
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
of
T
chosen in Step 2
has a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid rectangle presentation

\mathcal{R}_{k}:=\mathcal{R}_{k}(S):=\{\tilde{R}^{k}_{1},\,\cdots,\tilde{R}^{k}_{d+1}\,;\,\sim_{\mathcal{R}_{k}}\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}

(see also the proof of Proposition
3.3
).

This sequence
(\mathcal{R}_{k})_{k}
of BT rigid
rectangles
presentations for
S
produce a sequence of BT rigid
towers
presentations

\mathcal{T}^{k}(T):=\{\mathcal{T}^{k}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, for
T
,
through the relation between rectangles and towers explained in §
3.2.3
.
For
1\leqslant i\leqslant d+1
, the base of the Rohlin tower
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{1}
is the base
\tilde{I}^{k}_{i}
of the rectangle
\tilde{R}^{k}_{i}
. We will denote by
\tilde{q}^{k}_{i}
the (integer) height of the tower
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{i}
and set

q_{k}:=\tilde{q}^{k}_{2}=\tilde{q}^{k}_{d+1}
to be the common height of
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}
.
Let
\Delta_{k}:=q_{k}|\tilde{I}_{1}^{k}|
. By Lemma
5.1
, we have that
c_{0}<\Delta_{k}<c_{1}
, so this gives that
q_{k}
is a
C
-BT rigidity time with
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
.

The
cramping
of the singularities of the rectangle presention
\mathcal{R}_{k}(S)
(condition
(iv)
of Definition
3.2
) induce
cramping
of
discontinuities of
T
(in the sense of
(v)
of Definition
3.8
); more precisely, if
0\leq\underline{\ell}_{k}<\overline{\ell}_{k}^{\prime}<\tilde{q}_{k}
are such that
\underline{\ell}_{k}
is the first iteration of
\tilde{I}^{k}
via
T
that sees a discontinuity (or in other words, this
\underline{\ell}_{k}
is the height of the lowest discontiinuity)
and
\overline{\ell}_{k}
is the last one before first return (or in other words, this
\underline{\ell}_{k}
is the height of the lowest discontinuity), then

(5.21)

\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}\leq\rho\cdot q_{k}.

Finally, we claim that for any point
x\in I\setminus X_{n}
,
and every
\beta\in Disc(T)
if
x_{\beta}^{+}
(respectively
x_{\beta}^{-}
) is the closest visit to
\beta
from the right (resp. left) in time
q
, and
(a^{+},b^{+})
(respectively
(a^{-},b^{-})
) is the level of
\mathcal{T}_{2}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{d+1}
) which contains
x_{\beta}^{+}(x_{\beta}^{-})
, then

(5.22)

\max\{\big||m^{+}_{\beta}(x,q_{k})-\beta|-|m^{+}_{\beta}(x,q_{k})-a^{+}|\big|,\big||m^{-}_{\beta}(x,q_{k})-\beta|-|m^{-}_{\beta}(x,q_{k})-b^{-}|\big|\}\leq|\tilde{I}^{k}_{3}|+\ldots+|\tilde{I}^{k}_{d}|.

This follows from
by the definition of
\Omega_{\rho_{0}}
given in the proof of Lemma
5.1
, since by considering the vertical orbits starting at the base of the zippered rectangles
R_{1}
,
R_{d}
as the return sets for the horizontal translation flow, we get that each horizontal orbit of such points crosses the zippered rectangles
\tilde{R}_{3},\ldots,\tilde{R}_{d}
at most once (for a detailed proof of this claim, we refer the reader to Proposition 4.1 in
[
BFT
]
). In view of the bound on
|\tilde{I}^{k}_{i}|
for
i=3,\ldots,d
and (
5.22
), the final condition follows.

Thus, this concludes the proof that the
towers
presentations

\mathcal{T}^{k}(T):=\{\mathcal{T}^{k}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}\}
are
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
