-BT rigid presentations (according to Definition
3.8
) for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Step 4. (Choice of reference orbit)
:
We now choose for every
k\in\mathbb{N}

a point
z_{k}\in I
which will serve as a reference point when constructing the matching orbit segments. We take for example
23
23

23

It is worth mentioning, that the point
z_{k}
can be chosen among many other points and, in particular, does not have to be in the base of a Rohlin tower. In the end, we are proving that the matching set is
large
, and the matching property is
transitive
. Hence every point from the final matching set would work as well.

z_{k}
to be the mid-point of the interval
\tilde{I}_{2}^{k}
, that is the base of the tower
\mathcal{T}_{2}
.
Let
q_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, be the common height of the ’large’ BT rigid towers
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}
constructed in Step 2.
The orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},q_{k})
of lenght
q_{k}
of the reference point
z_{k}
will be our reference orbit for matchings.

Step 5. (Large towers at shifted return times give linear trimmed derivative bounds)
: Consider any
f\in\mathcal{P}SymLog^{2}(T)
.
Since by its definition the recurrence set
F\subset E
(see (
6.46
) in Step 1)
and by Step 2
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a subsequence of the returns for
(\pi,\lambda,\tau)
to
E
, by Proposition
5.3
, there exists
B_{0}
such that all the Rohlin towers
\mathcal{T}^{(n_{k})}_{j}
with
1\leqslant j\leqslant d+1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
give

B_{0}
-linear bounds on trimmed derivatives for
f
.

Consider now the shifted return sequence
(m_{k})_{k}
and let
n
be the integer such that
m_{k}=n_{k}+n
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the ’large’ Rohlin towers
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}

in the Rohlin tower rigid representations
\mathcal{T}^{k}
build in Step 3 and remark that
Area(\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2})\geqslant 1/8
and
Area(\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1})\geqslant 1/8
for every
k
sufficiently large. Thus, by the persistence of linear
bounds Lemma
5.4
, there exists a constant
B_{1}>0
,
depending only on
B_{0}
,
n
and
\nu=1/8
, such that the Rohlin towers

(\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2})_{k}
and
(\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1})_{k}
all give
B_{1}
-linear bounds on
trimmed derivatives for
f
. In particular, since any subsegment

\mathcal{O}(z_{k},r)
of the orbit
\mathcal{O}(z_{k},q_{k})
crosses at most once
the Rohlin towers over
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}
(since
z_{k}
was chosen in Step 4 to be the midpoint of
\tilde{I}_{2}^{k}
), and
q_{k}=\tilde{q}_{k}^{2}=\tilde{q}_{k}^{d+1}
, we have that, for any
r\leqslant q_{k}
,

|S_{r}f^{\prime}(z_{k})|\leqslant|S_{r}f^{\prime}(z_{k})|\leqslant B_{1}\tilde{q}_{k}\text{ if}\ 0\leqslant r<q_{k},

so that
\mathcal{O}(z_{k},q_{k})
also satisfies
B_{1}
-linear bounds on trimmed Birkhoff sums for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Step 6. (Definining matching sets and matchings)
:
We will now define, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a subset
M_{k}:=M_{k}^{L}\subset\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2}\cup\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}
and show that it is a
L
-fold
(z_{k},q_{k})
-matching set. We set
M_{k}:=[0,1]\backslash X_{k}
where
X_{k}=X_{k}^{L}
is a region which we throw away since we cannot define matchings either already for the
orbits of length
q_{k}
, or in order to have an
L
-fold matching (see
Definition
4.5
):

(5.23)

X_{k}=X_{k}^{L}:=\left(\bigcup_{i\in\{3,\ldots,d\}}\bigcup_{j=-Lq_{k}}^{q^{k}_{i}-1}\tilde{I}^{k}_{i}\right)\ \cup\ \bigcup_{i=0}^{L-1}\left(\bigcup_{j=\underline{\ell}_{k}-iq_{k}}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-iq_{k}}T^{j}[\partial\tilde{I}^{k}_{d+1},\partial\tilde{I}^{k}_{d+1}+|I^{k}_{1}|]\right).

Notice that the first part of the union in (
5.23
) contains the ’small’ towers
\tilde{\tau}_{i}^{k}
over
\tilde{I}^{k}_{i}
for
i=3,4,5
, as well as the points that enter them in time at most
Lq_{k}
; the second part of the union in (
5.23
) contains the intervals which belong to the
bad zone

B_{k}
(which have width
|I_{1}^{k}|
and endpoints at height between
\underline{\ell}_{k}
and
\overline{\ell}_{k}
, which are the smallest and largest of the discontinuities heights) where one does not control BT rigidity (see the definition (
5.16
) of
B_{k}
in the proof of Proposition
3.3
in §
5.4
), as well points whose orbit enter the bad zone after a multiple
lq_{k}
with
1\leqslant l<L
(since throwing these points is needed to control
L
fold matchings). In particular, if
X_{k}^{1}
denotes the set (
5.23
),
X_{k}^{1}
is union of the small towers
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_{3}^{k}\cup\ldots\cup\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_{d}^{k}
and the bad zone
B_{k}
, we have that

(5.24)

\mathcal{O}(T^{jq_{k}}x,q_{k})\cap X_{k}^{1}=\emptyset,\quad\text{for}\ j=0,\dots,L-1.

We now illustrate that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
the set
M_{k}:=I\setminus X_{k}
is a
L
-fold
(z_{k},q_{k})
-matching set. We note that due to the way
\tilde{I}^{k}
was defined, the left-endpoints of the floors of the towers
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2}
over
\tilde{I}^{k}_{1}
and
\tilde{I}^{k}_{2}
do not contain any
discontinuity of
T
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{1}
,
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}
were cut from the Rohlin towers over the ’large’ subintervals of
I^{(n_{k})}
(see Figure
10
), we have the following identifications:

(5.25)

\displaystyle T^{i}(\tilde{I}^{k}_{1})

\displaystyle\text{ is glued to the right-hand side of
}T^{q_{k}-q_{k}^{\prime}+i}(\tilde{I}^{k}_{d+1})\text{ for }i=0,\ldots q_{k}^{\prime}-1,

(5.26)

\displaystyle T^{q_{k}^{\prime}+i}(\tilde{I}^{k}_{2})

\displaystyle\text{ is glued to the right-hand side of
}T^{i}(\tilde{I}^{k}_{d+1})\text{ for }i=0,\ldots q_{k}-q_{k}^{\prime}-1,

where
q_{k}^{\prime}
is the height of the tower
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{k}
.
A crucial consequence of these identifications is that one can verify that

(5.27)

\max_{x\in M_{k}}|T^{q_{k}}(x)-x|<\frac{1}{q_{k}},

which shows that
\{q_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are BT rigidity times for points in
M_{k}
.

From these observations, we claim that it follows that given any
x\in I\setminus X_{k}
,
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{k})
is matched to the reference orbit segment
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},q_{k})
, so that
M_{k}
is a
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},q_{k})
-matching set. The choice of the matching intervals depends on the initial
position of
x
in
M_{k}
and exploits BT rigidity times and vertical identifications. We
illustrate all possible matchings in Figure
11
. There are six possible types of matchings, corresponding to six possible regions of
M_{k}
in which
x
can be located (these correspond to the six subpictures in Figure
11
; in each the initial location of
x
is in the shaded region in that pictures). In color in the figures of Figure
11
, we illustrate the matching intervals which match
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{k})
to
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},q_{k})
;
different colors in the figure correspond to matching intervals of different lengths (so that in a
k
-matchings there are
k
colors). One can check that all
M_{k}
are
\kappa
-
(z_{k},q_{k})
matchings with
\kappa=3
(since in all subfigures of Figure
11
at most
3
-colors, i.e. three different lengths of matching intervals, are used).

Figure 11.
All six possible types of matchings
between the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{k})
of the point
x\in M_{k}
and the reference orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},q_{k})
. The red points denote the discontinuities of
T
, while
the blue and green segments on the vertical sides are identified due to the sides rectangles (and hence Rohlin towers) being identified. In each subfigure, the shaded region denotes the region of possible locations of
x
in which the illustrated type of matching applies.
Colored areas denote the location of matching intervals. Each color denote a set of matching intervals of the same length, thus showing that each matching is a
3
-matching (
3
colors only).

As an explict example, we illustrate the first subfigure that is the case when
x\in T^{j}(\tilde{I}^{k}_{2})
for some

j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}\}
. Under this assumption, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{k})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{k}-j)\sqcup\mathcal{O}_{T}(T^{q_{k}-(j-p_{k})}x,j),

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},q_{k})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},j)\sqcup O_{T}(T^{j}z_{k},q_{k}-(j))),

and match the orbit segment
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{k}-j)
with
\mathcal{O}_{T}(T^{j}z_{k},q_{k}-j))
(via the intervals in the green area in the top left subfigure of Figure
11
), while
\mathcal{O}_{T}(T^{q_{k}-j}x,j)
is matched with
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},j)
(via the intervals in the brown colored region of the same top left subfigure of Figure
11
),.

To conclude the proof that
M_{k}
, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, is an
L
-fold matching set (see Definition
4.5
), we now need to match to the reference orbit
\mathcal{O}(z_{k},q_{k})
also to all other orbits segments of length
q_{k}

obtaining subdividing the orbit
\mathcal{O}(x,Lq_{k})
of
x\in I\setminus X_{k}^{L}
of length
Lq_{k}
into
L
pieces, namely
\mathcal{O}(T^{jq_{k}}x,q_{k})
for
j=1,\cdots,L-1
. The definition of
X_{k}=X_{k}^{L}
guarantees that for every such
j
, the
point
T^{jq_{k}}x
belongs to
I\backslash X_{k}^{1}
(i.e. their orbits of length
q_{k}
avoid the small rectangles and the bad set
B_{1}
), see (
5.24
)), and hence allows to apply to each
\mathcal{O}(T^{jq_{k}}x,q_{k})
exactly the same
matching arguments.

Step 7. Verifying the balanced matchings properties:

Let us show that the matching sets
M_{k}=I\setminus X_{k}
are
1/5
-balanced matchings for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, (see Definition
4.6
). Thus, we have to prove that properties
(B1)
to
(B4)
of Definition
4.6
hold for
c:=1/5
. We will then show at the end of this step that the same proof also gives that they are indeed
L
-fold
1/5
-balanced matching sets.

To see that (B1) holds, it is more convenient to look at the dynamics or orbits inside the Rohlin towers over the intervals
I^{(n_{k})}_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
(before the cut and stack corresponding to inducing on the interval
\tilde{I}^{k}
). The orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},q_{k})
(since
q_{k}=q^{(n_{k})}_{1}+q^{(n_{k})}_{d}
and
z_{k}\in I^{(n_{k})}_{1}
) goes once through the Rohlin tower over
I_{1}^{(n_{k})}
, and once through the tower over the interval
I_{d}^{(n_{k})}
. Moreover, since the relative displacement
\Delta_{k}<1/10
(by assumptions on
C
), we have that the point
T^{q_{1}^{(n_{k})}}(z_{k})
lies in the middle half of the interval
I_{d}^{(n_{k})}
, while
T^{q_{1}^{(n_{k})}+q_{5}^{(n_{k})}}(z_{k})
lies in the middle half of the interval
I_{1}^{(n_{k})}
. Hence every point in the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},q_{k})
has distance at least
\frac{1}{4}|I_{5}^{(n_{k})}|
from the endpoints of levels of towers, which contain in particular the set
Disc(T)
. It remains to notice that for
k
large enough we have by (
5.14
) that
|I_{d}^{(n_{k})}|>\frac{9}{10q_{k}}
.

The proof of (B2) goes along the similar lines as the proof of (B1), by noticing that if a point does not belong to a level of tower which has a discontinuity as its endpoint then its distance from any element of
Disc(T)
is at least the length of one level of one of the bigger towers. On the other hand, the property (B3) follows from the fact that no matching interval is longer than the length of the
\tilde{I}^{k}
, as seen in Figure
11
.

Let us now prove (B4). For a given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, assume that
x\in M_{k}
. If

x\notin\bigcup_{j=-q_{k}}^{\tilde{q}_{1}^{k}-1}(\tilde{I}_{1}^{k})
, then the orbit of

x
of length
q_{k}
is fully contained in the Rokhlin tower

\left(\bigcup_{j=0}^{q^{k}-1}T^{j}(\tilde{I}^{k}_{2}\cup\tilde{I}^{k}_{d+1})\right)
and
each point is on a different floor. Thus
(B4)
follows from the fact that
\Delta_{k}\in C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
.
If on the other hand
x\in\bigcup_{j=-q_{k}}^{\tilde{q}_{1}^{k}-1}(\tilde{I}_{1}^{k})
, then
T^{\ell}x\in\tilde{I}_{1}^{k}
for some

\ell\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}\}
if and only if
T^{\ell}x\notin\tilde{I}_{2}^{k}\cup\tilde{I}_{d+1}^{k}
. Thus, again by
\Delta\in C
, we get that (B4)
is satisfied for every
x\in M_{k}
.

Step 8. Verifying the good matchings properties:

We now show that
(M_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of
(\gamma,B)
-good matchings for exponential tails for
f
, in the sense of Definition
4.16
, for
\gamma:=(L+1)\cdot{\rho}\cdot c_{1}+\epsilon
(as claimed in the statement of Proposition
4.18
) and a suitable
B>0
. We then have to verify properties (G0) to (G3) of Definition
4.16
.

To prove (G0), we have to estimate the measure of
[0,1]\backslash M_{k}=[0,1]\backslash([0,1]\backslash X_{k})=X_{k}
, see (
5.23
).
While the measure of the first union in the definition (
5.23
) of
X_{k}
convergences to 0 as
k\to\infty
(since
\epsilon_{k}=1/k\to 0
), the second part of the union in (
5.23
) has non-trivial, although small, measure. More precisely, in view of the control on
\tau
entries in terms of
\rho
given by
(
5.21
), we have

(5.28)

\limsup_{k\to+\infty}Leb(X_{k})\leq(L+1)\cdot{\rho}\cdot c_{1}+\epsilon.

This shows that (G0) holds with
\gamma:=(L+1)\cdot{\rho}\cdot c_{1}+\epsilon
up to replacing
(M_{k})_{k}
with a subsequence if necessary.

The proof that (G1) holds, namely that
(M_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are
c
-good for
c=1/5
, was given in Step 7.
In Step 5 we proved that the reference orbit

\mathcal{O}_{T}(z_{k},q_{k})
satisfies
B_{1}
-linear bounds for every
k
for a uniform constant
B_{1}>0
by using that
(n_{k})_{k}
are visit times to the set
E
of Proposition
5.3
(and the persistence Lemma
5.4
); thus (G2) holds taking as constant the constant
B_{1}
(in the place of
B_{0}
in (G2)).

Finally, by Lemma
4.19
, there exists
B>0
(which depends only on
B_{1}
) such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, any orbit
\mathcal{O}(x,q_{k})
of
x\in M_{k}
satisfy
B
-linear bounds on trimmed derivatives, namely (G3) of Definition
4.16
holds.
This concludes the proof that
(M_{k})_{k}
are

(\gamma,B)
-good matching sets for exponential tails.

The
proof that
(M_{k})_{k}
are also

(\gamma,B)
-good
L
-
fold
matching sets is similar. Indeed, the arguments in Step 7 (giving
1/5
-balance) as well as the arguments in this Step, can be applied to all other orbit segments
\mathcal{O}(T^{jq_{k}}x,q_{k})
for
j=1,\ldots,L-1
in which each orbit
\mathcal{O}(x,Lq_{k})
with
x\in M_{k}
is subdivided. As in Step 6, the definition of
X_{k}:=X^{L}_{k}
guarantees that each of the initial points
T^{jq_{k}}x
stays in
X_{k}^{1}
, see (
5.24
), and allows to reason in exactly the same way to verify the required properties.
∎

6.
Tail estimates and disjointness

In this section we will prove Theorem
1.1
, namely disjointness for
rescalings. To do so, we apply the disjointness criterion given by Proposition
2.11
.
The key technical result of this section is that some
tail sets
, namely sets of points which lie in the
tail
of the limit distribution of Birkhoff sums (at multiples of) rigid times with exponential tails,
can be
distinguished
, namely have different measures (see §
6.1
for more details).

We first describe, in §
6.1
, the strategy of proof and the idea in a special, simplified setting.
As we explain in §
6.4
(see the beginning of the proof of Theorem
1.1
), it suffices to prove that the flows
\varphi^{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varphi^{K}_{\mathbb{R}}
are disjoint for all pairs of positive integers
K,L\in\mathbb{N}
.
To keep the notation simple and clarify the main ideas of the proof, we will first prove the main estimate (Proposition
6.1
) with full details in the special case in which we consider
\varphi^{K}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varphi^{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
with
K=1
and
L=2
(see §
6.2
).
We will then indicate the modifications one needs to do for the general case in §
6.3
.
Finally, the final arguments to get the proof of Theorem
1.1
are presented in §
6.4
.

Assumption
: To simplify the exposition of the proof, we assume throughout this section and the next that, WLOG,
f
is normalized so that the constant
C_{f}
given by Definition
2.1
is
C_{f}=1
, so that

(6.1)

\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{-}=1=\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{+}.

Notice also that this implies that all the constants
C_{i}^{\pm}
are in
[0,1]
.

6.1.
Strategy of the proof.

Let us start by giving in this section an overview of the strategy of the proof and the key idea behind the key tail estimate in a simplified setting.

6.1.1.
Tail sets.

In view of the Criterion
2.6
, if
(q_{k})_{k}
is a sequence of rigidity times with exponential tails, we need to
find a set for which the limit distributions of the (centralized) Birkhoff sums
of
f
of length
q_{n}
, namely
S_{q_{k}}f-a_{k}
for a suitable
a_{k}
, and
S_{2q_{k}}f-2a_{k}
are going to differ, i.e. can be
distinguished
. More precisely,
we will find a sequence
(t_{k})_{k}
of real numbers, converging to some
t\in\mathbb{R}
, such that, if we define the tail sets

A_{k}:=\{x\in[0,1)|\,\,S_{q_{k}}f-a_{k}>t_{k}\},\quad\text{and}\quad B_{k}:=\{x\in[0,1)|\,\,S_{2q_{k}}f-2a_{k}>2t_{k}\},\quad k\in\mathbb{N},

then
Leb(B_{k})-Leb(A_{k})>c>0
.
Here,
q_{n}
are
will be the heights of Rauzy-Veech towers obtained by Rauzy-Veech
algorithm. More precisely, we will use a sequence of BT rigidity times and the corresponding good sets for exponential tails produced in Section
5.4
and
q_{k}
will be the common height of the
large
towers
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{2}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{d+1}
of the BT-rigid presentation. The constant
c
providing the lower bound will be proportional to the displacement given by BT-rigidity.
We call the set
A_{k}
the
single tail
set (since it corresponds to the Birkhoff sums for a
single
iteration of the rigidity time
q_{k}
only); we call he set
B_{k}
the
double tail
set (since it corresponds to the Birkhoff sums for a
double
iteration of the rigidity time
q_{k}
, namely for
S_{2q_{k}}f
). The control of the Birkhoff sums distributions that we prove, will not be obtained on the whole interval, but rather considering only a sequence of good matching sets
(M_{k})_{k}
. A main difficulty to overcome, is the fact that the measure of the set
reminder
set
X_{k}=[0,1)\setminus M_{k}
is also proportional to the displacement. This makes the required estimates delicate; however we are able to show that the constant of proportionality can be made much smaller for the set
X_{k}
, thus making the assumptions of the disjointness criterion given by Propostiion
2.6
hold.

In the proof we will show that the double tail set
B_{k}
contains a substantial part of
A_{k}
, but also an additional part, so that
B_{k}\backslash A_{k}
has larger measure than
A_{k}\backslash B_{k}
. Thus,

Leb(B_{k})-Leb(A_{k})=Leb(B_{k}\backslash A_{k})-Leb(A_{k}\backslash B_{k})>0,

and prove a lower bound for the measure of this difference.
To estimate the measures of these tail sets, we first show that the Birkhoff sums
S_{q_{k}}f
and
S_{2q_{k}}f
both blow up
monotonically
near the endpoints of the floors of the Rohlin towers (see Step 3 in the proof of Proposition
6.1
in §
6.2
), so that the tail sets
A_{k}
and
B_{k}
can both be described by a union of intervals which contain these floors
endpoints
where the Birkhoff sums blow up. We will call these intervals
tail intervals
, or more precisely
single tail intervals
when they belong to
A_{k}
, and
double tail intervals
when they belong to
B_{k}
. Single (resp. double) tail intervals occur at near the
endpoints
of the floors, due to closest visits to a singularity from the
left
(in which case they happen near the right endpoint of an interval of the tower
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2}
), or due to closest visits to a singularity from the
right
(in which case they happen near the left endpoint of an interval of the tower
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}
). We call these tail intervals respectively
left
and
right
tail intervals).

A picture of the double tail and single tail sets is shown in Figure
12
, which shows that the sets both consists of intervals that are either to the right of a floor of the tower
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{2}
or to the left of a floor of the tower
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{d+1}
and hence travel close to the singularities (that all belong to the boundary of the ’small towers’
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{3},\cdots\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{d}
).
The
tail intervals
belonging to different floors of the same tower (either
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{2}
or
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{d+1}
) turn out to all have approximately the same measure (see Steps 3 to 8 of the proof of Proposition
6.1
), so let us focus in the next §
6.1.2
on one right interval and one left interval and describe the single tail and double tail restricted to these two intervals.

6.1.2.
The idea of the key estimate in the simplified setting.

Let
(a,b)
be one of the floors of the
large
tower
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_{2}
. For simplicity let it be the floor such that
b
is one of the singularities.
Let us compare the single and double tail inside
(a,b)
, namely
A_{k}\cap(a,b)
and
B_{k}\cap(a,b)
. One can first show that if
x
is sufficiently close to
b
,
S_{q_{k}}f(x)
is monotonically increasing (see Step 1 of the proof of Proposition
6.1
). Thus, both sets are intervals with
b
as an endpoint. Furthermore, since for any
x\in(a,b)
,
T^{q_{k}}x
is to the left of
x
, by monotonicity
S_{q_{k}}f(T^{q_{k}}x)<S_{q_{k}}f(x)
, so that

S_{2q_{k}}f(x)=S_{q_{k}}f(x)+S_{q_{k}}f(T^{q_{k}}x)<2S_{q_{k}}f(x).

From this, one can see that if
x\in B_{k}
, then
x\in A_{k}
(since
S_{2q_{k}}f(x)>2a_{k}+2t_{k}
implies that
S_{q_{k}}f(x)>a_{k}+t_{k}
), so that
B_{k}\cap(a,b)\subset A_{k}\cap(a,b)
i.e. in this floor (as in other floors of this tower
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{2}
), the double tail set is contained in the single tail set.

We now want to estimate the measure of the difference
A_{k}\backslash B_{k}\cap(a,b)
.
By monotonicity,
B_{k}\cap(a,b)
is an interval of the form
(v_{k},b)
where the point
v_{k}
where ’the double tail starts’ is defined by
S_{2q_{k}}f(v_{k})-2a_{k}=2t_{k}
. It will be chosen, so that
|b-v_{k}|\approx\tfrac{\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}
, where
\Delta_{k}=q_{k}\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{k}
is the relative shift given by
T^{q_{k}}
. Moreover,
x\in(a,b)\backslash B_{k}
is in

A_{k}\backslash B_{k}\cap(a,b)
exactly when
2S_{q_{k}}f(x)\geq 2t_{k}+2a_{k}=S_{2q_{k}}f(v_{k})
, namely when

2S_{q_{k}}f(x)-S_{2q_{k}}f(v_{k})\geq 0
.
One can show (exploiting the bounds on trimmed Birkhoff sums of the derivative) that the main order of
S_{q_{k}}f(x)
for
x
close to
b
is given by the contribution of the
closest visits
to singularities. Furthermore, since singularities in a BT rigid presentation are all
cramped
close to each other (see Figure
12
, in which singularities are indicated by red dots) and by assuming that
C_{f}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{-}=1
, the effect of the
d
singularities is (up to a negligible error, computed in Step 4 of the proof of Proposition
6.1
) the same than the contribution of the closest taking the constant
1
, namely
-\log(b-x)
. Similarly,
S_{2q_{k}}f(v_{k})
can be approximated by the contribution the closest visit for
S_{q_{k}}f(v_{k})
(which gives a contribution
-\log(b-v_{k})
) and of the closest visit of
S_{q_{k}}f(T^{q_{k}}v_{k})
(which is of order
(\log(b-T^{q_{k}}v_{k})
).
Thus, the difference
2S_{q_{k}}f(x)-S_{2q_{k}}f(v_{k})
, when
x=v_{k}-\tfrac{1}{2}(b-v_{k})
(i.e. halfway between
v_{k}
and
T^{q_{k}}(v_{k})
), is approximately

-2\log(b-x)+\left(\log(b-T^{q_{k}}v_{k})+\log(b-v_{k})\right)=-2\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)+\log(2)<0.

By continuity, if we consider a point
x
in
(v_{k},b)

sufficiently close to
v_{k}
, we continue to have that
2S_{q_{k}}f(x)-S_{2q_{k}}f(v_{k})<0
, so that
x\notin A_{k}
.
More precisely,
x
can be chosen so that
|b-x|=\frac{\alpha}{q_{k}}e^{-D}
, with
\alpha\in(\sqrt{2},\frac{3}{2})
(in the main proof that follows we take
\alpha=\frac{10}{7}
). This shows that strictly less than a third of
A_{k}\cap(a,b)
does
not
belong to
B_{k}
.

Similarly, one can then treat also the left intervals of the tails, namely those that correspond to orbits come close to the cramped singularities from the left. Since again one can show that they all have approximately the same size (see Step 7 and 8 of the proof of Prop.
6.1
), let us here only consider the floor
(\hat{a},\hat{b})
which has a singularity
\hat{a}
as (left) endpoint.
Again, we assume that
x
is sufficiently close to
\hat{a}
, so that
S_{q_{k}}f(x)
is monotone and we can approximate its values with the contribution of the closest visits, which are all cramped and contribute as the closest. We first show that both the single and the double right tail on this side too are union of intervals. In particular, we show that

B_{k}\cap(\hat{a},\hat{b})=(\hat{a},\hat{v}_{k})
where
\hat{v}_{k}
is defined by
S_{2q_{k}}f(\hat{v}_{k})-2a_{k}=2t_{k}
. To show that any
x\in(c,\hat{v}_{k})
is also in
B_{k}
, since
T^{q_{k}}x
is (as before) to the
left
of
x
(and hence now moves
in direction
on the singularities rather than away from them) we have to discuss more cases, according to whether
T^{q_{k}}x
is still to the right of
c
(in which case one can use a simple monotonicity argument, analogous to the one on the left side), or if it is to the left of
c
(and hence visits the singularities also from the left). In this case one can still use matching and monotonicity to show that
(\hat{a},\hat{v}_{k})\subset B_{k}
(see Step 8 of the proof of Prop.
6.1
for details).

A key difference on this side is that now that the single tail interval
A_{k}\cap(\hat{a},\hat{b})
in
(\hat{a},\hat{b})
is
contained
in the double tail interval
(\hat{a},\hat{v}_{k})=B_{k}\cap(\hat{a},\hat{b})
(see Figure
12
). Indeed, while
T^{q_{k}}x<x
as
as before , now

S_{q_{k}}f(x)
is decreasing; therefore, if we assume for example that
T^{q_{k}}(x)
is still in
(\hat{a},\hat{b})
,

S_{q_{k}}f(T^{q_{k}}x)>S_{q_{k}}f(x)
and

S_{2q_{k}}f(x)=S_{q_{k}}f(x)+S_{q_{k}}f(T^{q_{k}}x)>2S_{q_{k}}f(x),

so if
S_{q_{k}}f(T^{q_{k}}x)-a_{k}>t_{k}/2
(i.e.
x\in A_{k}
), we also have that
S_{2q_{k}}f(x)-2a_{k}>t_{k}
(i.e.
x\in B_{k}
).

Exploiting convexity as well as the symmetry of the function
f
(which plays here an essential role, since it allows for a fine control of the constants involved in the estimates), one can then prove, similarly as before, that if
\hat{v}_{k}
is the right endpoint of the right double tail
(\hat{a},\hat{b})\cap B_{k}
, all the points
x
such that
\hat{v}_{k}-\frac{\Delta_{k}}{2q_{k}}\leq x<\hat{v}_{k}
are contained in
(\hat{a},\hat{b})\cap B_{k}\backslash A_{k}
. Notice that (essentially for a convexity effect) this is an interval of size roughly
\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}
(i.e.
\frac{7}{6}
for
\alpha=\frac{10}{7}
) of the size of the interval
(a,b)\cap A_{k}\backslash B_{k}
.
Since
7/6>1
this gives an heuristic explanation of
why we can expect

Leb\left((\hat{a},\hat{b})\cap B_{k}\backslash A_{k}\right)>Leb\left((a,b)\cap A_{k}\backslash B_{k}\right)

and how to provide an estimate from below for its measure. Since all the other tail intervals whose orbits get close to
all
cramped singularities, from the bottom one to the top one, in one
run
(as defined in Step 2 of the proof of Prop.
6.1
) give contributions to the single and double tails of comparable sizes to the tail intervals in
(a,b)
and
(\hat{a},\hat{b})
(see Steps 5 to 8 of the proof of Prop.
6.1
). We consider separately the tail intervals which are contained in floors close to the cramped singularities, whose orbits travels close to some but not all of them (we call these
incomplete runs
). These provide a contribution of comparable, but negligible order (in the sense that it is proportional to the measure of the union of all the other tail intervals, but with a small proportionality constant; see Step 9 of the proof of Prop.
6.1
for details). Adding up the contribution of all left and right tail intervals (corresponding to complete and incomplete runs)
we then show that
Leb(B_{k}\backslash A_{k})-Leb(A_{k}\backslash B_{k})>0
, and furthermore give a lower bound for the measure of the difference.

The proof of Proposition
6.1
that we present in the next section §
6.2
follows the lines of this simplified sketch, taking into account the contribution of the error terms due to the simplifications and approximations made above.

Figure 12.
An illustration of the tail sets considered in
proof of Theorem
1.1
.
Single
tail intervals are in color:

right
single tail intervals in red,
left
ones in yellow; those coming from incomplete visits are in light blue.

Double
tail intervals are shaded with patterns:
left
double tail intervals are dotted, right ones are shaded with waves and the right double tail intervals which come from incomplete visits have an octagonal pattern. One sees that the single tail
contains
the double tail intervals (dotted) on the right, while on the left the double tails intervals (with waves) contain the (yellow) single tails intervals.

6.2.
Distinguishing simple and double tails

The following proposition shows that properly chosen tails can be distinguished when
K=1
and
L=2
.

Proposition 6.1

(Distinguishing tails when
K=1,L=2
)
.

For a sufficiently small
\rho>0
, for any
B>0
,
there exists

\epsilon>0
and a non-empty
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

such that
for any sequence
(M_{k})_{k}
of
(z_{k},q_{k})-
matchings for
T
and
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
given by a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid presentation which are
(\gamma,B)
-good for

\gamma<3\rho c_{1}+\epsilon
,
there exists a
bounded
sequence
(t_{k})_{k}

such that if, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the tail sets:

A_{k}:=\{x\in M_{k}\mid S_{q_{k}}(f)(x)-a_{k}\geq t_{k}\},\quad\text{and}\quad B_{k}:=\{x\in M_{k}\mid S_{2q_{k}}(f)(x)-a_{k}\geq 2t_{k}\},

then we have that
Leb(B_{k})-Leb(A_{k})\geqslant\gamma
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

The proof is divided into steps, which will hopefully help to follow the strategy.

Step 0: Choice of parameters.

Let us fix any
0<\rho\leqslant 10^{-6}
. If
C_{i}
,
i\in\{0,\cdots,d-1\}
are the constants from Definition
2.1
, let

(6.2)

C_{min}:=\min\{C_{i}^{\pm}>0\mid p=0,\ldots,d-1\}.

For the parameter
B>0
given by the Definition
5.2
, consider a number
D\in\mathbb{R}
such that:

(6.3)

D\geq 10^{6}\cdot\min\{1,B\}\ \text{ and }\ Be^{-D}\leq\min\{\tfrac{1}{20}\log(\tfrac{50}{49}),C_{min}\cdot e^{-2B}\}.

(The parameter
D
will be used to define the size of the tail, see
Step 1 in the proof of Prop.
6.1
). Set

\epsilon:=10^{-6}e^{-2D-B}.

We then define the BT-rigidity interval
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
in terms of
D
and the
auxiliary constant
\delta>0
to be

c_{0}:=(1-\delta)e^{-D},\quad c_{1}:=e^{-D},\qquad\text{where}\ \delta=e^{Be^{-D}}-1.

Step 1: Definition of the tail sets.

Let
(M_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of
(\gamma,B)
-good
(z_{k},q_{k})
-matching sets given by
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid presentations.
Since by assumption
\gamma<3\rho c_{1}+\epsilon
, we have that

(6.4)

\gamma\leqslant 3\cdot 10^{-6}e^{-D}+10^{-6}e^{-2D-B}<4\cdot 10^{-6}e^{-D}.

We will denote by
{{\mathcal{T}}}^{k}_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
the towers of the
k^{th}
BT rigid presentation (used to define
M_{k}
).
We define the centering sequence
(a_{k})
using the Birkhoff sums of the matching point
z_{k}
for
M_{k}
as a reference point, namely setting:

a_{k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{q_{k}-1}f(T^{i}(y_{k})),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

To choose the tail parameters
(t_{k})_{k}
(which will be used to define the tail sets), we consider for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
points
v_{k}^{\pm}
in the base whose orbits

\mathcal{O}(v_{k}^{\pm},2q_{k})
of lenght
2q_{k}
have distance from singularities respectively from the right and the left of order
e^{-D}/q_{k}
(where
D
is the constant defined above). More precisely,
v^{-}_{k}\in(a,b):=\tilde{I}_{2}^{k}
belongs to the base of the (large) tower
\tilde{I}_{2}^{k}
and
v^{+}_{k}\in\tilde{I}_{d+1}^{k}

belongs to the base of the (large) tower
\tilde{I}_{d+1}^{k}
, that we will denote
(\hat{a},\hat{b}):=\tilde{I}_{d+1}^{k}
, and are implicitely defined by
24
24

24

Notice that
T^{q_{k}}v_{k}^{\pm}=v^{\pm}_{k}-\Delta_{k}/q_{k}<v^{\pm}_{k}
. Therefore, the closests visits from the right within
\mathcal{O}(v_{k}^{-},2q_{k})
is contained in the first half of the orbit, namely in

\mathcal{O}(v_{k}^{-},q_{k})
, while the closest visits from the left in
\mathcal{O}(v_{k}^{+},2q_{k})
is contained in the second half of the orbit, namely

\mathcal{O}(T^{q_{k}}v_{k}^{+},q_{k})
. This is the reason why we consider the distance from
\hat{c}
of the point
T^{q_{k}}v_{k}^{+}
.

b-v_{k}^{-}=\frac{e^{-D}}{q_{k}},\quad\text{and}\quad T^{q_{k}}(v_{k}^{+})-\hat{a}=\frac{e^{-D}}{q_{k}}.

We then compare the Birkhoff sums
S_{2q_{k}}(f)
at these two points and let
v_{k}\in\{v_{k}^{+},v_{k}^{-}\}
be the point with the smallest one, so that

(6.5)

S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})=\min\{S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}^{+}),S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}^{-})\}.

We can then define the tail parameters
(t_{k})
using the Birkhoff sums of
v_{k}
, by setting

(6.6)

t_{k}:=\frac{S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})}{2}-a_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\left(S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-2a_{k}\right),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Consider now, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
single tail set

A_{k}:=\{x\in M_{k}\mid S_{q_{k}}(f)(x)-a_{k}\geq t_{k}\}

and the
double tail
set

B_{k}:=\{x\in M_{k}\mid S_{2q_{k}}(f)(x)-2a_{k}\geq 2t_{k}\}.

Note that the tail sets are defined considering only points in the matching set

M_{k}=I\setminus X_{k}
.
We remark also, for later use, that, by the definitions of the tail sets and
v_{k}
, so that (
6.5
) holds,

(6.7)

\text{if}\ v_{k}=v_{k}^{-},\ \text{then}\,v_{k}^{+}\in B_{k},\quad\text{while}\quad\text{if}\ v_{k}=v_{k}^{+},\ \text{then}\,v_{k}^{-}\in B_{k}.

Let us now show that, as claimed, the sequence
(t_{k})_{k}
giving the thresholds of the tail sets
A_{k}
and
B_{k}
is
bounded
(as claimed as part of the statement we are proving). To show this, let us bound the difference of Birkhoff sums
S_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-S_{q_{k}}(f)(z_{k})
by estimating separately the difference of the trimmed parts
\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-{\tilde{S}}_{q_{k}}(f)(z_{k})
(using mean value theorem and (
4.5
)) and the contribution of the closest visits (recalling that their contributions is positive, that the closest distance is bounded below by
e^{-D}
by construction, and using that
\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{f}^{+}=1
). We then get the estimate

(6.8)

0\leq S_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-a_{k}=S_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-S_{q_{k}}(f)(z_{k})\leqslant\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(z_{k})-\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}{C^{+}_{i}}\log{m(v_{k},q_{k})}\leq B+D.

One can then show that
0\leqslant t_{k}\leqslant S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-2a_{k}\leqslant 2(B+D)
for every
k
, so that
(t_{k})_{k}
is bounded.

Step 2: Tail intervals location.

Let us first identify the points
x\in M_{n}
such that the orbit
\mathcal{O}(x,q_{n})
gets close to
each
of the cramped singularities in sequence (from the lowest to the heighest), at the same distance. Let

\underline{\ell}_{k}
and
\overline{\ell}_{k}
in
\{0,1,\cdots,q_{k}-1\}
be the heights of (the floor which contains) respectively the
lowest
and the
heighest
of the cramped singuarities (see also the definition in Step 3 of Proposition
4.18
). Let
(a_{0},b_{0})=T^{\underline{\ell}_{k}}\tilde{I}^{k}_{2}=T^{\underline{\ell}_{k}}(a,b)
be the floor of the tower
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{k}_{2}
at the height of the lowest cramped singularity. One can then consider the intervals
25
25

25

One can show that
T^{-i}
on
(a_{0},b_{0})
is continuous for
0\leqslant i\leqslant q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})
and hence the preimages
T^{-j}(a_{0},b_{0})
considered in (
6.9
) are indeed intervals. One can indeed verify that, by the tower representation dynamics, none of the (cramped) singularities is hit by an iterate
T^{i}(a_{0},b_{0})
until
i=q_{k}-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})
.
given by

(6.9)

(a_{j},b_{j}):=T^{-j}(a_{0},b_{0}),\quad\text{for}\ j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})\}.

These intervals contain points whose orbit up to time
q_{k}
gets close to the
all
cramped singularities
from the left
, in order from the lowest to the heights (we will refer to this as a
run
of closests visits from the left). We will show that these intervals contain the
left
(single and double) tail intervals, which have as endpoints the
b_{j}
s.
Similarly, to describe the part of the tail sets which correspond to the closest visits
from the right side
, let
(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})=T^{\underline{\ell}_{k}}\tilde{I}^{k}_{d+1}=T^{\underline{\ell}_{k}}(\hat{a},\hat{b})
and consider the following preimages, that one can also show (as in footnote
25
) are all intervals:

(6.10)

(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j}):=T^{-j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0}),\quad\text{for}\ j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})\}.

These intervals contain points whose
forward
orbit up to time
q_{k}
gets close to the
all
cramped singularities
from the right
in a
run
(from the lowest to the heighest). We will show that they contain the
right
(single and double) tail intervals, which have the
\hat{a}_{j}
s as left endpoints.

Remark that if
x\in T^{i}(\hat{a},\hat{b})\backslash X_{k}
for
0\leqslant i\leqslant\overline{\ell}-\underline{\ell}
(i.e. belongs to the floors of
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d+1}
which
contain
the bad zone
X_{k}
but is not it), then
\mathcal{O}(x,q_{k})
will also have (as any orbit) a closest visit to each of the singularities, but it will not contain a full
run
of visits (from the lowest to the heighest) with the same approximte distance (up to a small error estimated in Step 4). We then say that this orbit (or the corresponding Birkhoff sum
S_{q_{k}}f(x)
) has an
incomplete run
of closest visits. These points can still belong to the (single or double) tail sets (in Figure
12
these points are denoted in light blue or hexagonal pattern).

Step 3: Monotonicity near the endpoints.

We now claim that the function
S_{q_{k}}(f)
is monotone, close to the endpoints of the intervals defined in Step 1 (more precisely, increasing in
(a_{j},b_{j})
sufficiently close to
b_{j}
and decreasing in
(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
sufficiently close to
\hat{a}_{j}
.
More precisely, since (from the form of
f
in Definition
2.1
) visits from the left of a discontinuity give positive contributions to
f^{\prime}
and visits to the right negative ones,
since
B
is chosen in accordance to Definition
5.2
, for points in
y\in(a_{j},b_{j})
which are sufficiently close to
b_{j}
(which get close to singularities
from the left
in time
q_{k}
),
we can estimate

S_{q_{k}}(f^{\prime})(y)\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{C_{i}^{-}}{m^{-}_{i}(y,q_{k})}+\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f^{\prime})(y)\geqslant\frac{1}{|b_{j}-y|}-Bq_{k}

so that, if
|b_{j}-y|<1/Bq_{k}
, the
S_{q_{k}}f
, and similarly
S_{2q_{k}}f
, are increasing. Similary,
for a point
y\in(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
sufficiently close to
\hat{a}_{j}
, we can estimate

S_{q_{k}}(f^{\prime})(y)\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\frac{C_{i}^{+}}{m^{-}_{i}(y,q_{k})}+\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f^{\prime})(y)\leqslant\frac{1}{|\hat{a}_{j}-y|}-Bq_{k}

so that, if
|\hat{a}_{j}-y|<1/Bq_{k}
, then
S_{q_{k}}f
is decreasing. Similarly one can show that also
S_{2q_{k}}f
is increasing in
(a_{j},b_{j})
sufficiently close to
b_{j}
and descreasing in
(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
sufficiently close to
\hat{a}_{j}
as long as the distance from the endpoints is not greater than
1/2B
.

The tail thresholds chosen in Step 1 automatically guarantee that all the tail sets that we will describe in the next Steps are contained in these monotonicity intervals near enpoints.
(Indeed, we will always work with points that are at most
{e^{-D+2B}}/{q_{k}}
far from either the endpoint
b_{j}
of
(a_{j},b_{j})
or the
\hat{a}_{j}
for right tail intervals), and by definition
D\geqslant 10^{6}\cdot{\min\{1,B\}}
, so that one has that
e^{-D+2B}\leqslant 1/2B
. This guarantees that the
A_{k}\cap(a_{j},b_{j})
and
B_{k}\cap(a_{j},b_{j})
are both
intervals
with
b_{j}
as endpoint and similarly that the
A_{k}\cap(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
and
B_{k}\cap(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
are
intervals
with
\hat{a}_{j}
as endpoint.

Step 4: Errors in closest visits approximation.

For a given
j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})\}
, the distance from the actual discontinuities of the orbit of a points
y
in
(a_{j},b_{j})
or
(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})

is not
|b_{j}-y|
or
|y-\hat{a}_{j}|
, since the intervals
\tilde{I}_{3}^{k},\ldots,\tilde{I}_{d}^{k}
have positive lengths. These distances may differ by up to
|\tilde{I}^{k}_{3}|+\ldots+|\tilde{I}^{k}_{d}|
, see (
5.22
). However, by the definition of
BT
-rigid configuration, for
k
large enough we have

|\tilde{I}_{3}^{k}|+\ldots+|\tilde{I}_{d}^{k}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{q_{k}}.

Let
j=0,\ldots,q_{k}-1
and
y\in(a_{j},b_{j})
with
|b_{j}-y|\geq\frac{e^{-D-B}}{q_{k}}
. Remembering that
\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}C_{i}^{+}=1
, we have

(6.11)

\begin{split}-\log(b_{j}-y)&+\sum_{p=1,\ldots,d-1}C_{p}\log({m_{p}^{-}(y,q_{k})})=\sum_{p=1,\ldots,d-1}C_{p}\left(\log\frac{m_{p}^{-}(y,q_{k})}{b_{j}-y}\right)\\
&\leq\log\left(\frac{b_{j}-y+\frac{\epsilon}{q_{k}}}{b_{j}-y}\right)=\log\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{q_{k}(b_{j}-a)}\right)\leq\log(1+\frac{\epsilon}{e^{-D-B}})\leq 10^{-6}e^{-D},\end{split}

where the last inequality follows from the choice of
\epsilon
.
We define

\xi_{k}^{j}:=\sup_{y\in\left(a_{j},b_{j}-\frac{e^{-D-B}}{q_{k}}\right)}-\log(b_{j}-y)+\sum_{p=1,\ldots,d-1}C_{p}\log({m_{p}^{-}(y,q_{k})}).

Analogously, for the right-hand side, we define

\hat{\xi}_{k}^{j}:=\sup_{y\in\left(\hat{a}_{j}+\frac{e^{-D-B}}{q_{k}},\hat{b}_{j}\right)}-\log(\hat{a}_{j}-y)+\sum_{p=1,\ldots,4}C_{p}\log({m_{p}^{+}(y,q_{k})})

and by proceeding analogously as in (
6.11
) we get that for any
k
large enough, we have

(6.12)

\xi_{k}:=\max_{j=0,\ldots,q_{k}-1}\{\xi_{k}^{j},\hat{\xi}_{k}^{j}\}<10^{-6}e^{-D}.

Step 5: Estimate of the left double tail intervals.

From now on we assume that
v_{k}=v_{k}^{-}
. If the point
v_{k}
defined in Step 1 had been
v_{k}^{+}

we would have right tail intervals that are larger than the left tail intervals (i.e.
b-v_{k}>\hat{v}_{k}-\hat{a}
) and the monotonicity arguments and computations done in the following Steps would remain analogous, with the role of the left-hand side tails and right-hand side tails swapped.

Let us describe the set
B_{k}\cap\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-1}(a_{j},b_{j})
, where
(a_{j},b_{j}):=T^{-j}(a_{0},b_{0})
are the sets defined in Step 2 (dotted in Figure
12
). We will show that
B_{k}\cap(a_{j},b_{j})

are intervals roughly the same size (which correspond to the points whose closest
visit is on the left-hand side of the singularities) namely of the intervals
of the form
(v^{j}_{k},b_{j})
.
For each
j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}-1\}
consider the interval and let

z^{j}_{k}\in
be the starting point of the tail, namely the unique point (which exists by monotonicity) such that

(6.13)

S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v^{j}_{k})-2a_{k}=S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-2a_{k}:=2t_{k},

and let us define

(6.14)

D^{j}_{k}:=-\log(b_{j}-v^{j}_{k})-\log(q_{k})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad b-v^{j}_{k}=\frac{e^{-D^{j}_{k}}}{q_{k}}.

By the choice of
v^{j}_{k}
and monotonicity (Step 3), we then have the inclusions

(6.15)

[v_{k}^{j},b_{j})\subset B_{k}\text{ for every }j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})\}.

We now want to compare
D^{j}_{k}
and
D
to show that all left double tail intervals
[v_{k}^{j},b_{j})
, which by definition have size
{e^{-D^{j}_{k}}}/{q_{k}}
, have roughly the same size, comparable to
{e^{-D}}/{q_{k}}
. We claim that we have a first have a comparison that comes from the choice of
B
(see Definition
5.2
) and (
6.12
), which is

(6.16)

D-2B\leq D^{j}_{k}\leq D+2B.

Indeed, otherwise, if for example
D^{j}_{k}<D-2B
, then by Mean Value Theorem we would have

\begin{split}S_{2q_{k}}&(f)(v_{k}^{j})-S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})\\
&<(D-2B)-D-\log(e^{-D+2B}+\Delta_{k})+\log(e^{-D}+\Delta_{k})+\tilde{S}_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})+4\xi_{k}\\
&<-{2}B+4\xi_{k}<0,\end{split}

which contradicts the choice of
v_{k}^{j}
. One proves analogously that
D^{j}_{k}>D+2B
cannot hold either.
Moreover, we have

(6.17)

\begin{split}0=S_{2q_{k}}(f)(z^{j}_{k})-S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})&=\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(z^{j}_{k})+\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}z^{j}_{k})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}v_{k})\\
&-\log(e^{-D^{j}_{k}}+\Delta_{k})+\log(e^{-D}+\Delta_{k})+D^{j}_{k}-D+\xi_{k},\end{split}

where
\xi_{k}
is defined in (
6.12
). By the choice of
B
, the fact that
M_{n}
is a good matching set and Mean Value Theorem we get

\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(z^{j}_{k})+\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}z^{j}_{k})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}v_{k})\leq 4Be^{-D+2B}.

Note that the expressions
-\log(e^{-D^{j}_{k}}+\Delta_{k})+\log(e^{-D}+\Delta_{k})

and
D^{j}_{k}-D
are of the same sign. Hence, by (
6.17
) and
(
6.12
), we get

(6.18)

|D^{j}_{k}-D|\leq 4Be^{-D+2B}+|\xi_{k}|\leq 5B{e^{-D+2B}}.

Equation (
6.18
), in view of the definition (
6.14
) of
D_{k}^{j}
, shows that the part of the double tail inside a left tail interval
(a_{j},b_{j})
, namely
(a_{j},b_{j})\cap B_{k}
, is an interval
(z_{j}^{k},b_{j})
whose length
|b_{j}-z_{j}^{k}|
are the same up to a multiplicative constants.

Step 6: Estimate of the left single tail intervals.
We will now show that the single tail from the left (in red in Figure
12
) satisfies

(6.19)

(a_{j},b_{j})\cap A_{k}\subset\left(b_{j}-\frac{10}{7}(b_{j}-v_{k}^{j}),b_{j}\right),\qquad\forall\ 0\leqslant j<q_{k}.

This is a crucial step to show that the tail
A_{k}
is sufficiently smaller than
B_{k}
.
Let
j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}-1\}
and let
(a_{j},b_{j})
,
v_{k}^{j}
and
D_{k}^{j}
be as above. We will show that the point

(6.20)

y_{k}^{j}:=b_{j}-\frac{10}{7}(b_{j}-v_{k}^{j})=b_{j}-\frac{10}{7}\cdot\frac{e^{-D_{k}^{j}}}{q_{k}}

does not belong to
A_{k}
. By monotonicity of the tail (Step 1) this suffices to get the desired inclusion (
6.19
). Note that by (
6.18
) we have

(6.21)

|y_{k}^{j}-v_{k}^{j}|,\ |T^{q_{k}}y_{k}^{j}-T^{q_{k}}v_{k}^{j}|<\frac{e^{-D}}{q_{k}}.

We want

(6.22)

S_{q_{k}}(f)(y_{k}^{j})-a_{k}\leq\frac{1}{2}(S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}^{j})-2a_{k})=\frac{1}{2}(S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-2a_{k}),

or, in other words we need to show that

(6.23)

2S_{q_{k}}(f)(y_{k}^{j})\leq S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}^{j}).

By (
6.21
) and Mean Value Theorem, we have

|\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(y_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}^{j})|<Be^{-D}\text{ and }|\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(y_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}v_{k}^{j})|<Be^{-D}.

Recall that in view of (
6.12
), we have
\xi_{k}<10^{-6}e^{-D}<Be^{-D}
.
Since by the choice of recurrence set we have
(1-\delta)e^{-D}<\Delta_{k}<e^{-D}
(recall that
\delta<e^{Be^{-D}}-1<2Be^{-D}
), again by Mean Value Theorem (applied to
\log
) we obtain

|\log(e^{-D}+\Delta_{k})-\log(e^{-D}+e^{-D})|<\frac{\delta e^{-D}}{e^{-D}}<2Be^{-D}.

Hence we get

\begin{split}2S_{q_{k}}&(f)(y_{k}^{j})-S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}^{j})\\
\leq&2\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(y_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}v_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}^{j})-2\log({\tfrac{10}{7}e^{-D_{k}^{j}}})+\log(e^{-D}+\Delta_{k})+\log(e^{-D})+4\xi_{k}\\
\leq&2Be^{-D}+2\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(y_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}v_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}^{j})-2\log({\tfrac{10}{7}e^{-D_{k}^{j}}})+\log(e^{-D}+e^{-D})+\log(e^{-D})+4\xi_{k}\\
\leq&8Be^{-D}-2\log({\tfrac{10}{7}e^{-D-Be^{-D}}})+\log(e^{-D}+e^{-D})+\log(e^{-D})\\
=&10Be^{-D}-\log({\tfrac{100}{49}})+\log(2)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\log(\tfrac{50}{49})<0,\end{split}

where the penultimate inequality follows from the choice of
D
in Step 0. This shows that
y_{k}^{j}
does not belong to
A_{k}
, and hence, by monotonicity (namely Step 3), concludes the proof of Step 6.

Step 7: Description of the right double tail intervals.

We now describe the part of the
double
tail, which corresponds to closest visits from the right (which is shaded by waves in Figure
12
). Let
\hat{v}_{k}\in(\hat{a},\hat{b})
be the point in the base
(\hat{a},\hat{b}):=\tilde{I}_{d+1}^{k}
of the tower
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_{d+1}^{k}
such that

(6.24)

S_{2q_{k}}(f)(\hat{v}_{k})=2t_{k}+2a_{k}=S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})

and let us define
\hat{D}
so that

(6.25)

\hat{D}=-\log q_{k}(\hat{a}-T^{q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\hat{a}-T^{q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}=\frac{e^{-\hat{D}}}{q_{k}}.

Since we are under the assumption that
v_{k}=v_{k}^{-}
(see Step 1), and by monotonicity (Step 2) we have that

(6.26)

\hat{D}\leq D\Leftrightarrow e^{-\hat{D}}\geq e^{-D},

namely the point
T^{q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}
(which is chosen to have distance
e^{-\hat{D}}/q_{k}
from the closest singularity of the run) is
further
from the singularities than
v_{k}
(which has distance
e^{-D}/q_{k}
).

Consider now the intervals
(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j}):=T^{-j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})
defined in Step 2, which contain the right tail intervals.
Similarly as before, let
\hat{v}^{j}_{k}\in(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
be such that

(6.27)

S_{2q_{k}}(f)(\hat{v}^{j}_{k})=2t_{k}+2a_{k}=S_{2q_{k}}(f)(\hat{v}_{k})=S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k}),

and (analogously to the definition (
6.14
) of
D^{j}_{k}
) let us then define:

(6.28)

\hat{D}^{j}_{k}:=-\log(T^{q_{k}}\hat{v}^{j}_{k}-\hat{a}_{j})-\log(q_{k})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad T^{q_{k}}\hat{v}^{j}_{k}-\hat{a}_{j}=\frac{e^{-\hat{D}^{j}_{k}}}{q_{k}}.

Similarly as in (
6.16
), we have

(6.29)

\hat{D}-2B\leq\hat{D}_{k}^{j}\leq\hat{D}+2B.

Moreover, in view of (
6.24
), by proceeding similarly as in
(
6.18
), we get that

(6.30)

|\hat{D}^{j}_{k}-\hat{D}|\leq 5B{e^{-D+2B}}.

We now claim that we have

(6.31)

\left[\hat{a}_{k},\hat{v}_{k}^{j}\right)\cap M_{k}\subset B_{k}\ \text{
for every }j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})\},\\

i.e. also the right double tail consists of intervals.
26
26

26

Notice that here we cannot use directly a monotonicity argument, since the Birkhoff sum
S_{2q_{k}}f
is not necessarily continuous on
(\hat{a}_{k},\hat{b_{k}})
, since
T^{i}((\hat{a}_{k},\hat{b_{k}}))
can hit the cramped discontinuities for some
q_{k}\leqslant i\leqslant 2q_{k}
. It turns out that one can still use monotonicity arguments, but one has to split the Birkhoff sums
S_{2q_{k}}f(x)
in two
halves
, namely
S_{q_{k}}f(x)
and
S_{q_{k}}f(T^{q_{k}}(x))
, and use different arguments and matchings on each part.

Indeed, fix
0\leqslant j\leqslant q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})
and consider
x\in\left[\hat{a}_{k},\hat{v}_{k}^{j}\right)\subset[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
.
We consider three separate cases (illustrated in Figure
13
), according to the relative position of the iterate
T^{q_{k}}(x)
with respect to the singularities.
27
27

27

Remark that
T^{q_{k}}(x)=x-q_{k}\Delta_{k}<x
; if
T^{q_{k}}(x)>\hat{a}_{j}
(Case
1
), then
\mathcal{O}(T^{q_{k}}(x),q_{k})
is still to the right of the cramped singularities); otherwise,
T^{q_{k}}(x)
belongs to the floor at the same height of
(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
, but on the other
side
of the singularities, namely
(a_{j},b_{j})
. In this case
\mathcal{O}(T^{q_{k}}(x),q_{k})
is still to the right of the cramped singularities (see Cases 2 and 3).

Figure 13.
Illustration of the three Cases considered in Step 7 of the proof. Vertical (coloured) lines represent orbit segments and arrows indicate which orbit segments are matched and compared using different monotonicity arguments. The top figure illustrates
Case 1
, the next (middle) figure illustrates
Case 2
. The last figure (bottom) illustrates
Case 3
.

Case 1 (second run also from the right)
. Assume first that
T^{q_{k}}(x)
still belongs to
(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
. Then one can show that
x\in B_{k}
by exploiting the
monotonicity of
S_{q_{k}}(f)
and direct comparison of
S_{q_{k}}(f)(x)
with

S_{q_{k}}(f)(\hat{v}_{k}^{j})
and
S_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}x)
with

S_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}^{j})
, which gives that

S_{2q_{k}}(f)(x)=S_{q_{k}}(f)(x)+S_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}(x))>S_{q_{k}}(f)(\hat{v}_{k}^{j})+S_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}(\hat{v}_{k}^{j}))=S_{2q_{k}}(f)(\hat{v}_{k}^{j})

(see Figure
13
top configuration, which shows that the two parts of the Birkhoff sum (in black) can be
slided
to the left to match the other two parts (in green).

Assume now that
T^{q_{k}}(v_{k}^{j})\notin(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
. Then, since
x\in M_{k}
(so that it cannot belong to the small towers
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{3}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{T}^{k}_{d}
) it has to belong to the floor of
\mathcal{T}^{k}_{2}
at the same height than
(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
, namely
(a_{j},b_{j})
. We consider two subcases (Case 2 and Case 3).

Case 2 (second run to the left, but in the double tail)
.
If

\hat{a}_{j}\leq x\leq\hat{v}_{k}^{j}
and
v_{k}^{j}\leq T^{q_{k}}x\leq b_{j}
, where
v_{k}^{j}\in({a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
is the starting point of the double tail. Then,
by two different monotonicity arguments, namely since
x<\hat{v}^{j}_{k}
and
S_{q_{k}}(f)
is decreasing in
(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
, while
T^{q_{k}}x>{v}^{j}_{k}
and
S_{q_{k}}(f)
is increasing in
({a}_{j},{b}_{j})
(refer to Figure
13
, middle), we get:

\begin{split}&S_{q_{k}}(f)(x)\geq S_{q_{k}}(f)({\hat{v}_{k}^{j}})\geq\frac{1}{2}(S_{2q_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-2a_{k}),\\
&S_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}x)\geq S_{q_{k}}f(v_{k}^{j})\geq\frac{1}{2}(S_{2q_{k}}f(v_{k})-2a_{k}).\end{split}

Hence, adding these two estimates up, we proved that
x\in B_{k}
in this case as well.

Case 3 (second run to the left and outside of the double tail)
. If we are neither in Case 1, nor in Case 2, that is
x
does not satisfy any of the considered conditions, then we claim that either

|x-b_{j}|\leq\frac{12Be^{-D+2B}\cdot e^{-D}}{q_{k}},\quad\text{or}\quad|T^{q_{k}}x-b_{j}|\leq\frac{12Be^{-D+2B}\cdot e^{-D}}{q_{k}}.

This means that in this case, the orbit has to pass very close to singularities (as illustrated in the last picture in Figure
13
, bottom). To formally prove the claim, notice that, by construction we have

\Delta_{k}\in C=[c_{0},c_{1}]
,
by (
6.18
) and (
6.30
), we have

(6.32)

\max\{|\Delta_{k}-e^{-D}|,|\Delta_{k}-e^{-D_{k}^{j}}|\}\leq 6Be^{-D+2B}\cdot e^{-D}.

Assume that
|x-b_{j}|\leq 12Be^{-D+2B}\cdot e^{-D}\cdot q_{k}^{-1}
, the other case is considered analogously. Since
b_{j}
is a preimage of a discontinuity, the closest visit of the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(x,q_{k})
has distance at most
{(12Be^{-D+2B}\cdot e^{-D})}/{q_{k}}
.
Thus, by using the
B
-linear trimmed derivative bounds (see Definition
5.2
) and separating the closest visit from the remaining trimmed sum, we get
S_{q_{k}}(f)(x)-a_{k}\geq 2D-3B
. Note also, that the closest visit of the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{T}(T^{q_{k}}(x),q_{k})
is at most
{(12Be^{-D+2B}\cdot e^{-D}+\Delta_{k})}/{q_{k}}
. Hence
S_{q_{k}}(f)(x)-a_{k}\geq D-3B
. Thus, by the choice of
D
, we have

(6.33)

S_{2q_{k}}(f)(x)-2a_{k}\geq 3D-6B>2D+2B,

which by (
6.8
) implies that
x\in B_{k}
and concludes the proof of the claim and of this Step in this case.

Step 8: Estimate of the right single tail intervals.

We now describe the second part of the single tail, which corresponds to the

right
-hand side visits. We claim that

(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})\cap A_{k}\subset\left(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{y}_{k}^{j}\right),\quad\text{for}\ j\in\{0,\cdots,q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}-\underline{\ell})\},

where
\hat{y}_{k}^{j}\in(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})
is a point satisfying

(6.34)

|\hat{y}_{k}^{j}-\hat{a}_{j}|=\Big|\hat{v}_{k}^{j}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}-\hat{a}_{j}\Big|(1+10Be^{-D}).

We will show that
\hat{y}_{k}^{j}
does
not
belong to
A_{k}
. By (
6.12
), Jensen’s inequality and Mean Value Theorem we have

\begin{split}2S_{q_{k}}&(\hat{y}_{k}^{j})-S_{2q_{k}}(\hat{v}_{k}^{j})\\
&\leq 2\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(\hat{y}_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(\hat{v}_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}^{j})\\
&\quad-2\log(e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot{\Delta_{k}})-2\log(1+10Be^{-D})+\log(e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{j}})+\log(e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{j}}+\Delta_{k})+4\xi_{k}\\
&\leq 2\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(\hat{y}_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{-2q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}^{j})-\tilde{S}_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{-q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}^{j})-2\log(1+10Be^{-D})+4\xi_{k}\\
&\leq 6Be^{-D}-10Be^{-D}\leq 0\end{split}

Hence (since
\hat{y}_{k}^{j}
does not belong to
A_{k}
) we have the inclusion

\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})\cap A_{k}\subset\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}T^{-q_{k}}\left(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{y}_{k}^{j}\right).

By combining this with Step 6, we get that

(6.35)

A_{k}\cap\left(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}(a_{j},b_{j})\cup\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})\right)\subseteq\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}\left(y_{k}^{j^{\prime}},\hat{y}_{k}^{j}\right),

where
j^{\prime}=q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})-j
.

Step 9: Contribution to the tails of orbits with incomplete runs.

Let us now prove an
upper
bound on the measure of the set
A_{k}
and
lower
bound for the set
B_{k}
, one needs to consider also points which only make
incomplete
runs.

In addition to the contribution from points whose orbits contain a full
run
(see Step 2)
The initial points of these orbits belong to the sets

\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}T^{j}(a_{0},b_{0})\ \text{ and}\ {\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}T^{j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0}).}

Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}\}
and let
(a,b):=T^{j}(a_{0},b_{0})
. In view of (
6.23
), we have that
y_{k}^{0}=b_{0}-\frac{\frac{10}{7}e^{-D_{k}^{0}}}{q_{k}}\notin A_{k}
. We claim that

S_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{j}y_{k}^{0})-S_{q_{k}}(f)(y_{k}^{0})<0
,
and thus

(6.36)

A_{k}\cap T^{j}(a_{0},b_{0})\subset(T^{j}y_{k}^{0},T^{j}b_{0}).

Indeed, if
r\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}
is such that for
i\in\{0,\ldots,r\}
, we have
m_{i}^{-}(T^{j}y_{k}^{0},q_{k})=m_{i}^{-}(y_{k}^{0},q_{k})+\frac{\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}
, then, since
\Delta_{k}\in C
and in view of the trimmed derivative
B
-bounds (see Definition
5.2
), we get

\begin{split}S_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{j}y_{k}^{0})-S_{q_{k}}(f)(y_{k}^{0})&\leq B\Delta_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}C_{i}^{-}\log(m_{p}^{-}(T^{j}y_{k}^{0},q_{k}))+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}C_{i}^{-}\log(m_{p}^{-}(y_{k}^{0},q_{k}))\\
&\leq Be^{-D}+\sum_{i=0,\ldots,r}C_{i}^{-}\log\left(\frac{\frac{10}{7}e^{-D_{k}^{0}}}{\frac{10}{7}e^{-D_{k}^{0}}+\Delta_{k}}\right)\\
&\leq Be^{-D}+C_{min}\cdot\log\left(\frac{\frac{10}{7}e^{-D_{k}^{0}}}{\frac{16}{7}e^{-D_{k}^{0}}}\right)<0,\end{split}

where the penultimate inequality follows from the choice of
D
, the definition (
6.2
) of
C_{min}
and (
6.32
).

In a similar fashion, let us now prove that for
j=\{1,\dots,(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})\}
we have the inclusion

(6.37)

A_{k}\cap T^{j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})\subset\left(T^{j}\hat{a}_{0},T^{j}\hat{y}_{k}^{0}\right).

To see this, let again
r\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}
be such that for
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, we have
m_{i}^{+}(T^{j}\hat{y}_{k}^{0},q_{k})=m_{i}^{+}(\hat{y}_{k}^{0},q_{k})+\frac{\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}
. Using (
6.16
), we have

\begin{split}S_{q_{k}}(f)(T^{j}\hat{y}_{k}^{0})&-S_{q_{k}}(f)(\hat{y}_{k}^{0})\leq Be^{-D}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{+}\log(m_{p}^{+}(T^{j}\hat{y}_{k}^{0},q_{k}))+\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{i}^{+}\log(m_{p}^{+}(\hat{y}_{k}^{0},q_{k}))\\
&\leq Be^{-D}+\sum_{i=1,\ldots,r}C_{i}^{+}\log\left(\frac{(e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{0}}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta_{k})(1+10Be^{-D})}{(e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{0}}+\frac{3}{2}\Delta_{k})(1+10Be^{-D})+\Delta_{k}}\right)\\
&\leq Be^{-D}+C_{min}\ \log\left(\frac{e^{-D+2B}+\frac{3}{2}e^{-D}}{e^{-D+2B}+2e^{-D}}\right)\\
&=Be^{-D}+C_{min}\ \log\left(1-\frac{1}{e^{2B}+4}\right)\leq Be^{-D}-C_{min}\ e^{-2B}<0,\end{split}

which shows (
6.37
).

Finally, we estimate the contribution of incomplete runs to the double tail is also non-trivial, to show that it is comparable with the contribution to the single tail. More precisely, we show that for
\hat{v}_{k}^{0}
with
|T^{q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}^{0}-\hat{a}_{0}|=\frac{e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{0}}}{q_{k}}
and

j\in\{1,\ldots,(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})\}
we have

(6.38)

(T^{j}\hat{a}_{0},T^{j}(\hat{v}_{k}^{0}))\cap M_{k}\subset B_{k}.

To prove (
6.38
), for a fixed
j\in\{1,\ldots,(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})\}
, we need to show that
S_{2q_{k}}(f)(T^{j}\hat{v}_{k}^{0}))\geq S_{2q_{k}}(f)(\hat{v}_{k}^{0})
, since the point
\hat{v}_{k}^{0}
marks the right-hand side endpoint of the tail
B_{k}
(see Step 7). Let
r\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}
be such that for
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, we have
m_{i}^{+}(T^{j}(\hat{v}_{k}^{0}),q_{k})=m_{i}^{+}(T^{2q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}^{0},q_{k})-\frac{2\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}
. Note that the rest of closest visits cancels out i.e. the points which are the closest visit or the second closest visit to a singularity in the orbits
\mathcal{O}(T^{j}(T^{-q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}^{0}),2q_{k})
and
\mathcal{O}(T^{-2q_{k}}\hat{v}_{k}^{0},2q_{k})
are the same, except the
r
points pointed above. Thus, by using again the
B
-linear trimmed derivative bounds (see Definition
5.2
), we get

\begin{split}S_{2q_{k}}(f)(T^{j}({\hat{v}_{k}^{0}})&-S_{2q_{k}}(f)({\hat{v}_{k}^{0}})\\
&\geq-2Be^{-D}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{p}^{+}\log(m_{p}^{-}(T^{j}(\hat{v}_{k}^{0}),q_{k}))+\sum_{i=1}^{d}C_{p}^{+}\log(m_{p}^{-}(\hat{v}_{k}^{0},q_{k}))\\
&\geq-2Be^{-D}+\sum_{i=1,\ldots,r}C_{p}^{+}\log\left(\frac{e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{0}}+\Delta_{k}}{e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{0}}-\Delta_{k}}\right).\end{split}

Thus, recalling the definition of
C_{min}
in (
6.2
), we have

\begin{split}S_{2q_{k}}(f)(T^{j}({\hat{v}_{k}^{0}}))&-S_{2q_{k}}(f)({\hat{v}_{k}^{0}})\geqslant\\
&\geq-2Be^{-D}+C_{min}\cdot\log\left(\frac{e^{-D+2B}+e^{-D}}{e^{-D+2B}-e^{-D}}\right).\\
&=-2Be^{-D}+C_{min}\ \cdot\log\left(1+\frac{2}{e^{2B}-1}\right)\geq-2Be^{-D}+C_{min}\,\cdot e^{-2B}>0,\end{split}

which shows (
6.38
).

We finish this Step by estimating from above the difference in measures of the single and double tails, on the set where the points realize the incomplete visits. By combining (
6.36
), (
6.37
) and (
6.38
), taking into account (
5.21
), we get

(6.39)

Leb\left((A_{k}\setminus B_{k})\cap\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}T^{j}(a_{0},b_{0})\quad\cup\quad{\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}}T^{j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})\right)\right)\leq(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})\cdot\frac{3e^{-D}}{q_{k}}\leq 3\rho e^{-D}.

Step 10: Putting together all estimates.

Let us now combine the estimates from the previous Steps (Step 5 to Step 9), to estimate
Leb(B_{k})-Leb(A_{k})
.
We divide the comparison into two parts, one corresponds to
complete
runs, which were studied in Steps 5 to 8, while the other comes from the
incomplete
runs, with which we dealt in Step 9. First we deal with the latter. As a direct consequence of (
6.39
) we get

(6.40)

\begin{split}\Bigg|Leb\,&\Bigg(B_{k}\cap\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}T^{j}(a_{0},b_{0})\cup{\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}}T^{j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})\Bigg)\Bigg)\\
&-Leb\,\Bigg(A_{k}\cap\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}T^{j}(a_{0},b_{0})\cup{\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}}T^{j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})\Bigg)\Bigg)\Bigg|\\
&\leq Leb\,\Bigg((A_{k}\cap B_{k})\cap\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}T^{j}(a_{0},b_{0})\cup{\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}}T^{j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})\Bigg)\Bigg)\leq 3\rho e^{-D}.\end{split}

Now we go back to the contribution of the orbits which undergo complete runs. In view of (
6.31
) and (
6.35
) we get

(6.41)

\begin{split}Leb\,&\Bigg(B_{k}\cap\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}(a_{j},b_{j})\cup\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})\Bigg)\Bigg)\\
&-Leb\,\Bigg(A_{k}\cap\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}(a_{j},b_{j})\cup\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}(\hat{a}_{j},\hat{b}_{j})\Bigg)\Bigg)\\
&\geq Leb\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}\big(v_{k}^{j},\hat{v}_{k}^{j}\big)\Bigg)-Leb\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}\big(y_{k}^{j},\hat{y}_{k}^{j}\big)\Bigg).\end{split}

By the definitions of points
v_{k}^{j},\hat{v}_{k}^{j},y_{k}^{j}
and
\hat{y}_{k}^{j}
(see (
6.13
), (
6.27
), (
6.20
) and (
6.34
), respectively), we get

(6.42)

\begin{split}Leb&\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}\big(v_{k}^{j},\hat{v}_{k}^{j}\big)\Bigg)-Leb\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}\big(y_{k}^{j^{\prime}},\hat{y}_{k}^{j}\big)\Bigg)\\
&=Leb\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}\Big(\hat{y}_{k}^{j},\hat{v}_{k}^{j}+2\frac{\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}\Big)\Bigg)-Leb\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}\big(y_{k}^{j},v_{k}^{j}\big)\Bigg)\end{split}

In view of definition of
y_{k}^{j}
and
v_{k}^{j}
, as well as of (
6.18
), we get that

(6.43)

v_{k}^{j}-y_{k}^{j}=\frac{3}{7}\cdot\frac{e^{-D_{k}^{j}}}{q_{k}}=\frac{3}{7}\cdot\frac{e^{-D}}{q_{k}}\cdot e^{-D_{k}^{j}+D}\leq\frac{3}{7}\cdot\frac{e^{-D}}{q_{k}}\cdot e^{5Be^{-D+2B}}\leq\frac{3}{7}\cdot\frac{e^{-D}}{q_{k}}(1+6B^{-D+2B}).

Moreover, in view of definition of
\hat{y}_{k}^{j}
and
\hat{v}_{k}^{j}
, using again (
6.18
) and (
6.26
), we get

(6.44)

\begin{split}\hat{v}_{k}^{j}-\hat{y}_{k}^{j}&=e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{j}}+\frac{\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}-\big(e^{-\hat{D}_{k}^{j}}+\frac{\Delta_{k}}{2q_{k}}\big)(1+10Be^{-D})=\frac{\Delta_{k}}{2q_{k}}-\frac{10Be^{-D}}{q_{k}}(e^{-D+2B}+2e^{-D})\\
\geq&\frac{e^{-D}(1-\delta)}{2q_{k}}-\frac{11Be^{-D}}{q_{k}}e^{D+2B}\geq\frac{1}{2}\frac{e^{-D}}{q_{k}}(1-25Be^{-D+2B})\end{split}

By applying (
6.43
) and (
6.44
) to (
6.42
), we get

Leb\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}\big(v_{k}^{j},\hat{v}_{k}^{j}\big)\Bigg)-Leb\Bigg(\bigcup_{j=0}^{{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}}\big(y_{k}^{j},\hat{y}_{k}^{j}\big)\Bigg)\geq\frac{1-\rho}{14}e^{-D}(1-16Be^{-D+2B})\geq\frac{1-\rho}{16}e^{-D},

which by (
6.41
), (
6.42
), as well as (
6.40
) gives

(6.45)

Leb(B_{k})-Leb(A_{k})\geq\frac{1-4\rho}{16}e^{-D}\geq\frac{e^{-D}}{20}.

Since, in view of (
6.4
), we have
\gamma\leq 4\cdot 10^{-6}e^{-D}<\frac{1}{2}
, by (
6.45
), we get

Leb(B_{k})-Leb(A_{k})\geq\gamma.

which finishes the proof of the proposition.
∎

6.3.
Distinguishing tails in the general case

We now deal with the case when the rescalings under consideration are the
K
and
L
rescalings, with general
K,L
positive integers. We assume throughout that
L>K
.

6.3.1.
The
K
and
L
tail sets.

Let
(M_{k})_{k}
be a sequence of good
L
-fold matchings, given by
(c,\delta)
-BT rigidity sets. We let, as in the previous sections:

-

B
be the constant given by the linear control of trimmed derivatives (see Definition
5.2
);

-

a_{k}
be the centering constants given by
a_{k}:=S_{q_{k}}(f)(z_{k})
where
z_{k}
is the reference point of the matching sets,

-

X_{k}=[0,1]\backslash M_{k}
be the bad set for matchings.

-

\Delta_{k}=q_{k}\,\tilde{\lambda}^{k}_{1}
be the rescaled displacement,

-

t_{k}:=\frac{K}{L}S_{Lq_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-Ka_{k}
, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

where
v_{k}
marks the beginning of
B_{k}^{L}
on the set
\tilde{I}_{2}^{k}
.
To deduce from the criterion on disjointness that
K
-th and
L
-th acceleration of our flow are disjoint, we need to consider the sets

(6.46)

\displaystyle B_{k}^{L}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in I\setminus X_{k}\mid S_{Lq_{k}}(f)(x)-La_{k}\geq\frac{L}{K}\,t_{k}:=S_{Lq_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-La_{k}\},

\displaystyle B_{k}^{K}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in I\setminus X_{k}\mid S_{Kq_{k}}(f)(x)-Ka_{k}\geq t_{k}:=\frac{K}{L}S_{Lq_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-Ka_{k}\}

and prove that asymptotically the measures of
B_{k}^{L}
and
B_{k}^{K}
are different.

The main estimate which allows to distinguish the tail sets is the following:

Proposition 6.2

(Distinguishing tails in the general case)
.

Given any
L\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
\rho>0
such that, for every
f\in SymLog^{2}(T)
and for any
B>0
,
there exists

\epsilon>0
and
C=[c_{0},c_{1}]\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
, so that for any
sequence
(M_{k})_{k}
of
(\gamma,B)
-good
L
-fold
(z_{k},q_{k})-
matchings given a
(C,\epsilon,\rho)
-BT rigid presentation with

{\gamma<(L+1)\rho c_{1}+\epsilon}
we have that

Leb(B_{k}^{L})-Leb(B^{K}_{k})\geqslant\gamma\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N},

where
B_{k}^{K}
and
B_{k}^{L}
are respectively the
L
and
K
-tail sets defined above. Furthermore,
the sequence
(t_{k})_{k}
in the definition of the tail sets, namely
t_{k}=\frac{K}{L}S_{Lq_{k}}(f)(v_{k})-KS_{q_{k}}(f)(z_{k})
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, is bounded.

The proof of Proposition
6.2
is given in §
6.3.4
. We first prove to auxiliary results (in §
6.3.2
and §
6.3.3
respectively).

6.3.2.
A convexity inequality.

The key estimate which is used to show that the tails can be distinguished is based on convexity. We first state and prove the following Lemma, which will provide the key step to distinguish the tails.

Lemma 6.3

(A convexity estimate)
.

For any non-negative number
R\geq 0
and for every two positive integers
L\geqslant K>0
, it holds that

(6.47)

\sum_{i=1}^{K}\log\left(R+\frac{L-K}{2}+i\right)>\frac{K}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\log(R+i).

The rest of this subsection is taken by the proof of this Lemma.

Note that the inequality (
6.47
) does not depend on dynamics, it is just a functional inequality. If
K=1
then it is just standard Jensen’s inequality, so we assume that
K\geqslant 2
. To prove
that it is always satisfied for any
L\geqslant K>0
,
we need the following version of Jensen’s Inequality (see e.g. Lemma 1 in
[
KPW
]
).

Lemma 6.4

(Jensen inequality)
.

Let
a<b
be two real numbers and consider two convex combinations

\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}x_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}y_{j}\text{ with }1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}

for some
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in[a,b]
, while
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\notin[a,b]
.
Then for any strictly concave function
f
on
\mathbb{R}
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}f(x_{i})>\sum_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}f(y_{j}).

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

To prove that (
6.47
) is satisfied, consider two cases.

Case 1 (same parity)

First assume that
K
and
L
are of the same parity.
We have

\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{K}&\log\left(R+\frac{L-K}{2}+i\right)-\frac{K}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\log(R+i)\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{K}-\frac{1}{L}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{K}\log\left(R+\frac{L-K}{2}+i\right)-\frac{1}{L}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{L-K}{2}}\log(R+i)+\sum_{i=\frac{L+K}{2}+1}^{L}\log(R+i)\right)\\
&=\frac{L-K}{L}\left(\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\log\left(R+\frac{L-K}{2}+i\right)-\frac{1}{L-K}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{L-K}{2}}\log(R+i)+\sum_{i=\frac{L+K}{2}+1}^{L}\log(R+i)\right)\right).\end{split}

Then, by applying Lemma
6.4
to the following data:

\begin{split}&p_{1}=\ldots=p_{K}=\frac{1}{K};\quad x_{i}=R+\frac{L-K}{2}+i\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,K;\quad q_{1}=\ldots=q_{L-K}=\frac{1}{L-K};\\
&y_{j}=R+j\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,\frac{L-K}{2}\ \text{ and }\ y_{j}=R+K+j\ \text{ for }\ j=\frac{L-K}{2}+1,\ldots,L-K,\end{split}

we get(
6.47
) in this case.

Case 2 (opposite parity)
If
K
and
L
are not of the same parity, then we start by applying classical Jensen’s Inequality to each of the logs separately and obtain

\begin{split}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K}&\log\left(R+\frac{L-K}{2}+i\right)>\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\frac{1}{2K}\left(\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}+i\right)+\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}+i+1\right)\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{2K}\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}+i\right)+\frac{1}{2K}\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}+K\right).\end{split}

Then we get that

\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{K}&\log\left(R+\frac{L-K}{2}+i\right)-\frac{K}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\log(R+i)\\
&\geq\left(\frac{1}{2K}-\frac{1}{L}\right)\left(\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}\right)+\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}+K\right)\right)\\
&+\left(\frac{1}{K}-\frac{1}{L}\right)\left(\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}+1\right)+\ldots+\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}+K-1\right)\right)\\
&-\frac{1}{L}\left(\log(R+1)+\ldots+\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}-1\right)+\log\left(R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}+K+1\right)+\ldots+\log(R+L)\right)\end{split}

If
2K<L
, the by applying Lemma
6.4
to the following data:

\begin{split}&p_{1}=p_{K+1}=\frac{L-2K}{2K(L-K-1)}\text{ and }p_{2}=\ldots=p_{K}=\frac{L-K}{K(L-K-1)};\\
&x_{i}=R+\frac{L-K-1}{2}+i\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,K+1;\\
&q_{1}=\ldots=q_{L-K-1}=\frac{1}{L-K-1};\\
&y_{j}=R+j\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,\frac{L-K-1}{2}\ \text{ and }\ y_{j}=R+K+j+1\ \text{ for }\ j=\frac{L-K-1}{2}+1,\ldots,L-K-1,\end{split}

we get (
6.47
).
If on the other hand
2K\geq L
, then we apply Lemma
6.4
to

\begin{split}&p_{1}=\ldots=p_{K-1}=\frac{1}{K-1};\quad x_{i}=R+\frac{L-K+1}{2}+i\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,K-1;\\
&q_{1}=q_{L-K+1}=\frac{2K-L}{(L-K)(K-1)}\ \text{and}\ q_{2}=\ldots=q_{L-K}=\frac{K}{(L-K)(K-1)};\\
&y_{j}=R+j\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,\frac{L-K+1}{2}\ \text{ and }\ y_{j}=R+K+j\ \text{ for }\ j=\frac{L-K+1}{2},\ldots,L-K,\end{split}

and obtain (
6.47
) in the remaining case.
∎

6.3.3.
An auxiliary tail estimates.

To deduce the estimate of measures of both tail sets (which will be used in the proof of Proposition
6.2
in the next §
6.3.4
)
at the same time, we first prove the following estimate.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
H>0
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
let
w_{k}\in(a,b)
be such that
b-w_{k}=\frac{e^{-H}}{q_{k}}
and consider the following set:

E^{H,N}_{k}:=\{x\in M_{k}\mid S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(x)-Na_{k}\geq S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(w_{k})-Na_{k}\}.

Let
\hat{w}_{k}\in(\hat{a},\hat{b})
be such that
S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(\hat{w}_{k})=S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(w_{k})
and let
\hat{H}>0
be defined as
T^{(N-1)q_{k}}\hat{w}_{k}-\hat{a}=\frac{e^{-\hat{H}}}{q_{k}}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

If
H
is such that
3NB\leqslant D-3NB<H,\hat{H}\leqslant D
, then

(6.48)

|Leb(E^{H,N}_{k})-(e^{-H}+{e^{-\hat{H}}}+(N-1)\Delta_{k})|<(N+5)\rho e^{-D}.

The proof, that takes the rest of this subsection, largely follows the computations already done in the
K=1,L=2
case.

Proof.

First notice that, due to the choice of interval for
H
, as in Step 3 of the proof of Proposition
6.1
, all orbits under consideration lie in the monotonicity intervals of
S_{q_{n}}(f)
, by increasing
D
if necessary, depending on
N
. Hence, in the reminder of the proof, we always assume that we are in a proper monotonicity interval.

For every
j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}-1\}
, define
w_{k}^{j}
,
\hat{w}_{k}^{j}
,
H_{k}^{j}
and
\hat{H}_{k}^{j}
analogously as in (
6.13
), (
6.27
), (
6.14
) and (
6.28
).
By proceeding similarly as in the proof of (
6.18
) (and (
6.30
)) we get

(6.49)

|H_{k}^{j}-H|\leq Be^{-D+2B}\quad\text{and}\quad|\hat{H}_{k}^{j}-\hat{H}|\leq Be^{-D+2B}\quad\text{for every}\quad j\in\{0,\ldots,q_{k}-1\}.

We will show that
E^{H}_{k}
is essentially a union of intervals and the above expression shows that their left- and right-handside endpoints are very comparable.

Due to the fact that
M_{k}
is a good matching set, we know that for every
j
, the points
w_{k}^{j}
and
\hat{w}_{k}^{j}
are endpoints of an interval of continuity of
f
of length
\frac{e^{-H_{k}^{j}}}{q_{k}}+\frac{e^{-\hat{H}_{k}^{j}}}{q_{k}}+\delta_{k}
, where
\delta_{k}:=|\tilde{I}_{3}^{k}|+\ldots+|\tilde{I}_{d}^{k}|
. We will show that
E_{k}^{H}
consists essentially of intervals.

More precisely, let
x\in[w_{k}^{j},\hat{w}_{k}^{j}]\cap(I\setminus X_{k})
. Then either
x\in[w_{k}^{j},b_{j}]
or
x\in[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{w}_{k}^{j}]
. If
x\in[w_{k}^{j},b_{j}]\cap(I\setminus X_{k})
, then by monotonicity of
S_{q_{k}}(f)
, we have

S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(x)\geq S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(w_{k}^{j})

and thus
x\in E_{k}^{H,N}
. Similarly, if
x\in[\hat{a}_{j}+\frac{(N-1)\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}},\hat{w}_{k}^{j}]\cap M_{k}
, then again by monotonicity argument we have

S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(x)\geq S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(\hat{w}_{k}^{j})

and thus again
x\in E_{k}^{H,N}
. Let then
x\in[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{a}_{j}+\tfrac{(N-1)\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}]\cap M_{k}
, and assume that there exists
M\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
such that
T^{Mq_{k}}(x)\in[w_{k}^{j},b_{j}]
and
T^{(M-1)q_{k}}(x)\in[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{w}_{k}^{j}]
. Then

S_{Nq_{k}}(f)\left(T^{Mq_{k}}(x)\right)\geq S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(w_{k}^{j}).

However, due to monotonicity of
S_{q_{k}}(f)
, we have

S_{q_{k}}(f)\left(T^{Mq_{k}}(x)\right)\geq S_{q_{k}}(f)\left(T^{q_{k}}\circ T^{Mq_{k}}(x)\right)\geq\ldots\geq S_{q_{k}}(f)\left(T^{(N-M-1)q_{k}}\circ T^{Mq_{k}}(x)\right).

Thus

(6.50)

S_{(N-M)q_{k}}(f)\left(T^{Mq_{k}}(x)\right)\geq\frac{N-M}{N}S_{Nq_{k}}(w_{k}^{j}).

Analogously

(6.51)

S_{Mq_{k}}(f)(x)\geq\frac{M}{N}S_{Nq_{k}}(T^{-Nq_{k}}\hat{w}_{k}^{j}).

By summing up (
6.50
) and (
6.51
) we get that

S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(x)\geq S_{Nq_{k}}(w_{k}^{j})

and thus
x\in E^{H,N}_{k}
.

It remains to consider the case when
x\in[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{a}_{j}+\tfrac{(N-1)\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}]\cap M_{k}
but there is no
M\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
for which
T^{Mq_{k}}(x)\in[w_{k}^{j},b_{j}]
and
T^{(M-1)q_{k}}(x)\in[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{w}_{k}^{j}]
. This may happen for example if
H\approx\hat{H}\approx D
. Then, since as in (
6.32
), by (
6.49
) we have

(6.52)

\max\{|\Delta_{k}-e^{-D}|,|e^{-H}-e^{-H_{k}^{j}}|,|e^{-\hat{H}}-e^{-\hat{H}_{k}^{j}}|\}\leq 4Be^{-D+2B}\cdot e^{-D},

if
x
does not satisfy any of the considered conditions, then for some
M\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
it holds that
|T^{Mq_{k}}x-b_{j^{\prime}}|\leq 8Be^{-D+2B}\cdot e^{-D}
. Then, analogously as in the proof of (
6.33
), since
H>3NB
, we have

S_{Nq_{k}}(f)(x)-Na_{k}\geq(N+1)H-2NB>NH+NB,

which implies that
x\in E_{k}^{H,N}
. To sum up we have

(6.53)

\begin{split}E_{k}^{H,N}&\cap\left(\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}T^{-j}(a_{0},b_{0})\cup\bigcup_{j=0}^{(q_{k}-1-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}T^{-j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})\right)\cap M_{k}\\
&=\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})-1}[w_{k}^{j},\hat{w}_{k}^{j}]\cap M_{k}\end{split}

We have described the set
E_{k}^{H,N}
outside the set
\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}T^{j}(a_{0},b_{0})
and
\bigcup_{j=1}^{-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}T^{j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})
. Let us denote

V_{k}:=E_{k}^{H,N}\cap\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}T^{j}(a_{0},b_{0})\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}T^{j}(\hat{a}_{0},\hat{b}_{0})\right)\cap M_{k}.

Then, analogously as in (
6.36
) and (
6.37
) we have

(6.54)

V_{k}\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}(T^{j}(w_{k}^{0}),T^{j}b_{0})\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}(T^{j}\hat{a}_{0},T^{j}(\hat{w}_{k}^{0})),

while similarly as in (
6.38
) we have

(6.55)

\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k}}(T^{j}(T^{-q_{k}}w_{k}^{0}),T^{j}b_{0})\cup\bigcup_{j=-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})}^{-1}(T^{j}\hat{a}_{0},T^{j}(T^{q_{k}}\hat{w}_{k}^{0}))\right)\cap M_{k}\subseteq V_{k}.

We note that in (
6.55
), the left-hand side of the tail did not appear in the formula since the measure of this part was small enough so that it could be considered as an error. However, although (
6.55
) is more general, the proof goes along the same lines as the proof of (
6.38
).

To summarize, in view of (
6.53
), using (
5.21
), (
6.52
) and (
6.54
), we have

\begin{split}Leb(E_{k}^{H,N})&=Leb\left(\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})-1}[w_{k}^{j},\hat{w}_{k}^{j}]\cap M_{k}\right)+Leb(V_{k})\\
&\leq q_{k}\cdot\frac{1}{q_{k}}\cdot((1+4Be^{-D+2B})(e^{-H}+e^{-\hat{H}})+(N-1)\Delta_{k})\\
&\leq(e^{-H}+e^{-\hat{H}}+(N-1)\Delta_{k})+\rho e^{-D},\end{split}

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
H,\hat{H}\geq 3NB
.

On the other hand, using (
5.21
), (
6.52
), and (
6.55
) we have

\begin{split}Leb(E_{k}^{H,N})&=Leb\left(\bigcup_{j=0}^{q_{k}-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})-1}[w_{k}^{j},\hat{w}_{k}^{j}]\cap M_{k}\right)+Leb(V_{k})\\
&\geq q_{k}\cdot\frac{1}{q_{k}}\cdot((1-4Be^{-D+2B})(e^{-H}+e^{-\hat{H}})+(N-1)\Delta_{k})-(\overline{\ell}_{k}-\underline{\ell}_{k})2\frac{\Delta_{k}}{q_{k}}-Leb(X_{k})\\
&\geq(e^{-H}+e^{-\hat{H}}+(N-1)\Delta_{k})-(N+5)\rho e^{-D},\end{split}

Thus we get
